Abstract

\urladdr
https://sites.google.com/site/junyancao

\urladdr
https://ydeng.perso.math.cnrs.fr

https://www.math.utah.edu/ hacon/

\urladdr
https://www.mathe8.uni-bayreuth.de/de/team/prof-paun/index.php

In this article, we pursue two main objectives. The first is to demonstrate that the fundamental results of Green–Lazarsfeld
[
GL87
,
GL91
]
and Budur–Wang
[
BW15
,
BW20
]
can be established in a unified framework, using various versions of the

\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma. Our second—and primary—goal is to develop the technical tools necessary to achieve this. We begin by highlighting some of our key contributions, and conclude this introductory section with a discussion of additional applications, which will be explored in a companion piece to the present work.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold endowed with a holomorphic line bundle
(L,\bar{\partial}_{L})
, and let
\alpha
be a
(0,1)
-form whose complex conjugate is holomorphic. For each
t\in(\mathbb{C},0)
, consider the twisted
\bar{\partial}
-operator

\bar{\partial}_{t}:=\bar{\partial}_{L}+t\alpha

(0.0.1)

acting on the space of smooth
L
-valued
(p,q)
-forms on
X
.
By basic Hodge theory, this operator satisfies the integrability condition
\bar{\partial}_{t}^{2}=0
.
A natural problem is to study how the finite-dimensional spaces

\mathrm{Ker}\,(\bar{\partial}_{t})\big/\mathrm{Im}\,(\bar{\partial}_{t})

(0.0.2)

vary with
t
. General results (see
[
Kod86
]
) ensure that the dimension of the spaces in (
0.0.2
) defines an upper semicontinuous function of
t
.

As starting point for the type of results we obtain in this article, we re-interpret next a beautiful and important
statement due to Green-Lazarsfeld,
[
GL87
]
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\bar{\partial}
be a holomorphic
(n,0)
-form on
X
, that is to say, a section of the canonical bundle. We say that
u

extends to order one in the direction
\alpha
if the wedge product
u\wedge\alpha
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact, i.e. we can solve the equation

\bar{\partial}v=u\wedge\alpha.

(0.0.3)

Following
[
GL87
, §1]
, we have the following.

Theorem 0.1
.

Let
u
be a holomorphic section of
K_{X}
, which extends to order one in the direction
\alpha
. Then there exists a smooth family
\displaystyle(u_{t})_{t\in(\mathbb{C},0)}
of
(n,0)
-forms, such that
the following hold

u_{0}:=u,\qquad u_{t}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\bar{\partial}_{t},

(0.1.1)

so that
u
can be extended to a holomorphic section for the operator
\bar{\partial}_{t}
.

Phrased in more colloquial terms, this shows that extension to order one implies extension to arbitrary order.
Basically all the results we will discuss here have this flavor. We will present them in an increasing degree of complexity, but we first introduce some notations.

Let
L\to X\times\mathrm{Jac}(X)
be the holomorphic line bundle whose restriction to the fiber
X\times\gamma
is the flat line bundle corresponding to the
(0,1)
-form
\gamma
.

The higher rank analogue of this construction involves a hermitian flat vector bundle
E\to X
, together with a harmonic
(0,1)
-form
\xi
with values in
End(E)
such that
\xi\wedge\xi=0
. Then the twisted
\bar{\partial}
operator

\bar{\partial}_{E}+t\xi

(0.1.2)

is integrable, and induces a deformation
\mathcal{E}\to X\times\mathbb{C}
of
E
.

In sections 1-4 we give a proof of the next well-known statements from the perspective we promote here.

Theorem 0.2
.

[
Siu11
]
,
[
Wan12
]
,
[
Bud09
]
Let
u
be one of the following objects:

(1)

a holomorphic section of
K_{X}+L|_{X\times\gamma}
,

(2)

a holomorphic section of
K_{X}+M+L|_{X\times\gamma}
, where
M
is a line bundle on
X
whose first Chern class contains the snc divisor
\sum a_{i}D_{i}
, with
0<a_{i}<1
, or

(3)

a
\bar{\partial}
-closed,
E
-valued
(n,p)
-form.

We assume that
u
extends to order one. Then it extends to any arbitrary order.

Before stating our next result, notice that the "extension to order one" hypothesis is the same as assuming that
u
admits an extension to the first infinitesimal neighborhood of the fiber on which it is defined.

A somehow weaker theorem holds in the pluricanonical case.

Theorem 0.3

(
[
HPS18
, Section 10]
)
.

Let
u
be a section of
kK_{X}+L|_{X\times\gamma_{0}}
, and let

\gamma:(\mathbb{C},0)\to\mathrm{Jac}(X)
be a smooth path, such that
\gamma(0)=\gamma_{0}
. Assume further that the function

t\to h^{0}(X,kK_{X}+L|_{X\times\gamma(t)})

is constant. Then
u
deforms along the linear path
t\to\gamma(0)+t\gamma^{\prime}(0)
.

These results are established in Sections 1-4, ultimately by using appropriate versions of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
–lemma in Hodge theory. In Section 5 we consider the following problem.

Conjecture 0.4
.

Let
p:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{D}
be a smooth, proper family of Calabi-Yau manifolds and let
L\to\mathcal{X}
be a line bundle, whose restriction to the central fiber of
p
is globally generated. We assume that
h^{1,0}(X)=0
where
X
is the central fiber. Then the function

t\to h^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{t},L|_{\mathcal{X}_{t}})

is constant.

The main results in
[
CP20
]
are used to verify
0.4
in certain special cases.
For example, in Theorem
5.2
, we show that if there exists an integer
m\geqslant 2
and an element
f\in H^{0}(X,mL)
such that
\operatorname{Div}(f)
is smooth and
f
can be extended to
\mathcal{X}
, then Conjecture
0.4
holds.

We come now to the main part of our work, concerning the quasi-compact version of this type of theorems. Let
(X,D)
be a pair consisting of a compact Kähler manifold
X
and an snc divisor
D
. We denote by
M_{\rm B}(X_{0})
the space of rank one local systems on
X\setminus D
.
Then the following holds true.

Theorem 0.5

(
[
BW20
]
)
.

Consider the germ of a holomorphic disk
\gamma:(\mathbb{C},0)\to M_{\rm B}(X_{0})
, and fix
\gamma(0)=\tau
. Let
k
be a positive integer so that we have
\displaystyle\dim H^{p}(X\setminus D,\gamma(t))\geqslant k
for every
|t|\ll 1
. Then we have

\dim H^{p}(X\setminus D,\tau+t\dot{\alpha})\geqslant k

(0.5.1)

for
|t|\ll 1
, where
\dot{\alpha}
is the infinitesimal deformation
d\gamma(0)
.

In the statement above, we denote by
H^{\bullet}(X\setminus D,\tau)
the cohomology groups with coefficients in
\tau
.
Our proof of
0.5
is self-contained and differs substantially from the original argument in
[
BW20
]
, which relies on global considerations involving moduli spaces and the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence (with some ideas tracing back to
[
Sim93
]
).
We now briefly outline our main ideas of the proof of
0.5
.

Let
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
be the space of logarithmic flat bundles, i.e. pairs
(L,\nabla)
consisting of line bundles
L\to X
together with a holomorphic connection

\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}(\log D)

with log poles along
D
such that
\nabla^{2}=0
. We have a natural map

\rho:M_{\rm DR}(X/D)\to M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D),

(0.5.2)

which is surjective, by a well-known result due to Deligne, see
[
Del70
, II, 6.10]
. As shown in the same reference, the correspondence between the
two spaces in (
0.5.2
) goes much further: if we consider the complex

0\to\mathcal{O}(L)\to\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\to\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{2}(\log D)\to\cdots\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{n}(\log D)\to 0

(0.5.3)

induced by the connection
\nabla
, then we have
\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{p}(X,L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D))\simeq H^{p}(X_{0},\tau)
,
provided that the residue of
(L,\nabla)
on any component of
D
is not a positive integer. Here
\tau
is the image of
(L,\nabla)
via the map
\rho
.

The strategy we adopt for the proof of Theorem
0.5
consists in developing the necessary tools in order to construct a resolution of (
0.5.3
). Then we show that the cohomology of the resulting complex is computed by the kernel of a Laplace-type operator corresponding to
\nabla+\bar{\partial}
. After that, implementing the principle
extension to order one implies extension to infinite order
becomes relatively easy, and it is based on a version of
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma we establish here. We next provide a few details about all of this.

Let
h
be a metric on
L
, which is non-singular when restricted to

X\setminus D
, with at most analytic singularities along
D
. If
D^{\prime}_{h}
is the
(1,0)
-part of the corresponding Chern connection, then the difference

\theta_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla-D^{\prime}_{h})

is a
\partial
-closed
(1,0)
-form with log poles on
D
. Recall that the metric
h
is said to be
harmonic
if the operator

D^{\prime\prime}_{K}:=\bar{\partial}+\theta_{0}-\overline{\theta}_{0}

verifies the integrability condition
D^{\prime\prime}_{K}\circ D^{\prime\prime}_{K}=0
. Its conjugate reads as

D^{\prime}_{K}:=D^{\prime}_{h}+\theta_{0}+\overline{\theta}_{0}

so that
D_{K}:=D^{\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime\prime}_{K}
equals
\nabla+\bar{\partial}
.

Next, given
\tau\in M_{\rm B}(\mathcal{X})
we show that we can construct a logarithmic flat bundle
(L,\nabla)
such that
\rho(L,\nabla)=\tau
and such that the following hold:

•

L
admits a harmonic metric
h
;

•

the divisor
D
can be decomposed as follows
D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
, such that we have:

(a)

the residue
\mathrm{Res}(\theta_{0})
of the Higgs field along the components of
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
is zero, and it is non-zero along each component of
\Delta_{2}
;

(b)

the support of the curvature current
\sqrt{-1}\Theta(L,h)
is contained in
D
. All its Lelong numbers are rational, and those corresponding to components of
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
belong to
]-1,0[
. The Lelong numbers along the components of
\Delta_{1}
are equal to zero.

The resolution of the complex (
0.5.3
) mentioned above involves
D_{K}
-closed forms of fixed total degree with values in
L
and log poles on
D
. In order to analyze their properties, we endow
X_{0}:=X\setminus\Delta_{2}
with a Kähler metric
\omega_{D}
which has Poincaré singularities on
\Delta_{2}
and conic singularities
along
\Delta_{3}
, cf. Section
6
.
Let
\xi
be a log,
L
-twisted differential form.

\bullet
In case
\Delta_{1}=0
, we see that
its
L^{2}
-norm
\displaystyle\int_{X_{0}}|\xi|^{2}_{\omega_{D}}e^{-\varphi_{L}}dV<\infty
of
\xi
is finite (due to property (b) above), and therefore it is conceivable that a version of the familiar Hodge theory for the (Higgs version of the) Laplace operator

\Delta_{K}=[D_{K},D_{K}^{\star}]

(0.5.4)

still holds. In Section
7
we show that this is indeed the case: our main contribution here is the a-priori inequality §
7.2
, which incorporates the
Higgs field
\theta_{0}
. Based on it, we establish a Hodge decomposition for the space of
L^{2}
-forms, and we give a very precise description
of the space of harmonic forms
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
.

\bullet
In case
\Delta_{1}\neq 0
, the crucial
L^{2}
integrability of forms
\xi
as above fails to hold in general. There are at least two manners in which one can deal with this situation.

(i)

The form
\xi
writes as
\displaystyle\xi=\sum_{p+q=k}\xi_{p,q}
, where
\xi_{p,q}
is an
L
-valued form of type
(p,q)
with log-poles along
D
. Consider the vector bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
corresponding to the sheaf of logarithmic vector fields on
(X,D)
, endowed with a Hermitian metric
g_{D}
which has the same type of singularities as
\omega_{D}
(this is sometimes referred to as
metric with cylindrical singularities
, cf.
[
LZ16
]
). Then
\xi_{p,q}
can be seen as
(0,q)
-form with values in

\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L
and log-poles along
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
. It follows that we have

\displaystyle\int_{X_{0}}|\xi|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}e^{-\varphi_{L}}dV<\infty
. The differential
D_{K}
still makes sense in this context, and so does its formal adjoint
D_{K}^{\star}
(computed by using the metric data
g_{D},\omega_{D}
and
h
). We establish in Section
8
that the
Hodge decomposition holds, again based on an a-priori inequality for
\Delta_{K}
. Notice that the usual commutation relations in Kähler geometry are no longer true, which causes a few additional complications.

(ii)

Our form
\xi
can equally be interpreted as
L
-valued current on
X_{0}
, a
point of view which goes back to the work of Noguchi in
[
Nog95
]
.
This turns out to be extremely useful: we show that any current admits a Hodge decomposition (in the same spirit as Kodaira-deRham results in
[
dRK50
]
), and as consequence, that the analogue of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma holds. For this to be done it is indispensable that
D_{K}^{c}:=D^{\prime}_{K}-D^{\prime\prime}_{K}
and
D_{K}^{\star}
commute – which is not the case in (i), because the curvature of
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
comes into play.

With all this at hand, the proof of
0.5
reduces to solving certain differential equations, cf.
Section

10
. This is done in two steps: first we obtain solutions in the sense of currents, by using the results mentioned at (ii) above. Then we prove that among the solutions obtained we can find forms with log poles – here the techniques discussed in (i) are important.

It is understood that there are many details
we sweep under the rug
in this short presentation of our article, hoping that the reader will enjoy discovering them in the coming pages.

Anyway, we conclude the introduction by mentioning further applications. The results in (i) and (ii) can be seen as new analytic techniques for non-abelian Hodge theory in the quasi-compact setting. While introduced here by necessity, they are of independent interest and we expected them to have a broad range of applications, e.g. in the study of representation varieties and their Hodge structures.

In fact, in a subsequent work
[
CDHP25
]
we intend to address the following two fundamental problems, concerning the deformation theory for representations of
\pi_{1}(X_{0})
into
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

•

Analytic germs of representation varieties.

Let
R_{\rm B}(X_{0},{\rm GL}_{N})
denote the affine
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme of finite type representing the functor

A\longmapsto{\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(X_{0}),{\rm GL}_{N}(A)),

for commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
A
. A central problem is to describe precisely the analytic germ of
R_{\rm B}(X_{0},{\rm GL}_{N})(\mathbb{C})
at a semisimple representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

This circle of ideas originates in work of Deligne–Goldman–Millson
[
GM88
]
, who showed, in the compact case
D=\varnothing
, that the germ has quadratic singularities. Subsequently, Kapovich–Millson
[
KM98
, Theorem 1.13]
(see also Lefèvre
[
Lef19
]
) proved, under the additional assumption that the representation
\varrho
has
finite image
, that the analytic germ can be described as a quasi-homogeneous cone with generators of weights
1
and
2
and relations of weights
2
,
3
, and
4
, though without providing explicit equations. Their argument relies essentially on Sullivan’s theory of minimal models together with the foundational work of Morgan
[
Mor78
]
.

More recently, Pridham
[
Pri16
, Proposition 14.28]
, building on the
L^{2}
-methods of Timmerscheidt
[
Tim87
]
, obtained a refined description of the germ at
\varrho

without
assuming finite image, but under the assumption that
\varrho
has
unitary
local monodromy around
D
. The analytic techniques developed in the present paper allow us to remove this restrictive unitarity condition and to derive explicit defining equations for the singularity of the germ in the most general quasi-projective setting.

•

Extension of Eyssidieux–Simpson’s construction of mixed Hodge structures attached to the deformation theory of complex variations of Hodge structures on compact Kähler manifolds.

In the special case
D=\varnothing
, when
\varrho
underlies a complex variation of Hodge structure, Eyssidieux–Simpson
[
ES11
]
, based on the work of Goldman–Millson, constructed a universal variation of mixed Hodge structure for the
tautological representation

\hat{\varrho}:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{T}),

where
T
is a certain formal germ contained in the formal germ
{\rm Spf}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho})
of
\varrho
. This result plays a key role in the proof of the
linear Shafarevich conjecture
by Eyssidieux et al.
[
EKPR12
]
.

Building on the analytic methods introduced in the present work, we will extend Eyssidieux–Simpson’s theorem to the quasi-projective setting in our future work
[
CDHP25
]
.

Notations and conventions

•

Unless otherwise specified, throughout this paper,
X
denotes a compact Kähler manifold,
D=\sum D_{i}
is a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
.

•

We denote by
\Omega_{X}(\log D)
the sheaf of holomorphic forms with logarithmic poles along
D
, and with
\Omega_{X}\langle D\rangle

the corresponding vector bundle. We will use the same type of notations for the duals, exterior/symmetric powers, etc.

•

M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
denotes the moduli space of pairs
(L,\nabla)
, where
L
is a holomorphic line bundle on
X
, and
\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}(\log D)
is a logarithmic integrable connection.

•

A Hermitian metric on
L|_{X\setminus D}
is said to be
harmonic
if it satisfies the conditions described in
6.5
. Let
D_{K}^{\prime\prime}
,
D_{K}^{\prime}
, and
D_{K}
be the associated connections on
L
as defined in
6.4
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{K}
be the connection incorporating the current defined in (
10.4.3
).

•

For any given
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
with
\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,{D_{i}}(L,\nabla))\in]-1,0]
for every component
D_{i}
, a
harmonic metric
often refers to the specific construction provided in
Proposition

6.12
. In this context, we decompose the divisor
D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
as described therein.

•

With this setup, let
\omega_{D}
be a Kähler metric on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
with suitable asymptotic behavior as defined in (
7.1.1
).
If we decompose
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
into prime components, then for every multi-index
a=(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
we define the volume form
d\mu_{a}
on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
as in (
8.9.1
).

•

In the same setting, we denote by

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{i,K}(X,L)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{i,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
for the sheaves on
X
introduced in
8.9
.
When the degree
i
is not relevant, we abbreviate them as
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.
We denote their spaces of global sections by
C^{\infty}_{i,K}(X,L)
and
C^{\infty}_{i,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
, respectively.
In other words, the calligraphic symbol
\mathcal{C}
always refers to a sheaf, whereas the plain
C
refers to its global sections.

1
Families of flat line bundles

1.1
Preliminaries

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold. By elementary Hodge theory, the image of the map

\iota:H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O})

(1.0.1)

is a lattice. The quotient

\mathrm{Jac}(X):=H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O})/\mathrm{Im}(\iota)

(1.0.2)

is a complex torus called the Jacobian variety of
X
. Via the exponential sequence, it is isomorphic to the subgroup of

H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}^{\star})
corresponding to bundles with trivial Chern class.

We consider the holomorphic bundle

L\to X\times\mathrm{Jac}(X)

(1.0.3)

such that the restriction
\displaystyle L|_{X\times\{\gamma\}}
is the flat bundle induced by the
(0,1)
-form
\gamma\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)
. Following
[
Siu11
]
we describe next the transition functions of
L
. As a consequence, we will see that this bundle admits a natural metric whose associated curvature form plays a crucial role in what follows.

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}
be a basis of holomorphic
(0,1)
-forms on
X
and let
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of open subsets of
X
such that any finite intersection of them if Stein and simply connected.
It follows that
the restriction of each
\alpha_{j}
to any finite intersection

\displaystyle U_{i_{1}}\cap\dots\cap U_{i_{N}}
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact. We then write

\alpha_{j}|_{U_{p}}=\overline{\partial f_{jp}}

(1.0.4)

for some holomorphic function
f_{jp}
defined on
U_{p}
. For each fixed
j
the difference function
f_{jp}-f_{js}
defined on
U_{p}\cap U_{s}
is a constant which we denote by
c_{jps}
.
The family of constants
\displaystyle\left(\exp({\overline{c}_{jps}})\right)_{p,s}
are the transition function for the bundle
\displaystyle L|_{X\times\{\alpha_{j}\}}
.

Now an arbitrary element of
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
is represented by a form
\sum t^{j}\alpha_{j}
, and it follows that all in all the transition functions for
L
are given by

\Big(\exp\sum_{j=1}^{q_{X}}t^{j}\overline{c}_{jps}\Big)_{p,s}

(1.0.5)

on the intersection
U_{s}\cap U_{p}
.

Moreover, it follows that the set of functions

\Big(\exp 2\Re\big(\sum_{j=1}^{q_{X}}t^{j}\overline{f}_{jp}\big)\Big)_{p}

(1.0.6)

define a Hermitian metric
h_{L}
on the holomorphic bundle
L
. The
(1,0)
–part of the Chern connection writes as

D^{\prime}\sigma|_{U_{j}}:=\partial\sigma_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{q_{X}}\overline{f}_{jp}dt^{j}\wedge\sigma_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{q_{X}}\overline{t}^{j}df_{jp}\wedge\sigma_{j}

(1.0.7)

and thus the corresponding
curvature form is written as

\Theta(L,h_{L})=-\sum dt^{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}-\sum\overline{\alpha}_{j}\wedge d\overline{t}^{j}.

(1.0.8)

In particular, we see that
\sqrt{-1}\Theta(L,h_{L})
doesn’t have any positivity properties.

1.2
An extension result

For each positive number
m
we consider the set

Z_{m}:=\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)/h^{0}(X,K_{X}+L_{\alpha})\geqslant m\}

(1.0.9)

where for simplicity we denote by
L_{\alpha}
the restriction
\displaystyle L|_{X\times\{\alpha\}}
.

We consider a germ of holomorphic disk
\gamma:(\mathbb{C},0)\to\mathrm{Jac}(X)
such that
\gamma(0)\in Z_{m}
.
Let
\alpha_{0},\dot{\alpha}_{0}
the forms
\gamma(0)
and
d\gamma(0)
, respectively, where we identify
\dot{\alpha}_{0}
with its projection to

\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.
Then we prove the following statement.

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that
u\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}})
extends to order one in the direction
\dot{\alpha}_{0}
. We consider the segment
l(z):=\alpha_{0}+z\dot{\alpha}_{0}
in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
. Then there exists a positive
real
\varepsilon_{0}
and
U(z)\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}+z\dot{\alpha}_{0}})
such that
U(0)=u
, where
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|z|\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
u
be a holomorphic section of the bundle
\displaystyle K_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}}
. We show next that we can extend
u
in direction
\dot{\alpha}_{0}
by proving that
u
admits an extension to an infinitesimal neighborhood of the fiber
X:=X\times\{l(0)\}
inside the family

\mathcal{X}:=\big(X\times\{l(z)\}\big)_{|z|\leqslant 1}.

(1.1.1)

If we can do that, the conclusion follows from
[
Lev83
]
.

In all cases treated in
[
CP20
]
the extension of a holomorphic section defined on the central fiber
X
to the first infinitesimal neighborhood of
X
in
\mathcal{X}
is far easier then the general case –since the problem we have to solve concerns the central fiber
X
with its reduced structure. We will see that in the current context the obstruction to lift
u
to an infinitesimal neighborhood of arbitrary order is virtually the same as the one corresponding to the first order.

1.2.1
Extension to the infinitesimal neighborhood of
1^{\rm st}
order

We still use in what follows the notation
(L,h_{L})
for the holomorphic line bundle we have introduced above together with its Hermitian structure restricted to
\mathcal{X}
. The curvature formula (
1.0.8
) implies that the equality

\Theta(L,h_{L})=-dz\wedge\dot{\alpha}_{0}-\overline{\dot{\alpha}_{0}}\wedge d\overline{z}

(1.1.2)

holds.

Let
\displaystyle\Xi:=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
be the holomorphic vector field giving the derivative in the base direction on
\mathcal{X}
. We introduce the following operator

\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}:=D^{\prime}\circ\iota_{\Xi}

(1.1.3)

acting on
L
-valued
(n+1,q)
forms on the total space
\mathcal{X}
, that is to say we first contract our form with the vector field
\Xi
and then we apply the covariant derivative.

The commutation formula (cf.
[
CP20
]
for the general case) shows that we have

\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}\circ\bar{\partial}=\bar{\partial}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}-\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge.

(1.1.4)

Note that in our case the equality (
1.1.4
) is greatly simplified by the fact that
\Xi
is holomorphic, together with the shape of the curvature form in (
1.1.2
).

Let
U_{0}
be a smooth extension of
u
to the total space
\mathcal{X}
; it is a
(n+1,0)
form
such that

\bar{\partial}U_{0}=z\Lambda_{0}

(1.1.5)

for some
L
-valued
(n+1,1)
form
\Lambda_{0}
on
\mathcal{X}
. We apply the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}
to
(
1.1.5
) and restrict the result to the central fiber
X
. The RHS of (
1.1.5
) becomes simply
\displaystyle\lambda_{0}:=\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{dz}\Big|_{X}
; the formula (
1.1.4
) implies that we have

\bar{\partial}v-\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u=\lambda_{0}

(1.1.6)

where
v
is the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{0})
to
X
.

We see that the obstruction form

\lambda_{0}
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact if and only if this is the case
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u
. Recall that
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact by hypothesis, and therefore
u
admits an extension across the first infinitesimal neighborhood of
X
in

\mathcal{X}
.

1.2.2
Extension to the infinitesimal neighborhood of
2^{\rm nd}
order

We argue in a similar way, by applying the Lie derivative twice to the equality

\bar{\partial}U_{1}=z^{2}\Lambda_{1}

(1.1.7)

obtained at the previous step. After applying
\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}
a first time the LHS is equal to

\bar{\partial}\big(\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{1})\big)-\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge U_{1}

(1.1.8)

and a new derivation of (
1.1.8
) shows that the new obstruction form

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}:=\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{dz}\Big|_{X}
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact precisely when

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{1})}{dz}\Big|_{X}

(1.1.9)

is
\bar{\partial}
-exact on
X
. In order to show this we argue as follows.

As consequence of Hodge decomposition on
X
we have

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{1})}{dz}\Big|_{X}=v+D^{\prime}w

(1.1.10)

where
v
is holomorphic
\displaystyle L_{\alpha_{0}}
-valued
(n,0)
form and
w
is
a
(n-1,0)
form with values in the same bundle.

We already know that

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge v\in\mathrm{Im}(\bar{\partial})

(1.1.11)

by hypothesis.

For the other term, we note that the form

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}w

(1.1.12)

is
\bar{\partial}
-closed, as a consequence of the equality (
1.1.10
). On the other hand, we have the equality

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}w=-D^{\prime}\big(\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge w\big)

(1.1.13)

so the form (
1.1.12
) is
\bar{\partial}
-closed and
D^{\prime}
-exact. Hodge theory shows that the form

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}w
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact, so we are done with the second order.

Remark 1.2
.

The arguments before show that if
\beta
is any
\displaystyle L_{\alpha_{0}}
-valued
(n,0)
form on the central fiber such that
\bar{\partial}(\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\beta)=0
, then

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\beta
is automatically
\bar{\partial}
-exact.

1.2.3
Higher order extensions

We argue by induction in order to extend
u
to an arbitrary order.
Assume that we have

\bar{\partial}U_{k}=z^{k+1}\Lambda_{k}

(1.2.1)

on
\mathcal{X}
. We apply the Lie derivative to (
1.2.1
) and infer that we have

\bar{\partial}\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{k})-\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge U_{k}=(k+1)z^{k}\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{O}(z^{k+1}).

(1.2.2)

Another derivative gives

\bar{\partial}\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}^{2}(U_{k})-2\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{k})=(k+1)kz^{k-1}\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{O}(z^{k}),

(1.2.3)

so it is clear that after
k+1
such operations we obtain

\bar{\partial}\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}^{k+1}(U_{k})-(k+1)\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}^{k}(U_{k})=(k+1)!\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{O}(z).

(1.2.4)

By Remark
1.2
the
\bar{\partial}
-closed form

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}^{k}(U_{k})}{dz}\Big|_{X}

(1.2.5)

is
\bar{\partial}
-exact, and this is the end of the proof.
∎

2
Higher rank version

The local structure of moduli of hermitian flat bundles has been obtained by Nadel in
[
Nad88
]
. The main statement for vector bundles is due to Wang
[
Wan12
]
: cohomology jump loci of hermitian flat vector bundles are defined by linear equations. In this section we shall give a direct proof for that. Actually it is sufficient to show that the following hold.

Theorem 2.1
.

Assume that
E
is a hermitian flat vector bundle on a compact Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
. Assume that
\xi\in H^{0,1}(X,End(E))
is a harmonic
(0,1)
-form with value in
End(E)
so that
\xi\wedge\xi=0
. Then
\bar{\partial}_{E}+z\xi
defines the deformation of flat bundles of
E
, which we denote by
\mathcal{E}\to X\times\mathbb{C}
. Assume that the section
[u]\in H^{p}(X,K_{X}\otimes E)
extends to the first order of
\mathcal{E}
at
X\times\{0\}
. Then it extends to arbitrary order.

Proof.

We argue via a formal Taylor expansion: our aim is to solve this equation

(\bar{\partial}_{E}+z\xi)U=0

for

U=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}z^{i}u_{i}

with
u_{0}=u
and
u_{i}=A^{n,p}(X,E)
is the smooth
(n,p)
-form with value in
L
. To this end, we show that the following equations

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}_{E}(u_{1})+\xi\wedge u_{0}=0\\
\bar{\partial}_{E}(u_{2})+\xi\wedge u_{1}=0\\
\ldots\\
\bar{\partial}_{E}(u_{i+1})+\xi\wedge u_{i}=0\\
\ldots\end{cases}

(2.1.1)

admit a solution.
The condition that
u
extends to the first order means that existence of
u_{1}\in A^{n,p}(E)
so that the first
\bar{\partial}_{E}
-equation is solved. One can take
u_{1}=D_{E}^{\prime}v_{1}
with
v_{1}\in A^{n-1,p}(X,E)
by the Hodge decomposition

A^{n,p}(E)=\mathcal{H}^{n,p}(E)\oplus im(D_{E}^{\prime}).

Since
\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X,End(E))
, one has

D_{End(E)}^{\prime}(\xi)=\bar{\partial}_{End(E)}(\xi)=0.

For
\xi\wedge u_{1}\in A^{n,p+1}(X,E)
, one has

D^{\prime}_{E}(\xi\wedge u_{1})=D_{End(E)}^{\prime}(\xi)\wedge u_{1}-\xi\wedge D_{E}^{\prime}u_{1}=0

and

\bar{\partial}_{E}(\xi\wedge u_{1})=\bar{\partial}_{End(E)}(\xi)\wedge u_{1}-\xi\wedge\xi\wedge u_{0}=0

by
\xi\wedge\xi=0
. Moreover

D_{E}^{\prime}(\xi\wedge v_{1})=D_{End(E)}^{\prime}(\xi)\wedge v_{1}-\xi\wedge D_{E}^{\prime}v_{1}=-\xi\wedge u_{1}.

By the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma one concludes that
\xi\wedge u_{1}=-\bar{\partial}_{E}D^{\prime}_{E}v_{2}
for some
v_{2}\in A^{n-1,p}(E)
. Write
u_{2}:=D^{\prime}_{E}v_{2}
, which is our desired second order extension. The argument for higher order extensions is exactly the same.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that in order to establish the result in
[
Wan12
]
, it is sufficient to prove the next statement (in which the hypothesis is stronger than the one in Theorem
2.1
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\to X\times\mathbb{D}
be a family of hermitian flat vector bundles with
E_{z}:=\mathcal{E}|_{X\times\{z\}}
. Write
E:=E_{0}
. If for some
[u]\in H^{p}(X,K_{X}\otimes E)
, there are smooth sections
U(x,z)\in A^{n,p}(X,K_{X}\otimes E)
parametrized by
z\in\mathbb{D}
so that
U_{z}(x):=U(x,z)
in
H^{p}(X,K_{X}\otimes E_{z})
with
U_{0}(x)=u(x)
. Then
u
extends to first order.

Proof.

We do a Taylor expansion
U(x,z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}z^{i}u_{i}
with
u_{i}\in A^{n,p}(X,K_{X}\otimes E)
. We write

\bar{\partial}_{E_{z}}=\bar{\partial}_{E}+t\xi+O(t^{2})

By
\bar{\partial}_{E_{z}}U_{z}(x)=0
one has

\bar{\partial}_{E}u_{1}+\xi\wedge u_{0}=0.

This implies the first order extension.
∎

3
Adjoint version

In this section our target is the following result.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold, and let
M
be a line bundle on
X
so that
c_{1}(M)=[\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}a_{i}D_{i}]
with
0<a_{i}<1
and
D:=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}a_{i}D_{i}
is SNC. We consider a germ of holomorphic disk
\gamma:(\mathbb{C},0)\to\mathrm{Jac}(X)
such that
\gamma(0)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
and such that the any element of
H^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{0})
extends to order one in the direction
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\in H^{0,1}(X)
(i.e. the infinitesimal deformation
d\gamma(0)
).
Then the function

h^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{z\dot{\alpha}_{0}})

(3.1.1)

is constant for
0\leqslant|z|\ll 1
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that any section
u\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+M)
can be extended
smoothly
to sections in
H^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{z\dot{\alpha}_{0}})
for
|z|
small enough.
For
L_{\gamma(\tau)}
, there is
\xi_{i}\in A^{0,1}(X)
so that

\bar{\partial}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tau^{i}\xi_{i}

defines the complex structure for
L_{\gamma(\tau)}
. Since

(\bar{\partial}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tau^{i}\xi_{i})^{2}=0
, this means that

\bar{\partial}\xi_{1}=0

which is the infinitesimal deformation of
L_{\gamma(\tau)}
at
0
, namely
[\xi_{1}]=\dot{\alpha}_{0}
. By the assumption that
h^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{\gamma(\tau)})
is constant, there is
\tilde{U}_{\tau}\in H^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{\gamma(\tau)})
which depends on
\tau
smoothly so that
\tilde{U}_{0}(x)=u
. Hence one can write

\tilde{U}_{\tau}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\tau^{i}\tilde{u}_{i}

with
\tilde{u}_{0}=u
and
\tilde{u}_{i}=A^{n,0}(X,M)
is the smooth
(n,0)
-form with value in
M
, such that

(\bar{\partial}_{M}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tau^{i}\xi_{i})(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\tau^{i}\tilde{u}_{i})=0.

This implies that

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{M}\tilde{u}_{1}+\xi_{1}\wedge u=0

(3.1.2)

We can choose
[\xi_{1}]\in H^{0,1}(X)
the harmonic representative with respect to some Kähler metric
\omega_{X}
on
X
. In particular,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\xi_{1}=\partial\xi_{1}=0

(3.1.3)

We next use a few results in
L^{2}
-cohomology. Consider the manifold
X^{\circ}:=X-D
and equip it with the Poincaré type metric
\omega
. The restriction
M|_{X^{\circ}}
is endowed with the smooth metric
h_{M}
induced by
\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}a_{i}D_{i}
so that its curvature is equal to zero. The
equation to be solved is as follows

(\bar{\partial}_{M}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}z^{i}\xi_{1})(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}z^{i}u_{i})=0.

with
u_{0}=u|_{X^{\circ}}
and
u_{i}\in L^{n,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
. By identifying the coefficients of
z^{i}
, this is equivalent with the following system,

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}_{M}(u_{1})+\xi_{1}\wedge u_{0}=0\\
\bar{\partial}_{M}(u_{2})+\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}=0\\
\ldots\\
\bar{\partial}_{M}(u_{i+1})+\xi_{1}\wedge u_{i}=0\\
\ldots\end{cases}

(3.1.4)

modulo convergence issues.

By the Hodge decomposition for
L^{2}
-spaces, one has

L^{n,p}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)=\mathcal{H}^{n,p}(X^{\circ},M)\oplus imD_{M}^{\prime}

for
p\geqslant 0
,
where
D_{M}^{\prime}
is the
(1,0)
-connection of
(M,h_{M})
over
X^{\circ}
. Note that
\xi|_{X^{\circ}}
is
L^{2}
-integrable with respect to the complete metric
\omega
on
X^{\circ}
, and

\tilde{u}_{1}\in A^{n,0}(X,M)\subset L^{n,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M).

Hence
\xi_{1}\wedge u\in L^{n,1}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
. One can take

u_{1}=D_{M}^{\prime}v_{1}

for some
v_{1}\in L^{n-1,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
with
u_{1}-\tilde{u}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{n,0}(X^{\circ},M)
. Therefore,
by (
3.1.2
)

\bar{\partial}_{M}{u}_{1}+\xi_{1}\wedge u=0

By the
L^{2}
-norm estimate in
[
CP23
, Theorem 3.3]
, one has

\lVert{u}_{1}\rVert\leqslant C\lVert\xi_{1}\wedge u\rVert\leqslant C\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert\cdot\lVert u\rVert

where
C
is some universal constant.
We can thus make a rescaling of
\xi_{1}
a priori so that
C\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

For
\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}\in L^{n,1}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
, by (
3.1.3
) one has

\bar{\partial}_{M}(\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1})=-\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}=0

-D_{M}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}\wedge v_{1})=-\partial\xi_{1}\wedge v_{1}+\xi_{1}\wedge D_{M}^{\prime}v_{1}=\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}.

Hence

D_{M}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1})=0.

By the
L^{2}
version of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma, there is

v_{2}\in L^{n-1,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)

such that
D_{M}^{\prime}v_{2}\in L^{n,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
and

\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}=-\bar{\partial}_{M}D_{M}^{\prime}v_{2}

By
[
CP23
, Theorem 3.3]
again, one has

\lVert D_{M}^{\prime}v_{2}\rVert\leqslant C\lVert\xi_{1}\wedge u_{1}\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\lVert u_{1}\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\lVert u\rVert

Write
u_{2}:=D_{M}^{\prime}v_{2}
, which is our desired second order extension.
We do this inductively to find
u_{i}\in L^{n,0}(X^{\circ},M)
so that

\lVert u_{i}\rVert\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{i}}\lVert u\rVert

and the equations (
3.1.4
) solves. Then the sum

U_{z}:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}z^{i}u_{i}

belongs to
L^{n,0}_{(2)}(X^{\circ},M)
for any
|z|\leqslant 1
so that

(\bar{\partial}_{M}+z\xi_{1})(U_{z})=0

Note that
\bar{\partial}_{M}+z\xi_{1}
defines the complex structure for the line bundle
M+L_{z\dot{\alpha}_{0}}
. Hence

U_{z}\in H^{0}(X^{\circ},K_{X}+M+L_{z\dot{\alpha}_{0}}|_{X^{\circ}})

which is
L^{2}
-integrable with respect to the Poincare metric and
h_{M}
. It thus extends to a holomorphic section in
H^{0}(X,K_{X}+M+L_{z\dot{\alpha}_{0}})
.
∎

4
Pluricanonical version

Let
L\to X\times\mathrm{Jac}(X)
be the universal holomorphic line bundle from (
1.0.3
).
In this section, we consider the pluricanonical case.
Our main object of study is the set

Z_{k,m}:=\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)\mid h^{0}(X,mK_{X}+L_{\alpha})\geqslant k\},

(4.0.1)

where
k\geqslant 1
is an integer.
We show that
Z_{k,m}
has the same flatness properties as the analogous sets in the preceding sections.
That is, we have the following:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold. Then, for every
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
, the jumping locus

Z_{k,m}
defined in (
4.0.1
)
is a finite union of translates of subtori in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.

We provide in what follows two proofs of
4.1
. The first approach relies on a decomposition theorem for direct images proved in
[
HPS18
]
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety,
s\in H^{0}(mK_{X})
and
v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
If
s
deforms to 1-st order in the direction of
v
, then it deforms to arbitrary order. In particular, the jumping locus
Z_{k,m}
is a union of subtori in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\kappa(X)=0
. Let
a:X\to A
be the Albanese morphism, and identify
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})
. In this case
V^{0}(mK_{X}):=Z_{m}\neq\emptyset
consists of isolated points for some
m>0
and therefore
a_{*}(\omega_{X}^{m})
is a direct sum of torsion translates of unipotent vector bundles
a_{*}(\omega_{X}^{m})=\oplus U_{i}\otimes P_{i}
(see
[
HPS18
, Proposition 7.5]
). Since
\kappa(X)=0
and the
P_{i}
are torsion, then
a_{*}(\omega_{X}^{m})=U\otimes P
is indecomposable (see
[
HPS18
, Corollary 4.3]
).
By
[
CP17
]
(see also
[
HPS18
, Corollary 27.2]
), any surjective homomorphism
U\to P
where
P\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(A)
is split and hence
U\cong P
. The claim is now immediate because if
s\in H^{0}(mK_{X}\otimes P^{\vee})
, then
a_{*}s=1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{A})=H^{0}(a_{*}(mK_{X}\otimes P^{\vee}))
. But then
a_{*}(s\wedge v)=v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})
does not vanish.

In the general case, consider the Iitaka fibration
f:X\to Y
with general fiber
F
of Kodaira dimension 0. We denote by
\phi:A_{X}\to A_{Y}
the corresponding surjective map between the Albanese varieties of
X
and
Y
. By
[
HPS18
, Lemma 11.1]
, and the kernel of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(F)
is a finite union of torsion translates of

{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
. Suppose that
0\neq s\in H^{0}(mK_{X}\otimes P^{\vee})
and
s\wedge v=0
for some
v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{A})
, then
s|_{F}\wedge v|_{F}=0
. Since
s|_{F}\in H^{0}(mK_{F}\otimes P|_{F}^{\vee})
, it follows that
v|_{F}=0
and hence that
v\in H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})
.
However, by
[
HPS18
, Theorem 11.2]
, we know that
h^{0}(mK_{X}\otimes P^{\vee}\otimes Q)
is constant for
Q\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
and hence
s
deforms to arbitrary order in the direction of
v
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

We remark that Theorem
4.2
actually strengthens Theorem
4.1
in 2 important ways. First of all, it’s proof shows that all the components of
Z_{m}
are torsion translates of subtori of
{\rm Jac}(X)
and using similar arguments one can show that the same holds for
Z_{k,m}
. Secondly, the proof implies that if
S
is the symmetric algebra generated by
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\vee}
then locally around any point
P\in{\rm Jac}(X)
the deformations of
H^{0}(mK_{X}+P)
are formally determined by the derivative complex

0\to S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H^{0}(mK_{X}+P)\to S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H^{1}(mK_{X}+P)\to\ldots\to S\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}(mK_{X})\to 0.

Note that this is not a consequence of the second proof we give below (see Lemma
4.5
), because in that proof we make use of the metric
h_{m,L}
and it is a priori not clear that if
s\in H^{0}(mK_{X})
deforms to first order then so does the corresponding section in
s^{\prime}\in H^{0}(mK_{X}\otimes\mathcal{I}(h_{m,L}))
. For example, if
Z_{m}=\{\mathcal{O}_{X}\}
and
s=\sigma^{m}
where
\sigma\in H^{0}(K_{X})
, then
H^{0}(mK_{X}\otimes\mathcal{I}(h_{m,L}))=H^{0}(K_{X})
so that
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
. In this case it is conceivable that
[\sigma^{m}\wedge v]=0
whilst
[\sigma\wedge v]\neq 0
. Theorem
4.2
excludes these cases.

We now provide another proof of
4.1
, using the idea of the extension argument developed in the previous sections.
To begin with, we write

mK_{X}+L=K_{X}+(m-1)K_{X}+L,

and our first task will be to construct a “natural” metric on the bundle

L_{m}:=(m-1)K_{X}+L.

(4.3.1)

We then proceed as in the case
m=1
, that is, by showing that the system of equations of type (
2.1.1
) admits a solution.

Let
\mathfrak{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
be the ideal generated by the set of global sections of the
bundles
mK_{X}+L_{\tau}
, for all
\tau\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)
. In other words, the fiber of
\mathfrak{I}
at an arbitrary point
x\in X
is given by

\mathfrak{I}_{x}:=\{f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\exists\tau\in\mathrm{Jac}(X),\exists s\in H^{0}(X,mK_{X}+L_{\tau}),s=f\hbox{ near }x\}.

(4.3.2)

By the Noetherian property of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
there exists a finite number of sections

s_{i}\in H^{0}(X,mK_{X}+L_{\alpha_{i}})

(4.3.3)

generating
\mathfrak{I}
locally near every point of
X
, where
i=1,\dots,N
.

Now let
h_{i}=e^{-\varphi_{i}}
be the restriction of the metric
h_{L}
of the bundle
L
to the fiber
X\times\{\alpha_{i}\}
. The local weights

\psi_{i}:=\frac{m-1}{m}\left(\log|f_{i}|^{2}-\varphi_{i}\right)

(4.3.4)

define a metric for the bundle
(m-1)K_{X}
, for every
i=1,\dots,N
. We introduce the metric

h_{m,L}
on the bundle
(m-1)K_{X}+L
via the local weights

\varphi_{m,L}:=\log\big(\sum_{i}e^{\psi_{i}}\big)+\varphi_{L}

(4.3.5)

and we summarise its properties in the following statement.

Lemma 4.4
.

The following hold.

(1)

The metric
h_{m,L}
has logarithmic singularities and moreover the singular part of the weights in (
4.3.5
) is independent of the variable
t\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)
(cf. notations in section 1).

(2)

Let
\omega
be a fixed metric on
X
. For any global holomorphic section
\sigma
of
mK_{X}+L_{\tau}
there exists a positive constant
C>0
so that we have

\sup_{X}\frac{|\sigma|^{2}_{h_{m,L}}}{dV_{\omega}}\leqslant C.

(3)

The curvature of the bundle
\displaystyle\big(mK_{X}+L,h_{m,L}\big)

is semi-positive when restricted to the fiber
{X\times\{\tau\}}
for any
\tau\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.

All these properties (1)-(3) are simple consequence of the definitions and we will not provide any explanation.

We need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

In the setting of
4.1
, consider a germ of a holomorphic disk

\gamma:(\mathbb{D},0)\to Z_{k,m}
such that

t\longmapsto h^{0}\big(X,mK_{X}+L_{\gamma(t)}\big)

(4.5.1)

is constant for all
t\in\mathbb{C}
with
|t|
sufficiently small.
We denote by
\alpha_{0}
and
\dot{\alpha}_{0}
the elements
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, respectively.
Let
u\in H^{0}(X,mK_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}})
. Then the equation

\bar{\partial}w=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u

(4.5.2)

admits a solution
w
that is
L^{2}
with respect to the restriction of the
metric
\displaystyle h_{m,L}
to the fiber
X\times\{\alpha_{0}\}
.

Proof.

We consider the familly
X\times\mathbb{D}
together with the line bundle
L\to X\times\mathbb{D}
induced by
\gamma
.
By the semi-continuity theorem of Grauert there exists a holomorphic section
U_{\gamma}
of
\displaystyle mK_{X\times\mathbb{D}}+L
extending
u
. In particular,
U_{\gamma}
varies smoothly with respect to the base variable

t
and it follows that we have

\int_{X\times\mathbb{D}}|U_{\gamma}|^{2}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}<\infty

(4.5.3)

by the property
(2)
of Lemma
4.4

Let
v
be the holomorphic function representing
U_{\gamma}
on some open subset

\Omega\times\mathbb{D}
.
Cauchy inequalities show that we have

|\partial_{t}v(x,0)|^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{m,L}(z)}\leqslant C\int_{\mathbb{D}}|v(x,t)|^{2}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}d\lambda(t)

(4.5.4)

for some constant
C
, and by integrating this equality with respect to
z
it follows that

\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}v(x,0)|^{2}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}d\lambda(x)<\infty

(4.5.5)

since the RHS of (
4.5.4
) is bounded by (
4.5.3
).

In order to obtain the Lie derivative operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}
we are using the Chern connection

D^{\prime}
associated to the metric
h_{m,L}
on the line bundle
(m-1)K_{\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}}+L
, whose local expression is given by the following

\partial-\sum_{j}\frac{e^{\psi_{j}}}{\sum_{i}e^{\psi_{i}}}\partial\psi_{j}-\partial\varphi_{L}.

(4.5.6)

Then we have the equality

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{\gamma})}{dt}=\partial_{t}v(x,0)-v(x,0)\sum_{j}\frac{e^{\psi_{j}}}{\sum_{i}e^{\psi_{i}}}\partial_{t}\psi_{j}-v(x,0)\partial_{t}\varphi_{L}

(4.5.7)

locally on
\Omega
.

By equality (
4.3.5
) we see that
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{j}=-\frac{m-1}{m}\partial_{t}\varphi_{j}
so we finally infer that we have

\int_{X}\left|\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}(U_{\gamma})}{dt}\right|^{2}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}<\infty,

(4.5.8)

which shows that Lemma
4.5
holds true.
∎

After these preparations, we next present our second proof of
4.1
.

Proof of
4.1
.

Let
\alpha_{0}\in Z_{k,m}
be a generic point.
Consider a germ of a holomorphic disk
\gamma:(\mathbb{D},0)\to Z_{k,m}
such that
\gamma(0)=\alpha_{0}
.
We denote by
\dot{\alpha}_{0}
the derivative
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
.
Let
u\in H^{0}(X,mK_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}})
.

We aim to solve the following system of equations:

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}u_{1}=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u,\\
\bar{\partial}u_{2}=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{1},\\
\vdots\\
\bar{\partial}u_{i+1}=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{i},\\
\vdots\end{cases}

(4.5.9)

such that each
u_{i}
is
L^{2}
with respect to
h_{m,L}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Since
\alpha_{0}
is generic in
Z_{k,m}
, we can apply Lemma
4.5
to
\gamma
.
Hence, the first equation of (
4.5.9
) admits an
L^{2}
solution with respect to
h_{m,L}
.
Let
u_{1}
be the solution of

\bar{\partial}u_{1}=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u,

(4.5.10)

such that the
L^{2}
-norm of
u_{1}
is minimal.
Then
u_{1}
is orthogonal to the harmonic part
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta^{\prime\prime}
.
By
[
CP23
, Thm. 2.2]
,
u_{1}
is
D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}}
-exact, and there exists a
(n-1,0)
-form
v
with values in
(m-1)K_{X}+L_{\alpha_{0}}
such that

u_{1}=D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}}v,\qquad\int_{X}|v|_{\omega_{0}}^{2}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}\,dV_{\omega_{0}}<\infty.

(4.5.11)

Now we solve the second equation.
We claim that

\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{1}\in\operatorname{Im}(\bar{\partial}).

Indeed, the form
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{1}
is
L^{2}
and
\bar{\partial}
-closed.
It suffices to show that

\int_{X}\langle\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}}v,\beta\rangle_{\omega_{0}}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}\,dV_{\omega_{0}}=0

(4.5.12)

for any
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-harmonic
(n,1)
-form
\beta
that is
L^{2}
.
This is clear since
\beta
automatically belongs to the domain of
(D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}})^{\star}
, and by the Bochner formula we have

(D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}})^{\star}\beta=0,

(4.5.13)

by the curvature property (3) of Lemma
4.4
.

The form
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge v
is
L^{2}
by (
4.5.11
), so we can write

\int_{X}\langle\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}}v,\beta\rangle_{\omega_{0}}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}\,dV_{\omega_{0}}=-\int_{X}\langle\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge v,(D^{\prime}_{h_{m,L}})^{\star}\beta\rangle_{\omega_{0}}e^{-\varphi_{m,L}}\,dV_{\omega_{0}}=0,

(4.5.14)

where the last equality follows from (
4.5.13
).
As a consequence,
\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{1}\in\operatorname{Im}(\bar{\partial})
, and we can find
u_{2}
such that
u_{2}
is
L^{2}
with respect to
h_{m,L}
and

\bar{\partial}u_{2}=\dot{\alpha}_{0}\wedge u_{1}.

By repeating the same argument, we can solve the system (
4.5.9
) to any order.

Finally, using
[
Lev83
]
or the convergence argument in the proof of
3.1
, we know that the segment
\alpha_{0}+z\cdot\dot{\alpha}_{0}
lies in
Z_{k,m}
.
This implies that
Z_{k,m}
is a finite union of translates of subtori in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Note that we have the twisted strong Hodge decomposition from
[
CP23
, Thm. 2.2]
.
Together with the above argument, we can easily obtain the following twisted version of
4.1
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold, and let
F
be a pseudo-effective line bundle on
X
.
Let
h_{F}
be a possibly singular Hermitian metric on
F
such that
i\Theta_{h_{F}}(F)\geqslant 0
.
Assume that
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies
\mathcal{I}_{X}(h_{F}^{\frac{2}{m}})=\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the jumping locus

Z_{k,m}:=\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Jac}(X)\mid h^{0}(X,mK_{X}+F+L_{\alpha})\geqslant k\}

(4.6.1)

is a union of subtori in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.

In particular, let
F=F_{1}+\sum a_{i}E_{i}
, where
F_{1}
is a semipositive
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle and

\sum\frac{a_{i}}{k}[E_{i}]
is a klt
\mathbb{Q}
-effective divisor.
Then
Z_{k,m}

is a finite union of translates of subtori in
\mathrm{Jac}(X)
.

5
Further results involving infinitesimal extension of sections

In this section, we consider the following data.

•

p:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{D}
is a non-singular, proper family of Calabi-Yau Kähler manifolds, in the sense that
\displaystyle K_{X}\equiv 0
is trivial, where
X
is the central fiber.

•

L\to\mathcal{X}
is a holomorphic line bundle on
\mathcal{X}
, and
\displaystyle L_{0}:=L|_{X}
be the restriction of
L
to the central fiber
X
of the family
p
.

•

There exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and
s_{0},\dots,s_{N}\in H^{0}(X,mL|_{X})
a basis such that
\{s_{i}=0\}_{i=0}^{N}
has no common zeroes.

An important problem in this context is the following.

Conjecture 5.1
.

The function
\displaystyle t\to h^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{t},L|_{\mathcal{X}_{t}})
is constant.

Conjecture
5.1
holds when
X
is hyperkähler by a result of Matsushita,
[
Mat16
]
(see also
[
Voi92
]
). The case
\kappa(L|_{X})=\dim X-1
is due to Kollár
[
Kol15
]
.

In this context, our result is as follows.

Theorem 5.2
.

We assume that there exists a non-identically zero section
s
of
L_{0}
that admits an extension to
\mathcal{X}
. Then every holomorphic section
of
\displaystyle L_{0}
above extends to arbitrary order.

Proof.

To begin with, recall the following result established in
[
CP23
]
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}_{K}
be a set of line bundles
on
\mathcal{X}
such that

(1-r_{0})c_{1}(L)=r_{0}c_{1}(K_{\mathcal{X}})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}r_{i}c_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{i}),

(5.2.1)

where
0\leqslant r_{0}<1
are rational numbers and
r_{j}\geqslant 0
for
j=2,\dots,K
.
In (
5.2.1
) we denote by
c_{1}(\mathcal{E})
the first Chern class of
\mathcal{E}
.
Let
s
be a section of

\mathcal{F}_{k}:=\left(K_{\mathcal{X}}+L\right)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}/t^{k+1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},

and and for each
i=1,\dots,K
let
\sigma_{i}
be a section of the sheaf
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}/t^{k+2}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
(notice that this is one order higher than
s
). We denote by
h_{L}
the metric induced on the bundle
L_{0}
by the set of holomorphic sections
(s,\sigma_{i})
restricted to the central fiber
–notice that this makes sense thanks to (
5.2.1
).

Then the following holds true.

Theorem 5.3
.

[
CP23
]
We consider
\displaystyle(s_{0},\sigma_{i})_{i=1,\dots,K}
a family of holomorphic sections of
\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{k},\mathcal{E}_{i})_{i=1,\dots,K}
respectively as above. We assume that
s_{0}
admits a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
extension
s_{k}
such that if we write

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}s_{k}=t^{k+1}\Lambda_{k}

then

\int_{X}\left|\frac{\Lambda_{k}}{dt}\right|^{2}e^{-(1-\varepsilon)\varphi_{L}}dV<\infty

(5.3.1)

for any positive
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a section
\widehat{s}
of
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}
such that
s=\pi_{k}(\widehat{s})
.

In order to conclude the proof, it is sufficient to show that every section
s_{0}
of
L_{0}
with the following properties

•

the zero set
\Xi_{0}:=(s_{0}=0)
is non-singular;

•

the restriction
\displaystyle s|_{\Xi_{0}}
is not identically zero

extends to
\mathcal{X}
. This is clear, because these requirements are satisfied by the generic section of
L_{0}
, given the set-up at the beginning
of the current section.

The fact that the
L^{2}
-hypothesis (
5.3.1
) in Theorem
5.3
is satisfied follows from the following well-known statement.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}_{(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)}
be a holomorphic function defined near the origin, whose restriction to
z_{1}=0
is not identically zero.
Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the integral

\int_{(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)}\frac{d\lambda}{|f|^{2\varepsilon_{0}}|z_{1}|^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}}}<\infty

is convergent locally near zero.

Proof.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset such that
\overline{\Omega}
is contained in the definition domain of
f
. We denote by
\Omega_{0}:=\Omega\cap(z_{1}=0)
the intersection of
\Omega
with the hyperplane
(z_{1}=0)
. Then there exists a positive constant

\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the integral

\int_{\Omega_{0}}\frac{d\lambda}{|f|^{2\varepsilon_{0}}}<\infty

is convergent. This means that the function identically
1
is integrable with respect to the weight
\displaystyle\varphi:=\varepsilon_{0}\log|f|^{2}
. By Ohsawa-Takegoshi there exists a holomorphic function
F\in\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
such that

F|_{\Omega_{0}}=1,\qquad\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\frac{e^{-\varphi}d\lambda}{|z_{1}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{1}|^{2}}<\infty.

From this, our lemma follows immediately.
∎

We now apply Theorem
5.3
with the following data

K:=1,\quad\mathcal{E}_{1}:=L,\quad\sigma_{1}:=s,\quad 0<r_{1}\ll 1,

and by Lemma
5.4
, the integrability requirements in
5.3
are automatically satisfied. Repeated applications of this procedure shows that

s_{0}
extends to an open neighborhood of the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. The proof of
5.2
is complete.
∎

6
Quasi-compact version

In the remaining sections, we would like to generalise the previous results to the quasi-compact case. To begin with,
let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be a SNC divisor on
X
. Write
X\setminus D:=X-\sum D_{i}
. A logarithmic flat bundle
(L,\nabla)
consists of a holomorphic line bundle
L
on
X
and a logarithmic holomorphic connection

\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)

(6.0.1)

so that
\nabla^{2}=0
. The space of logarithmic flat bundles is denoted by
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.
We consider
M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
the space of rank
1
local system on
X\setminus D
. Then we have

M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(\pi_{1}(X\setminus D),\mathbb{C}^{\star})\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{\star})^{\ell}

where
\ell=b_{1}(X\setminus D)
.
Then there is a natural map

\rho:M_{\rm DR}(X/D)\to M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D).

(6.0.2)

Note that
[
Del70
, II, 6.10]
implies that this map is surjective, namely give a flat bundle on
X_{0}
, we can extend it to be a holomorphic line bundle on the total space
X
and the flat connection might have simple poles along
D
.
Moreover the kernel of this map is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
: given a log flat bundle
(L,\nabla)
, we can construct new log flat bundles
L\otimes\prod\mathcal{I}_{D_{i}}^{a_{i}}
for any
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\nabla
induces a flat connection
\nabla^{\prime}
on

L\otimes\prod\mathcal{I}_{D_{i}}^{a_{i}}
with log pole along
D
. We have
\rho((L,\nabla))=\rho(L\otimes\prod\mathcal{I}_{D_{i}}^{a_{i}},\nabla^{\prime})
.

Given a local system
\tau\in M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
, and let
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
such that
\rho((L,\nabla))=\tau
.
Let
H^{p}(X\setminus D,\tau)
be the cohomology of the locally constant sheaf of flat sections of
\tau
.
Note that the connection
\nabla
induces a complex

0\to\mathcal{O}(L)\to\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\to\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{2}(\log D)\to\cdots\mathcal{O}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{n}(\log D)\to 0.

(6.0.3)

Let
\mathbb{H}^{p}(X,L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D))
be the hypercohomology of the complex.
We have the following result due to Deligne

Theorem 6.1
.

[
Del70
, II, 6.10]

If the residue of
(L,\nabla)
on
D_{i}
is not a positive integer for every
i
, then we have

\mathbb{H}^{p}(X,L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D))\simeq H^{p}(X\setminus D,\tau)
.

The definition of the residue is as follows:

Remark 6.2
.

Here if
D_{i}
is locally defined by
z_{1}=0
, by the compactness of
D_{i}
, there exists some constant
a_{i}
such that
\nabla e_{L}=\frac{a_{i}dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge e_{L}+C^{\infty}
. We call
a_{i}
be the residue of
(L,\nabla)
on
D_{i}
. It is independent of the choice of the base
e_{L}
.

If there is a metric
h
on
L
such that
\nabla=D^{\prime}_{h}
, then
a_{i}
equals to the Lelong number of
h
on
D_{i}
by using the connection formula. In the case when
\nabla
does not come from a Chern connection, we have a similar result due to Y. Deng
[
Den23
, Lemma 2.3]
.

The (lengthy) proof of the following result unfolds in the coming sections. More precisely, it follows directly from Theorem
10.22
.

Theorem 6.3
.

[=
0.5
]
Let
\tau\in M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
and we consider a germ of holomorphic disk
\gamma:(\mathbb{C},0)\to M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
such that
\gamma(0)=\tau
and such that

\dim H^{p}(X\setminus D,\gamma(t))\geqslant k

(6.3.1)

for every
|t|\ll 1
.
We denote by
\dot{\alpha}
the infinitesimal deformation
d\gamma(0)
. Then

\dim H^{p}(X\setminus D,\tau+z\dot{\alpha})\geqslant k

(6.3.2)

for
|z|\ll 1
. Here
H^{p}(X\setminus D,\tau)
is cohomology with respect to the local system
\tau
.

6.1
Differential operators and harmonic metrics in log setting

We discuss here the existence of harmonic metrics in the log setting, i.e. corresponding to
(X,D)
. For a general presentation
we refer to
[
Moc07
]
. Since we are aiming at a reasonable self-contained paper, the proof of the special case we are interested is presented here in detail.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let

\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log\sum D_{i})

be our flat holomorphic connection. Set
D_{K}:=\bar{\partial}+\nabla
, where
\bar{\partial}
defines the complex structure of
L
. Then
D_{K}^{2}=0
on
X_{0}:=X\setminus\sum D_{i}
.

We now recall the definition of harmonic metrics.
Let
h
be a metric on
L
smooth on
X_{0}
and with possible analytic singularity along
\sum D_{i}
. Let
D^{\prime}_{h}
be the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection on
(X,L,h)
.
Set

-2\theta_{0}:=D^{\prime}_{h}-\nabla.

Then
\theta_{0}
is a
(1,0)
-form with log pole along
\sum D_{i}
. Since
\nabla^{2}=0
and
(D^{\prime}_{h})^{2}=0
, we have

\partial\theta_{0}=0.

(6.3.3)

We now follow the definitions of
[
Sim92
, Section 1]
(but in order to be coincide with the our contexts, the notations of the connections are not the same). Note that

\{\theta_{0}e,e\}=\{e,\overline{\theta}_{0}e\}.

Then the two connections
D^{\prime}_{h}+\bar{\partial}
and
\nabla+(\bar{\partial}-2\overline{\theta}_{0})
preserve the metric
h
. We can thus define
D_{K}^{\prime\prime}
as follows:

Definition 6.4
.

We set

D^{\prime\prime}_{K}:=\frac{\bar{\partial}+(\bar{\partial}-2\overline{\theta}_{0})}{2}+\frac{\nabla-D^{\prime}_{h}}{2}=\bar{\partial}+{\theta_{0}-\overline{\theta}_{0}}

(6.4.1)

and

D^{\prime}_{K}:=D^{\prime}_{h}+\theta_{0}+\overline{\theta}_{0}

(6.4.2)

From the definition, we know that

D^{\prime\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime}_{K}=D_{K}=\bar{\partial}+\nabla.

however,
D^{\prime\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime}_{K}
does not preserve the metric.

Definition 6.5

(Harmonic metric)
.

We set
G_{K}:=(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{2}
.
We say that
h
is a harmonic metric, if
G_{K}=0
on
X_{0}
.

In our rank
1
case, we have the following direct proposition

Proposition 6.6
.

h
is harmonic if and only if
i\Theta_{h}=0
on
X_{0}
.

Proof.

Thanks to (
6.3.3
), we have
\bar{\partial}\overline{\theta}=0
. Then we obtain

G_{K}=\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}.

(6.6.1)

Note that
i\Theta_{h}=[\bar{\partial},D^{\prime}_{h}]=-2\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}
. Then
G_{K}=0
on
X_{0}
if and only if
i\Theta_{h}=0
on
X_{0}
.
∎

We have the following important remarks.

Proposition 6.7
.

[
Sim92
, section 1]

Let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on
X_{0}
and let
(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star},(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{\star}
be the formal adjoint of
D^{\prime}_{K},D^{\prime\prime}_{K}
with respect to
(h,\omega)
.
Then the equalities

(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star}=i[\Lambda_{\omega},D^{\prime\prime}_{K}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{\star}=-i[\Lambda_{\omega},D^{\prime}_{K}]

(6.7.1)

hold true.

If
h
is harmonic, then

\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}=\Delta^{\prime}_{K}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{K}\qquad\text{on }X_{0},

(6.7.2)

where
\Delta_{K}:=[D_{K},D^{\star}_{K}]=[\partial+\nabla,(\partial+\nabla)^{\star}]
. Moreover,
D^{\prime\prime}_{K},D^{\prime}_{K},(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{\star},(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star}
commute with the Lapalces
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K},\Delta^{\prime}_{K}
.

Proof.

Since
\theta
is a
(1,0)
-form, we have

\theta^{\star}=-i[\Lambda_{\omega},\overline{\theta}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\overline{\theta})^{\star}=i[\Lambda_{\omega},\theta].

The curical point is that the second equation changes the sign.
Together with the basic Kähler identiy
(D^{\prime}_{h})^{\star}=i[\Lambda_{\omega},\bar{\partial}]
, we obtain

(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star}=i[\Lambda_{\omega},D^{\prime\prime}_{K}].

The second equality in (
6.7.1
) is proved in the same way.

Suppose next that
h
is harmonic. Since
D^{\prime\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime}_{K}=D_{K}=\bar{\partial}+\nabla
, we have

(D^{\prime\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime}_{K})^{2}=(\bar{\partial}+\nabla)^{2}=0.

Since
h
is harmonic, we have
(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{2}=(D^{\prime}_{K})^{2}=0
.
Then we obtain

[D^{\prime\prime}_{K},D^{\prime}_{K}]=D^{\prime\prime}_{K}D^{\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime}_{K}D^{\prime\prime}_{K}=0.

Together with (
6.7.1
) and the Jacobi equality, we know that

\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}=[D^{\prime\prime}_{K},(D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{\star}]=[D^{\prime}_{K},(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star}]=\Delta^{\prime}_{K}.

Finally, (
6.7.1
) and the Jacobi equality imply that
[(D^{\prime}_{K})^{\star},D^{\prime\prime}_{K}]=0
. Then

\Delta_{K}=[D^{\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime\prime}_{K},(D^{\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime\prime}_{K})^{\star}]=\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}+\Delta^{\prime}_{K}=2\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}.

By the Jacobi equality, we also have

[D^{\prime\prime}_{K},\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}]=0.

∎

Before proving the existence of harmonic metrics, we first explain the relationship between
c_{1}(L)
and
\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
where
a_{i}
is the residue of
\nabla
along
D_{i}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
a_{i}
be the residue of
\nabla
along
D_{i}
. Then we have

\sum\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]=c_{1}(L)\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R})

(6.8.1)

and

\sum\mathrm{Im}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]=0\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R}).

(6.8.2)

Proof.

Fix a smooth metric
h_{0}
on
L
. Then
D^{\prime}_{h_{0}}=\nabla+\theta_{0}
for some
(1,0)
-form
\theta_{0}
with log pole along
\sum D_{i}
. Since
h_{0}
is smooth on
(X,L)
, we know that
near
D_{i}=\{z_{i}=0\}
,
\theta_{0}=-a_{i}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+C^{\infty}
, where
a_{i}
is the residue of
\nabla
along
D_{i}
.

Now as
\nabla
is holomorphic, we have
[\bar{\partial},\nabla]=0
on
X_{0}
. Then

i\Theta_{h_{0}}(L)=[\bar{\partial},D^{\prime}_{h_{0}}]=i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}\qquad\text{ on }X_{0}.

(6.8.3)

On the other hand, since
\theta_{0}=-a_{i}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+C^{\infty}
, the divisorial part of the current
i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}
is
-\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
.
Note that
\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}
is exact in the sense of currents on
X
. Then (
6.8.3
) implies that

c_{1}(L)=[i\Theta_{h_{0}}(L)]=-i[\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}]_{\text{Div part}}=\sum a_{i}[D_{i}].

Note that
a_{i}
is a complex number, the RHS is written as
\sum\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]+i(\sum\mathrm{Im}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}])
. As
c_{1}(L)\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R})
, we obtain (
6.8.1
) and (
6.8.2
).
∎

We are now ready to establish the existence of harmonic metrics for line bundles.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
. Then there exists a unique harmonic metric (modulo constant)
h
on
L
such that the Lelong number of
h
along
D_{i}
is
\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})
for every
i
, where
a_{i}
is the residue of
\nabla
along
D_{i}
.

Moreover, if we have
c_{1}(L)=\sum b_{i}D_{i}
for some
b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, then there exists a unique harmonic metric (modulo constant)
h^{\prime}
on
L
such that the Lelong number of
h^{\prime}
along
D_{i}
is
b_{i}
for every
i
.

Proof.

We fix a smooth metric
h_{0}
on
L
and set
\theta_{0}:=D^{\prime}_{h_{0}}-\nabla
be a smooth
(1,0)
-form of log pole along
\sum D_{i}
.
Let
h:=h_{0}e^{-\phi}
for some real function
\phi
of analytic singularity along
\sum D_{i}
. Thanks to (
6.6.1
), we have

G_{K}=\bar{\partial}(\theta_{0}-\partial\phi).

Then
h
is harmonic if and only if we have

i\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=-i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}\qquad\text{ on }X_{0}.

To prove the theorem, it is thus sufficient to solve the above
\partial\bar{\partial}
-equation.

In Proposition
6.8
, we showed that the divisorial part of
i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}
is
-\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
. Together with (
6.8.3
), we know that the current

T:=i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}+i(\sum\mathrm{Im}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}])

is a real
(1,1)
-current on
X
. Recall that (
6.8.2
) says that the class of
(\sum\mathrm{Im}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}])
is
0
. Then
[T]=0\in H^{1,1}(X)
. In other word,
T
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact in the sense of current on
X
. Moreover, thanks to (
6.3.3
),
T
is also
d
-closed. Then
T
is a real
(1,1)
-current,
d
-closed and
\bar{\partial}
-exact on
X
. By the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma, we can always find a real function
\phi
such that

i\partial\bar{\partial}\phi=-T=-i\bar{\partial}\theta_{0}-i(\sum\mathrm{Im}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}])\qquad\text{ on }X.

(6.9.1)

As a consequence, for the metric
h:=h_{0}e^{-\phi}
, the curvature

2G_{K}=\bar{\partial}(\theta_{0}-\partial\phi)\equiv 0\qquad\text{ on }X_{0}.

Then
h
is a harmonic metric. Moreover, as the divisorial part of
-T
is
\sum\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]
, (
6.9.1
) implies that the Lelong number of
h
along
D_{i}
equals to
\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})
.

For the second part, by using Proposition
6.8
, we know that

\sum\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]=\sum b_{i}[D_{i}]=c_{1}(L)\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R}).

Then there exists a real function
\psi
such that
i\partial\bar{\partial}\psi=\sum b_{i}[D_{i}]-\sum\mathrm{Re}\,(a_{i})[D_{i}]
. Then
h^{\prime}:=h\cdot e^{-\psi}
is also harmonic and the Lelong number of
h^{\prime}
along
D_{i}
is
b_{i}
.
∎

Let
h
be a harmonic metric on
L|_{X\setminus D}
. By using (
6.6.1
), we know that

\theta:=D^{\prime}_{h}-\nabla\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))

is a holomorphic
1
-form with log pole. We have the following easy observation.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\theta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))
.
There exists a complex-valued function
\phi
of
\log|s_{D}|
-type singularity along
\sum D_{i}
such that
\partial\phi\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))
and

\theta-\partial\phi\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}).

Proof.

Note that
\bar{\partial}\theta
is a
d
-closed by
[
Del71
,
Nog95
]
and
\bar{\partial}
-exact current. By the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma, we have
\bar{\partial}\theta=\bar{\partial}\partial\phi
on
X
for some
\phi
. Then
\theta-\partial\phi
is a
\bar{\partial}
-closed
(1,0)
-current on
X
. Then
\theta-\partial\phi\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor. Let
A
be a connected component of
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.
We decompose

D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3},

where
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
corresponds to the components such that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}}(L,\nabla)
is invariant for every
(L,\nabla)\in A
.
Here
\Delta_{1}
(resp.
\Delta_{3}
) corresponds to the components such that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{1}}(L,\nabla)\in\mathbb{Z}
(
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{3}}(L,\nabla)\notin\mathbb{Z}
)

We denote
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}
. Then there exists
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}c_{1}(D_{i})=0
and
a_{i}\neq 0
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.

Proof.

Let
(L,\nabla),(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
be two points in
A
. Since
c_{1}(L)=c_{1}(L^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{Res}\,
is invariant on
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
. We have

0=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,(L,\nabla)_{D_{i}})-\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})_{D_{i}}))[D_{i}].

We claim that for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, there exists a
\{a_{i,j}\}_{j=1,\ldots,k}\subset\mathbb{\mathbb{Q}}^{k}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{i,j}c_{1}(D_{j})=0

and
a_{i,i}\neq 0
. In fact, since
\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}
is not invariant, we can find two
(L,\nabla),(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in A
such that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}((L,\nabla))\neq\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}((L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime}))
. Then we can find some real numbers
b_{i,j}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}b_{i,j}c_{1}(D_{j})=0

and
b_{i,i}\neq 0
. Note that
c_{1}(D_{1}),\ldots,c_{1}(D_{k})\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})=H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}
. Then for every
i
, we can find
\{a_{i,j}\}_{j=1,\ldots,k}\subset\mathbb{\mathbb{Q}}^{k}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{i,j}c_{1}(D_{j})=0

and
a_{i,i}\neq 0
.

By the above result, we can find rational numbers
\varepsilon_{k}\gg\cdots\gg\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for the sum

\varepsilon_{k}(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{k,j}c_{1}(D_{j}))+\cdots+\varepsilon_{1}(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{1,j}c_{1}(D_{j}))=:\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}^{\prime}c_{1}(D_{j}),

we have
a_{j}^{\prime}\neq 0
for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
The lemma is thus proved.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor. Let
\mathrm{Res}\,:M_{\rm DR}(X/D)\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
be the residue map, namely for every
(L,\nabla)\in A
,

\mathrm{Res}\,(L,\nabla):=(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{1}}(L,\nabla),\cdots,\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(L,\nabla)).

We shall decompose

D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}

as in Lemma
6.11
, namely
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
corresponds to the components such that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}}(L,\nabla)
is invariant for every
(L,\nabla)\in A
.
Here
\Delta_{1}
(resp.
\Delta_{3}
) corresponds to the components such that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{1}}(L,\nabla)\in\mathbb{Z}
(
\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{3}}(L,\nabla)\notin\mathbb{Z}
)

Let
A
be any connected component of
M_{\rm B}(X_{0})
. Then for any representation
\varrho\in A
, there exists some
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
whose monodromy representation is
\varrho
and there is a (non-unique) harmonic metric
h
for
(L,\nabla)
such that

•

For every
\beta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))
,
\mathrm{Res}\,\beta=0
along
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
.

•

\mathrm{Res}\,\theta=0
on
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
and
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta\neq 0
on every component of
\Delta_{2}
.

•

The Lelong number
\nu(h_{L})\in]-1,0]\cap\mathbb{Q}
on every component of
D
. Moroever,
\nu(h_{L})=0
on
\Delta_{1}
and
\nu(h_{L})\in]-1,0[\cap\mathbb{Q}
for every component of
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
.

Proof.

For the first point, by
[
Del71
,
Nog95
]
, we know that
\bar{\partial}\beta
is
d
-closed and
\bar{\partial}
-exact on
X
. Then
\bar{\partial}\beta
is
\partial\bar{\partial}
-exact.
On the other hand, we have
\bar{\partial}\beta=\sum\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}(\beta)[D_{i}]
. Then we have

\sum\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}(\beta)c_{1}([D_{i}])=0.

We now prove that
\mathrm{Res}\,(\beta)=0
over
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
.
Let
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]=0
. Let
L_{0}
be a trivial line bundle on
X
. Then
e^{-\prod a_{i}\log|s_{D_{i}}|^{2}}
defines a harmonic metric on
L_{0}
. It induces a
(1,0)
-connection
D^{\prime}
. Then
(L\otimes L_{0},\nabla\otimes D^{\prime})
is a flat log holomorphic connection whose residue is

\mathrm{Res}\,(L\otimes L_{0},\nabla\otimes D^{\prime})=(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{1}}(L,\nabla)+a_{1},\cdots,\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(L,\nabla)+a_{k}).

In particular, the pair

(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{1}}(L,\nabla)+\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}(\beta)),\cdots,\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(L,\nabla)+\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(\beta)))

and

(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{1}}(L,\nabla)+\mathrm{Im}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{i}}(\beta)),\cdots,\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(L,\nabla)+\mathrm{Im}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(\beta)))

belong to the image of
\mathrm{Res}\,:A\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
.
Then
\mathrm{Res}\,(\beta)=0
over
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
.

For the second part, we first fix a
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
such that its monodromy representation is
\rho
.
By applying
6.11
, there exists a harmonic metric
h_{L}
such that the Lelong number
\nu(h_{L})
is rational on every component of
D
.
Set
\theta:=\nabla-D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}
. By applying the first point, we know that
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta=0
on
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
. We assume that
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i\in I}[D_{i}]
.
By
6.11
, there exists
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}c_{1}[D_{i}]=0
and
b_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\star}
.
Then
h_{L}\cdot e^{-\varepsilon\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}\log|s_{i}|^{2}}
defines also a harmonic metric on
L
. For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
generic, we know that
\mathrm{Res}\,(\nabla-D^{\prime}_{h_{L}\cdot e^{-\varepsilon\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}\log|s_{i}|^{2}}})\neq 0
on
\Delta_{2}
.
Replacing
h_{L}
by
h_{L}\cdot e^{-\varepsilon\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}\log|s_{i}|^{2}}
, we know that
h_{L}
satisfies the second point.

For the third point, by construction,
\nu(h_{L})
is rational on every component of
D
.
Then
i\Theta_{h}(L)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}[D_{i}]
where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Replacing
L
by
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lfloor a_{i}+1\rfloor D_{i}))
, we may assume that
a_{i}\in]-1,0]\cap\mathbb{Q}
for each
i
. Now as the monodromy is zero along
\Delta_{1}
, we know that
a_{i}=0
on
\Delta_{1}
.

For
\nu(h_{L})
along
\Delta_{2}
, since
b_{i}\neq 0
for every
i\in I
, for a generic
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
, we know that the Lelong number of
h_{L}
along
\Delta_{2}
is not an integer. Then we know that
a_{i}\in]-1,0[\cap\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

In general, we cannot modify further the component
\Delta_{1}
. For example, let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D_{1}\subset X
be a smooth irreducible divisor.
We consider
M_{\rm DR}(X/D_{1})
. Let
A
be a connected component of
M_{\rm DR}(X/D_{1})
such that for every
(L,\nabla)\in A
,
c_{1}(L)=[D_{1}]\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R})
. Then the only harmonic metric on
L
is the singular metric
h_{L}
such that
i\Theta_{h_{L}}(L)=[D_{1}]
. We cannot find a harmonic metric such that the Lelong number along
D_{1}
is not integer.

Note that in this case, for every
(L,\nabla)\in A
, the residue of
(L,\nabla)
is an integer and the monodromy of
(L,\nabla)
along
D_{1}
is trivial. Let
\tau
be the local system of
(L,\nabla)
. If we would like to study the cohomology of
H^{\bullet}(X_{0},\tau)
, since
\tau
is trivial along
D_{1}
, if
X
is simply connected, we have
H^{\bullet}(X_{0},\tau)\simeq H^{\bullet}(X_{0},\mathbb{C})
. In general, it is not isomorphic to
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbb{C})
. In other words, the
\log D_{1}
in the hypercohomology
\mathbb{H}^{\bullet}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}(\log D_{1}))
is necessary.

Remark 6.14
.

As we can see from the previous results, the three components
\displaystyle(\Delta_{i})_{i}
of the divisor
D
have different geometric interpretations. Loosely speaking, the singularities of the metrics we will consider in the next subsections reflect this.

7
Technicalities I: elliptic estimates for
\Delta_{K}

The aim of this section is to establish some elliptic estimates for the Laplace operator
\Delta_{K}
.
In this section, we are always in the following setting.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
a SNC divisor on
X
. Write
X_{0}:=X-\sum D_{i}
. A logarithmic flat bundle
(L,\nabla)
consists of a holomorphic line bundle
L
on
X
and a logarithmic holomorphic connection

\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)

so that
\nabla^{2}=0
.
Recall that thanks to Proposition
6.12
, after replacing
(L,\nabla)
by another
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
with the same monodromy representation,
we can find a harmonic metric
h_{L}
on
L
such that we have the decomposition

D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}

with the following properties.
Set
\theta:=D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}-\nabla\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D))
.

Property 7.1
.

We assume that the following hold.

(1)

At a generic point of
\Delta_{1}
we have
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta=0
and
h_{L}
is smooth.

(2)

We have
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta\neq 0
for every component of
\Delta_{2}
, and
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta=0
for every component of
\Delta_{3}
.

(3)

The Lelong number of
h_{L}
belongs to
]-1,0[\cap\mathbb{Q}
for every component of
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
.

7.1
Laplace operators on smooth/log forms

Here we first define two types of Laplace operators
\Delta_{K}
, acting on
L
-valued smooth forms, and on

L
-valued smooth forms with log poles along the divisor
\Delta_{1}
, respectively.

For the latter, our strategy is to take advantage of the vector bundle structure of the logarithmic tangent bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
. More precisely, a
(p,q)
-form with log poles is seen as
(0,q)
-form with values in the vector bundle
\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})
.
If we disregard
L
, as soon as we fix a Hermitian metric on
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
we can define the adjoint of the exterior derivative

\partial:\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\to\Omega^{p+1}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})

and a corresponding Laplace operator.

Next we show that Hodge decomposition in the presence of the divisor
D
still holds:
this will be our main result in Section
8
. Our arguments mainly rely on the new a-priori inequalities we establish in the next subsection, cf.
7.2
. This is the reason why we need a detailed analysis of the properties of the metrics we introduce next next.

7.1.1
Metric structure on the (orbifold) tangent bundle of
X_{0}

Let
\omega_{D}
be a metric with Poincaré singularities along the support of the divisor
\Delta_{2}
, and with conic singularities of angles

\displaystyle 2\pi(1-1/k)
on each component of
\Delta_{3}
, where
k\geqslant 3
is a positive integer, sufficiently divisible.
In particular,
\omega_{D}
is smooth at a generic point of
\Delta_{1}
.

More specifically, we set
\displaystyle\Delta_{2}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
and
\Delta_{3}:=\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}D_{i}.

Then we define

\omega_{D}:=\omega_{X}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}|s_{i}|^{2/k}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log\frac{1}{|s_{i}|^{2}}

(7.1.1)

where
\omega_{X}
is a fixed Kähler metric on
X
, sufficiently positive so that
\omega_{D}
above is greater than

1/2\omega_{X}
. In the equality (
7.1.1
) we denote by
s_{i}
the defining equation for
D_{i}
, measured with respect to a non-singular metric
on
\mathcal{O}(D_{i})
. The motivation for working with this precise type of metric will become clear later, in Theorem
8.7
.

Our next goal is to estimate the coefficients of the metric
\omega_{D}
and their inverses. By a direct calculation, one easily sees that the following equalities

\partial\bar{\partial}|s_{i}|^{2/k}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{\langle D^{\prime}s_{i},D^{\prime}s_{i}\rangle}{|s_{i}|^{2-2/k}}-\frac{|s_{i}|^{2/k}}{k}\theta_{i}

(7.1.2)

and

-\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log\frac{1}{|s_{i}|^{2}}=\frac{\langle D^{\prime}s_{i},D^{\prime}s_{i}\rangle}{|s_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}{|s_{i}|^{2}}}+\frac{\theta_{i}}{\log{|s_{i}|^{2}}}

(7.1.3)

hold. Here we denote by
\theta_{i}
the curvature form of a fixed, non-singular metric on
\mathcal{O}(D_{i})
.

We have the following statement.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset X
be an open subset. Assume that we can choose the coordinate system
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
on
\Omega
such that

\Delta_{2}\cap\Omega=(z_{1}\dots z_{p}=0),\qquad\Delta_{3}\cap\Omega=(z_{p+1}\dots z_{p+q}=0).

Let
(\omega_{i\overline{j}})_{i,j}
be the coefficients of the metric
\omega_{D}
with respect to
z
. Then the following are true.

(1)

There exist smooth functions
A_{k},A_{\overline{m}},C_{l}
and
\widetilde{A}_{k},\widetilde{A}_{\overline{m}},\widetilde{C}_{l}
so that we have
