\begin{split}\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=&g_{i\overline{j}}+\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{i,p}}{|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{i,p}A_{i}}{z_{i}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{j,q}A_{\overline{j}}}{\overline{z}_{j}\log^{2}|z_{j}|^{2}}\\
+&\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{i,q}e^{\varphi_{i}}}{|z_{i}|^{2-2/k}}+\frac{\delta_{i,q}|z_{i}|^{2/k}\widetilde{A}_{i}}{z_{i}}+\frac{\delta_{j,q}|z_{j}|^{2/k}\widetilde{A}_{\overline{j}}}{\overline{z}_{j}}\\
+&\sum_{l=1}^{p}\frac{C_{l}+C_{l,1}\log|z_{l}|^{2}}{\log^{2}|z_{l}|^{2}}+\sum_{l=p+1}^{p+q}\widetilde{C}_{l}|z_{l}|^{2/l}\\
\end{split}

(7.2.1)

where the matrix
(g_{\beta\overline{\alpha}})
is positive definite and has smooth coefficients. We denote by
\delta_{ij}
the Kronecker symbol, and
\delta_{i,p}
equals 1 if
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and zero if not. Also,
\delta_{i,q}
equals 1 if
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+q\}
and zero if not.

(2)

Let
(\omega^{\overline{j}i})_{i,j}
be the inverse of
(\omega_{i\overline{j}})
. Then for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that
i\neq j
we have

\omega^{\overline{i}i}(z)=(1+\psi_{i}(z))|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2},\qquad\omega^{\overline{j}i}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}.

The functions
\psi_{i},\psi_{ij}
are smooth on
\Omega\setminus D
, we have
|\psi_{i}|<1/2
and

\sup_{\Omega\setminus D}(|d\psi_{i}|_{\omega_{D}}+|d\psi_{ij}|_{\omega_{D}})<\infty.

(3)

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
j\in\{p+1,\dots,p+q\}
we have

\omega^{\overline{j}i}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}.

(4)

For each
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
we have

\omega^{\overline{j}i}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)z_{i}.

(5)

For each
i,j\in\{p+1,\dots,p+q\}
such that
i\neq j
we have

\omega^{\overline{i}i}(z)=(1+\psi_{i}(z))|z_{i}|^{2-2/k},\qquad\omega^{\overline{j}i}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}.

(6)

For each
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+q\}
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
we have

\omega^{\overline{j}i}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)z_{i}.

(7)

Finally, in case both indexes
i\neq j
are bigger than
p+1
, then we have

\omega^{i\overline{i}}(z)=1+\psi_{i}(z),\qquad\omega^{i\overline{j}}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)

where the functions
\psi
have the same regularity properties as in
(2)
and moreover are small in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-norm.

Proof.

The first point (1) is a simple, direct calculation following from (
7.1.2
) and (
7.2.1
), see also

[
Gue14
]
. It follows that for
i=1,\dots,p
we have

\omega_{i\overline{i}}(z)=\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\big(1+\psi_{i}(z)\big)

(7.2.2)

and for
i=p+1,\dots,p+q
the corresponding diagonal coefficient can be written as follows

\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2-2/k}}\big(1+\psi_{i}(z)\big).

(7.2.3)

In case
i\geqslant p+q+1
, we have

\omega_{i\overline{i}}(z)=g_{i\overline{i}}(z)\big(1+\psi_{i}(z)\big)

(7.2.4)

where
g_{i\overline{i}}
are smooth, strictly positive.
In the equations above (
7.2.2
) and (
7.2.4
) we denote by
(\psi_{i})
a set of smooth functions in the complement of
D
, such that

\sup_{\Omega\setminus D}|\psi_{i}|_{\omega_{D}}<1/2,\qquad\sup_{\Omega\setminus D}|d\psi_{i}|<\infty.

We have similar expression for the coefficients outside the diagonal, as follows.

If
i\neq j
are both smaller than
p
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\frac{\psi_{ij}(z)}{z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{j}|^{2}}

(7.2.5)

with the same type of function
\psi
as before. This is a consequence of the fact that the differential of

z_{i}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2},\qquad\overline{z}_{i}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}

for
i=1,\dots,p
is bounded on
\Omega\setminus D
when measured with respect to the Poincaré metric.

If
i\leqslant p
and
p+1\leqslant j\leqslant p+q
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\frac{|z_{j}|^{2/k}}{z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\psi_{ij}(z).

(7.2.6)

Here (as always) the function
\psi_{ij}(z)
is bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norm: this is due to the fact that

\sup_{\Omega\setminus D}|d(|z_{j}|^{-2/k}z_{j})|_{\omega_{D}}<\infty

for
p+1\leqslant j\leqslant p+q
. It is at this point that the hypothesis
k\geqslant 3
is required.

If
i\leqslant p
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\frac{1}{z_{i}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\psi_{ij}(z)

(7.2.7)

If
i\neq j
and
p\leqslant i,j\leqslant p+q
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\frac{|z_{i}|^{2/k}|z_{j}|^{2/k}}{z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}}\psi_{ij}(z)

(7.2.8)

If
p\leqslant i\leqslant p+q
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\frac{|z_{i}|^{2/k}}{z_{i}}\psi_{ij}(z).

(7.2.9)

If
i\neq j
are both greater than
p+q
, then we have

\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)=\psi_{ij}(z)

(7.2.10)

i.e. a function which is bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-norm.

From the relations (
7.2.2
)–(
7.2.10
) it follows that

\det\big(\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)\big)=\big(1+\psi(z)\big)\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\prod_{j=p+1}^{p+q}\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2-2/k}}.

(7.2.11)

Indeed, we have

\det\big(\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)\big)=\sum_{\sigma}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod\omega_{i\overline{\sigma_{i}}}(z).

(7.2.12)

Consider first the set of permutations
\sigma
such that
\sigma(i)=i
for all
i=1,\dots,p+q
.
The corresponding sum will be of type

\big(1+\psi(z)\big)\prod_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\prod_{j=p+1}^{p+q}\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{2-2/k}}.

(7.2.13)

as it follows from the formulas (
7.2.2
), (
7.2.3
) and (
7.2.4
).

Let now
\sigma
be a permutation such that at least one element of the set
\{1,\dots,p\}
is not fixed.
Then we have

\prod\omega_{i\overline{\sigma_{i}}}(z)=\frac{\psi(z)}{\prod_{i=1}^{p}|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{m_{i}}|z_{i}|^{2}\prod_{j=p+1}^{p+q}|z_{j}|^{2-n_{j}/k}}

(7.2.14)

where each integer
m_{i},n_{j}
in (
7.2.14
) are greater than two, and at least one of them is equal to 4. Therefore, the sum
(
7.2.13
) is the dominant factor in the expression (
7.2.12
), and our claim is proved.

We will not do the complete calculations in order to establish the points (2)-(7) of the lemma, but simply evaluate

\omega^{\overline{1}1}
and
\omega^{\overline{2}1}
. One can derive the expression for general
\omega^{\overline{j}i}
in a similar, tedious manner.

Consider
\omega^{\overline{1}1}
first, i.e.

\omega^{\overline{1}1}(z)=-\frac{1}{\det\big(\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)\big)}\sum\varepsilon(\tau)\prod\omega_{i\overline{\tau}_{i}}(z)

(7.2.15)

where
\tau:\{2,3,\dots,n\}\to\{2,3,\dots,n\}
is bijective. The terms appearing in the sum (
7.2.15
) are

\frac{\psi(z)}{\prod_{i\neq 1}|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{m_{i}}|z_{i}|^{2}\prod_{j\neq 1}|z_{j}|^{2-n_{j}/k}}

(7.2.16)

where each of the integers
m_{i},n_{j}
are greater or equal to two. By the expression of the determinant (
7.2.12
), we obtain

\omega^{\overline{1}1}(z)=\big(1+\psi(z)\big)|z_{1}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{1}|^{2}.

Assume that
p\geqslant 2
. We have

\omega^{\overline{2}1}(z)=-\frac{1}{\det\big(\omega_{i\overline{j}}(z)\big)}\sum\varepsilon(\tau)\prod\omega_{i\overline{\tau}_{i}}(z)

(7.2.17)

where
\tau:\{2,3,\dots,n\}\to\{1,3,\dots,n\}
is bijective. Hence each of the terms of the sum in (
7.2.17
)
can be written as

\frac{1}{z_{2}\overline{z}_{1}\log^{2}|z_{2}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{1}|^{2}}\frac{\psi(z)}{\prod_{i\neq 1,2}|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{m_{i}}|z_{i}|^{2}\prod_{j\geqslant p+1}|z_{j}|^{2-n_{j}/k}}

(7.2.18)

where each of the integers
m_{i},n_{j}
are at least two. The equality

\omega^{\overline{2}1}(z)=z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}\psi_{12}(z)

(7.2.19)

follows, again by using (
7.2.12
).

If for example
p=0,q\geqslant 2
, then the expression (
7.2.18
) above is to be replaced with

\frac{|z_{1}|^{2/k}|z_{2}|^{2/k}}{z_{2}\overline{z}_{1}}\frac{\psi(z)}{\prod_{j\geqslant 3}|z_{j}|^{2-n_{j}/k}}

(7.2.20)

with
n_{j}\geqslant 2
. Multiplication with the inverse of the determinant shows that the point (5) of our lemma holds.
∎

An important invariant associated with a Hermitian metric on a vector bundle is its curvature. In our setting, and despite the singularities of
\omega_{D}
, one has the next well-known fact.

Lemma 7.3
.

[
Gue14
]
There exists a positive constant
C>0
so that the inequality

-C\omega_{D}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T_{X}}\leqslant\sqrt{-1}\Theta(T_{X},\omega_{D})\leqslant C\omega_{D}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T_{X}}

(7.3.1)

holds on
X\setminus D
.

Proof.

There are many ways in which this statement can be established. For example, one could simply refer to
[
Gue14
]
(and set the parameter
\varepsilon
in
loc. cit.
to zero). An alternative argument is the following. We choose coordinates
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
on an open subset
\Omega\subset X
as in Lemma
7.2
, and consider the
finite map

\pi:\mathbb{D}\to\Omega,\qquad\pi(w):=(w_{1}^{k},\dots,w_{p+q}^{k},w_{p+q+1},\dots,w_{n}).

(7.3.2)

Then
\pi^{\star}\omega_{D}
is a metric with Poincaré singularities along
\pi^{-1}(\Delta_{2})
. If so, the curvature is indeed
two-sided bounded as one can see by using the quasi-coordinates, see
[
Kob84
]
.
∎

It is easy to see that the Sobolev inequality does not hold for metrics with singularities of Poincaré-type – which is the case for
\omega_{D}
. However, we have the following substitute.

Lemma 7.4
.

[
Biq97
]

There exist positive constants
C>0,\delta_{n}>0
as well as
a
such that the following inequality holds true

\left(\int_{X}\rho^{2+2\delta_{n}}dV_{\omega_{D}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}}\leqslant C\int_{X}(\rho^{2}+|d\rho|^{2}_{\omega_{D}})d\mu_{1}

(7.4.1)

Here
d\mu_{1}
is the measure given by
\prod\log^{2}|s_{i}|^{2}dV_{\omega_{D}}
, where
\prod s_{i}=0
is the equation of
\Delta_{2}
.

Proof.

It follows from the Euclidean version of this inequality, via the use of quasi-coordinates on the open subset

\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}.

For each
0<\delta<1
we define the map

\phi_{\delta}:\frac{3}{4}{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}^{\star},\quad\xi\to\exp\left(-\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\frac{1+\xi}{1-\xi}\right).

(7.4.2)

Then given
\eta=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{p})
we have an induced application

\Phi_{\eta}:\frac{3}{4}{\mathbb{D}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{D}}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p},\quad\xi\to\big(\phi_{\delta_{1}}(\xi_{i}),\xi_{j}\big),

(7.4.3)

where
i=1,\dots,p
and
j=p+1,\dots,n
. Here the first
p
coordinates are supposed to be the local equations of
\Delta_{2}
.

Now for each
l\geqslant 1
we define the parameter
\displaystyle\delta_{l}:=\frac{k2^{l}-1}{k2^{l}+1}
. The degree of the corresponding quasi-coordinate function
\displaystyle\phi_{l}:=\phi_{\delta_{l}}
is of order
2^{l}
. In what follows, we will use the same letter
l
to denote a multi-index

l=(l_{1},\dots,l_{p})

and the induced map will be
\Phi_{l}
.

In addition, there exists a constant
C>0
such that we have the following inequalities

C^{-1}\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{|l|}}\int F_{l}d\lambda\leqslant\int FdV_{\rm Poinc}\leqslant C\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{|l|}}\int F_{l}d\lambda

(7.4.4)

as well as

C^{-1}\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{|l|}}\|\nabla^{j}(f_{l})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\nabla_{\rm Poinc}^{j}(f)\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{|l|}}\|\nabla^{j}(f_{l})\|_{L^{2}}

(7.4.5)

where
F
is a positive integrable function,
f_{l}:=f\circ\phi_{l}
(by abuse of notation…). Here
f
is defined on

\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}
, endowed with the standard metric (Poincaré on the first
p
components and Euclidean on the remaining ones), and
f_{l}
is defined by

\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{D}
. The constant “
C
” is uniform as long as
j
belongs to a finite family of indexes (which will be our case).

Let now
f
be a function defined on
\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}
, which vanishes near the boundary
\partial\mathbb{D}^{n}
.
If we denote by
\displaystyle\delta_{n}=\frac{n}{n-1}
, then we have

\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}}|f|^{2+2\delta_{n}}dV_{\rm Poinc}\leqslant C\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{|l|}}\int_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{D}^{n}}|f_{l}|^{2+2\delta_{n}}d\lambda

(7.4.6)

which implies the inequality

\left(\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}}|f|^{2+2\delta_{1}}dV_{\rm Poinc}\right)^{1/1+\delta_{n}}\leqslant C\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{|l|}{1+\delta_{1}}}}\left(\int_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{D}^{n}}|f_{l}|^{2+2\delta_{1}}d\lambda\right)^{1/1+\delta_{n}}.

(7.4.7)

For each of the terms of the right hand side of (
7.4.7
) we apply the usual
Sobolev inequality, and we get

\left(\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}}|f|^{2+2\delta_{n}}dV_{\rm Poinc}\right)^{1/2+2\delta_{n}}\leqslant C\sum_{l}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{|l|}{2+2\delta_{n}}}}\int_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{D}^{n}}(|f_{l}|+|\nabla f_{l}|)d\lambda.

(7.4.8)

We observe now that the following

\frac{1}{2^{\frac{l}{2+2\delta_{1}}}}=\frac{1}{2^{l}}(2^{l})^{\frac{1+2\delta_{n}}{2+2\delta_{n}}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{l}}\log\frac{1}{|\phi_{l}(\xi)|}

(7.4.9)

holds for each
l
and any
\xi\in\frac{3}{4}{\mathbb{D}}
, so summing up we get

\left(\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}}|f|^{2+2\delta_{n}}dV_{\rm Poinc}\right)^{1/2+2\delta_{n}}\leqslant C\int_{\mathbb{D}^{\star p}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-p}}(|f(z)|+|\nabla f(z)|)d\mu_{1},

(7.4.10)

where we have used the first part of the inequality (
7.4.5
).
∎

7.1.2
Metric structure on the logarithmic tangent bundle of
X_{0}

In what follows, an
L
-valued
k
-form with log poles along
\Delta_{1}
on
X_{0}
will simply be a global section of the bundle

F_{k}:=\oplus_{p+q=k}\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L\otimes\Lambda^{q}\overline{T}^{\star}_{X}.

(7.4.11)

That is to say, it represents a (formal) sum of
(0,q)
-forms with values in the holomorphic vector bundle
\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L
, such that the total degree
p+q
is equal to
k
.

Of course, in general differential forms with log poles are not
L^{2}
-integrable with respect to
\omega_{D}
introduced above, so we first have to agree about the scalar product on
F_{k}
we want to define. This will involve a modification of
\omega_{D}
by adding so-called "cylindrical singularities" along
\Delta_{1}
.

On the log tangent bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle

we introduce the following Hermitian metric

g_{D}:=\omega_{D}+\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i\in I}\frac{D^{\prime}s_{i}\wedge\overline{D^{\prime}s_{i}}}{|s_{i}|^{2}}

(7.4.12)

where the
D^{\prime}
in the RHS of (
7.4.12
) is induced by an arbitrary, smooth metric on the bundle
\mathcal{O}(Y_{i})
, with
(s_{i}=0)=Y_{i}
and

\Delta_{1}=\sum_{i\in I}Y_{i}
.

Remark 7.5
.

As such, this is only defined in the complement of
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
, with Poincaré singularities and conic singularities along
\Delta_{2}
and
\Delta_{3}
, respectively.

Let
u,v
be two sections of the bundle
F_{k}
. Their scalar product is defined as follows

\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}}:=\int_{X_{0}}\langle u,v\rangle_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}e^{-\varphi_{L}}dV_{\omega_{D}}

(7.5.1)

where the notation is supposed to suggest that we are using the metric induced by
g_{D}
for the "holomorphic vector bundle" part
\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta_{1})

and the metric
\omega_{D}
for the conjugate-holomorphic factor of
u
and
v
.

In order to state the analogue of Lemma
7.2
in this new setting, we first need a frame for
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
, as follows.
Let
U\subset X
be an open coordinate set,
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
are local holomorphic coordinates defined on
U
, such that

U\cap\Delta_{1}=(w_{1}\dots w_{r}=0).

(7.5.2)

We also define

e_{i}:=w_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{i}}

(7.5.3)

for
i=1,\dots r
and

e_{j}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{j}}

(7.5.4)

for
j\geqslant r+1
.

Let
(h_{\beta\overline{\alpha}})
be the coefficients of the Hermitian metric

g_{D}
with respect to this frame. As usual we denote by
(h^{\overline{\alpha}\beta})
the coefficients of the inverse matrix. We need a detailed understanding of these functions. The good news is that this was already done:
g_{D}
is a -singular- Hermitian metric on the vector bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
, and it is the perfect analogue of
\omega_{D}
, in which
T_{X}
was replaced with its logarithmic version. In what follows, we will simply write explicitly the estimates for the coefficients of
g_{D}
.

We can assume that the local coordinates
w
on
U
have been chosen such that the equalities

U\cap\Delta_{2}=(w_{r+1}\dots w_{r+p}=0)\quad U\cap\Delta_{3}=(w_{r+p+1}\dots w_{r+p+q}=0)

(7.5.5)

hold, where we recall that we already assume
\displaystyle U\cap\Delta_{1}=(w_{1}\dots w_{r}=0)
. Notice that these notations are consistent with the ones in the previous subsection, modulo the shift by
r
. With respect to this frame/coordinates, the metric
g_{D}
can be written as follows (cf. (
7.2.1
) for the analogous expression for
\omega_{D}
).

\begin{split}h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=&\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{r+1,r+p}^{i}}{|w_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{r+1,r+p}^{i}A_{i}}{w_{i}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}+\frac{\delta_{r+1,r+p}^{j}A_{\overline{j}}}{\overline{w}_{j}\log^{2}|w_{j}|^{2}}\\
+&\frac{\delta_{ij}\delta_{r+p+1,r+p+q}^{i}e^{\phi_{i}}}{|w_{i}|^{2-2/k}}+\frac{\delta_{r+p+1,r+p+q}^{i}|w_{i}|^{2/k}\widetilde{A}_{i}}{w_{i}}+\frac{\delta_{r+p+1,r+p+q}^{j}|w_{j}|^{2/k}\widetilde{A}_{\overline{j}}}{\overline{w}_{j}}\\
+&\sum_{l=r+1}^{r+p}\frac{C_{l0}+C_{l1}\log|w_{l}|^{2}}{\log^{2}|w_{l}|^{2}}+\sum_{l=r+p+1}^{r+p+q}\widetilde{C}_{l}|w_{l}|^{2/l}+\rho_{i\overline{j}}(w)\\
\end{split}

(7.5.6)

where the matrix
(\rho_{\beta\overline{\alpha}})
is positive definite and has smooth coefficients. We denote by
\delta_{ij}
the Kronecker symbol, and
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}^{i}
equals 1 if
i\in\{\alpha,\dots,\beta\}
and zero if not.
The functions
A_{k},A_{\overline{m}},C_{l}
and
\widetilde{A}_{k},\widetilde{A}_{\overline{m}},\widetilde{C}_{l}
are smooth.

We see that the expression of the coefficients
(h_{i\overline{j}})
in (
7.5.6
) is completely identical to (
7.2.1
) (of course, the frame with respect to which these coefficients are computed is not the same).
The following statements hold, in which the functions
(\psi)
are smooth in the complement of
D
, such that

\sup_{U\setminus D}|\psi|_{\omega_{D}}<1/2,\qquad\sup_{U\setminus D}|d\psi|<\infty.

(7.5.7)

We will not give any detail about the proof, since it closely follows the one in Lemma
7.2
.

(1)

For
i=r+1,\dots,r+p
we have

h_{i\overline{i}}(w)=\frac{1}{|w_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}\big(1+\psi_{i}(z)\big)

and for
i=r+p+1,\dots,r+p+q
the corresponding diagonal coefficient can be written as follows

\frac{1}{|w_{i}|^{2-2/k}}\big(1+\psi_{i}(w)\big).

(2)

In case
i\leqslant r
or
i\geqslant p+q+r+1
, we have

h_{i\overline{i}}(w)=\tau_{i\overline{i}}(w)\big(1+\psi_{i}(w)\big)

where
\tau_{i\overline{i}}
are smooth, strictly positive.

(3)

If
i\neq j
are both in
r+1,\dots,r+p
, then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{\psi_{ij}(w)}{w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|w_{j}|^{2}}.

(4)

If
r\leqslant i\leqslant p+r
and
p+r+1\leqslant j\leqslant p+q+r
, then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{|w_{j}|^{2/k}}{w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}\psi_{ij}(w).

(5)

If
r\leqslant i\leqslant p+r
and
j\geqslant p+q+r+1
or
j\leqslant r
then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{1}{w_{i}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}\psi_{ij}(w)

(6)

If
i\neq j
and
p+r\leqslant i,j\leqslant p+q+r
, then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{|w_{i}|^{2/k}|w_{j}|^{2/k}}{w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}}\psi_{ij}(w)

(7)

If
p\leqslant i\leqslant p+q
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
, then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{|w_{i}|^{2/k}}{w_{i}}\psi_{ij}(w).

(8)

If
i\neq j
are both greater than
p+q+r+1
or both smaller than
r-1
, then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)

i.e. a function which is bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-norm.

(9)

Finally, if
r+1\leqslant i\leqslant p+r
and
j\geqslant p+r+1
or
j\leqslant r-1
then we have

h_{i\overline{j}}(w)=\frac{\psi_{ij}(w)}{w_{i}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}},\qquad h_{j\overline{i}}(w)=\frac{\psi_{ji}(w)}{\overline{w}_{i}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2}}

(\star)

It follows that

\det\big(h_{i\overline{j}}(w)\big)=\big(1+\psi(w)\big)\prod_{i=r+1}^{r+p}\frac{1}{|w_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}}\prod_{j=p+r+1}^{p+q+r}\frac{1}{|w_{i}|^{2-2/k}}.

(a)

Let
(h^{\overline{j}i})_{i,j}
be the inverse of
(h_{i\overline{j}})
. Then for any
i,j\in\{r+1,\dots,r+p\}
such that
i\neq j
we have

h^{\overline{i}i}(w)=(1+\psi_{i}(w))|w_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|w_{i}|^{2},\qquad h^{\overline{j}i}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w))w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}.

The functions
\psi_{i},\psi_{ij}
are smooth on
U\setminus D
, and verify the condition (
7.5.7
).

(b)

For each
i\in\{r+1,\dots,r+p\}
and
j\in\{r+p+1,\dots,r+p+q\}
we have

h^{\overline{j}i}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}.

(c)

For each
i\in\{r+1,\dots,r+p\}
and
j\geqslant p+q+r+1
we have

h^{\overline{j}i}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)w_{i}.

(d)

For each
i,j\in\{r+p+1,\dots,r+p+q\}
such that
i\neq j
we have

h^{\overline{i}i}(w)=(1+\psi_{i}(w))|w_{i}|^{2-2/k},\qquad h^{\overline{j}i}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)w_{i}\overline{w}_{j}.

(e)

For each
i\in\{r+p+1,\dots,p+q+r\}
and
j\geqslant p+q+r+1
we have

h^{\overline{j}i}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)w_{i}.

(f)

Finally, in case both indexes
i\neq j
are smaller than
r
or bigger than
r+p+1
, then we have

h^{i\overline{i}}(w)=1+\psi_{i}(w),\qquad h^{i\overline{j}}(w)=\psi_{ij}(w)

where the functions
\psi
have the same regularity properties as before.

As a consequence, we have the following statement.

Lemma 7.6
.

There exists a positive constant
C>0
so that the inequality

-C\omega_{D}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle}\leqslant\sqrt{-1}\Theta(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})\leqslant C\omega_{D}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle}

(7.6.1)

holds on
X\setminus D
.

Proof.

The argument is identical to the one already given in Lemma
7.3
, so we will not repeat it here. We however add a (hopefully helpful) remark: with respect to the frames (
7.5.3
), (
7.5.4
), the coefficients of the metric
g_{D}
are practically identical to those of the
metric
\omega_{D}
. This explains (
7.6.1
).
∎

7.2
A-priori inequalities for
\Delta_{K}

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a snc divisor in
X
.
We denote by

\nabla:L\to L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)

a flat holomorphic connection, and let
h_{L}
be a harmonic metric on
L
. Then
h_{L}
is smooth on
X\setminus D
and
i\Theta_{h_{L}}(L)=0
on
X\setminus D
. Also, notice that
\displaystyle-2\theta_{0}=D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}-\nabla\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D))
has nothing to do with any metric on
X
.

The motivation for what will follow is to be able to state all our results in a "uniform" manner, i.e. regardless to the fact that our forms have or haven’t log poles along
\Delta_{1}
. First, consider a covering of
X
with a finite set of coordinate open subsets
(\Omega_{i},w_{i})
, see (
7.5.2
), (
7.5.5
). If
(\Omega,w)
is one of them, we define the local ramified maps

\pi:\mathbb{D}\to\Omega,\qquad\pi(\xi)=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{r},\xi_{r+1}^{k},\dots,\xi_{r+p+q}^{k},\xi_{r+p+q+1},\dots,\xi_{n}).

(7.6.2)

Convention 7.7
.

From this point on, and unless explicitly specified otherwise, we will consistently use the following conventions.
Recall that
X_{0}=X\setminus|\Delta_{2}|
is the complement of the support of
\Delta_{2}
.

{\rm C}_{0}

We denote by
\mathcal{E}_{k}
one of the following vector bundles

\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L,

(7.7.1)

\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L

(7.7.2)

{\rm C}_{1}

An
L
-valued
k
-form on
X_{0}
will be a smooth section
C^{\infty}(X_{0},\mathcal{E}_{k})
for
\mathcal{E}_{k}
equals to (
7.7.1
)
or (
7.7.2
)

{\rm C}_{2}

The holomorphic form
\theta_{0}
operates naturally on
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

{\rm C}_{3}

The adjoint of the operator
\theta_{0}
will be denoted by
\theta_{0}^{\star}
:

•

In the case (
7.7.1
), its adjoint
\theta_{0}^{\star}
is computed by using the metric

\omega_{D}
on
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

•

In the case (
7.7.2
), the adjoint
\theta_{0}^{\star}
is computed by using the cylindrical metric
g_{D}
.

{\rm C}_{4}

We denote by
D_{K}=D^{\prime}_{K}+D^{\prime\prime}_{K}
one of the following differential operators:

•

In the case (
7.7.1
), we introduce

D^{\prime}_{K}:=D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}+\theta_{0}+\overline{\theta}_{0},\qquad D^{\prime\prime}_{K}:=\bar{\partial}+\theta_{0}-\overline{\theta}_{0}

where locally
D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}=\partial-\partial\varphi_{L}
is the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection.

•

In the case (
7.7.2
), our operator will be

D^{\prime}_{K}:=D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}+\theta_{0}+\overline{\theta}_{0},\qquad D^{\prime\prime}_{K}:=\bar{\partial}+\theta_{0}-\overline{\theta}_{0}

where
D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}
is the operator on
\mathcal{E}_{k}
induced by the -twisted- exterior derivative
\partial-\partial\varphi_{L}
cf. Remark
7.8
below.
In particular,
it is
not
the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection induced by the metrics
g_{D}
and
h_{L}
.

{\rm C}_{5}

An
L
-valued
k
-form
u
with compact support on
X_{0}
is called smooth with respect to the conic structure
\big(X,(1-1/k)\Delta_{3}\big)
if:

•

In the case (
7.7.1
), we request that

u
is smooth on
X\setminus D
and moreover the quotient

u_{i}:=\frac{1}{w^{k\nu}}\pi_{i}^{\star}u

extends smoothly across the pre-image of
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
. Here
\nu_{\alpha}
are the Lelong numbers of the curvature current of
(L,h_{L})
along
D_{\alpha}
, and
\displaystyle w^{k\nu}:=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{p+q}w_{\alpha}^{k\nu_{\alpha}}
. Moreover,
\pi
is the ramified cover
(
7.6.2
).

•

In the case (
7.7.2
), we request that

u
is smooth on
X\setminus D
and moreover the quotient

u_{i}:=\frac{1}{w^{k\nu}}\pi_{i}^{\star}u

defines a form with log-poles on the inverse image of
\Delta_{1}
.

Remark 7.8
.

As one can see, in
C_{4}
, the differential operators
D^{\prime}_{K}
we are using in the smooth/logarithmic case are basically the same, modulo a small identification we now make precise. The
D^{\prime}_{K}
corresponding to forms with log-poles is defined by using the fact that sections of the dual of the vector bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
identify with
smooth forms with log poles. The exterior
D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}:=\partial-\partial\varphi_{L}
-derivative, composed with the map

\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log\Delta_{1})

is our operator.

Moreover, note that this is equally consistent with the
(1,0)
-derivative of forms with log-poles in the sense of distributions (unlike the
(0,1)
-derivative, they don’t have any residue on the components of
\Delta_{1}
).

Now let
\Delta_{K}
be the commutator
\displaystyle\Delta_{K}:=[D_{K},D_{K}^{\star}].

The key estimate in this article reads as follows.

Theorem 7.9
.

In the setting of this subsection, there exists a positive constant
C>0
such that
for any
L
-valued form
u
with compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
and which moreover is smooth in the orbifold sense, the inequality

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}u,u\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}\geqslant\frac{1}{C}\int_{X}\big(|u|^{2}|\theta_{0}|^{2}+\langle[D_{h_{L}},D^{\star}_{h_{L}}]u,u\rangle\big)dV_{\omega_{D}}

holds.

Mainly, due to the fact that the metric
\omega_{D}
is Kähler and
g_{D}
is not, we have adopted the following strategy for the proof we present next. We first complete the arguments in the smooth case (
7.7.1
). Then we explain how to adapt our arguments in the logarithmic case (
7.7.2
): the differences are insignificant, but they exist.
To begin with, we collect next a few facts about some commutator operators which will be needed in what follows.

7.2.1
Commutators in the smooth case (
7.7.1
)

We expand the expression of
\Delta_{K}
by using the equality
D_{K}=D_{h_{L}}+2\theta_{0}
, i.e.

[D_{K},D_{K}^{\star}]=[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]+4[\theta_{0},\theta_{0}^{\star}]+2[D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]+2[\theta_{0},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]

(7.9.1)

We evaluate next the operators
\theta_{0}^{\star}
and
[D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]
in the case (
7.7.1
).

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
(\Omega,z)
be a coordinate system of
X
, centred at an arbitrary point
x_{0}\in X
. We write

\theta_{0}|_{\Omega}=\sum\theta_{i}dz_{i}

Then following formulas hold true

\theta_{0}^{\star}=\sum_{i,k}\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor

(7.10.1)

as well as

\begin{split}[D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u=&\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}(\partial_{k}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{k}\varphi)dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
+&\sum u_{I\overline{J}}d(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k})\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
\end{split}

(7.10.2)

where
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor
means the contraction with respect to the respective vector field.

Proof.

Indeed, the formula (
7.10.1
) is a consequence of the fact that we have

\langle u,dz_{i}\wedge v\rangle=\langle\omega^{\overline{i}k}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor u,\wedge v\rangle.

In order to establish the second formula, we perform next a calculation in coordinates. Let

u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

be a
k=|I|+|J|
-form with values in
L
. Then we have

D_{h_{L}}u=\sum(du_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial\varphi_{L})\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

and it follows that

\theta_{0}^{\star}\circ D_{h_{L}}u=\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor(du_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial\varphi_{L})\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

and by the Leibniz rule we get

\begin{split}\theta_{0}^{\star}\circ D_{h_{L}}u=&-\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}(du_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial\varphi_{L})\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
+&\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}(\partial_{k}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{k}\varphi_{L})dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
\end{split}

(7.10.3)

where we remark that the second line preserves the type of the form. Also, we use the notation
\partial_{k}
for the holomorphic derivative in the direction

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}
.
Next we have

\begin{split}D_{h_{L}}\circ\theta_{0}^{\star}u=&\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}(du_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial\varphi_{L})\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
+&\sum u_{I\overline{J}}d(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k})\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
\end{split}

(7.10.4)

and by adding the two expressions (
7.10.3
) and (
7.10.4
) we get

\begin{split}[D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u=&\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}(\partial_{k}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{k}\varphi_{L})dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
+&\sum u_{I\overline{J}}d(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k})\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}\\
\end{split}

(7.10.5)

which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

The formula (
7.10.2
) can be used in order to express the "pure" part

[D_{h}^{\prime},\theta^{\star}]
of the commutator by using the covariant derivative.
We recall here that in general setting, given a
(p,q)
form
u
on a Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)

with values in a line bundle
(L,h_{L})
, its (1,0)-covariant derivative
\nabla^{1,0}
is induced by the operators

\nabla^{1,0}e=-\partial\varphi_{L}\otimes e,\qquad\nabla_{k}^{1,0}dz_{i}=-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}dz_{j},\qquad\nabla_{k}^{1,0}dz_{\overline{i}}=0

together with the Leibniz rule. We denote by
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}
the Christoffel symbols of
(X,\omega_{D})
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\nabla^{1,0}
be the
(1,0)
-covariant derivative associated to
(X,\omega_{D})
and
(L,h_{L})
. Then we have

[D_{h_{L}}^{\prime},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u=\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}u

at each point of
X\setminus D
, where
\nabla^{1,0}u
is a (1,0)-form with values in the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
bundle
E:=\Lambda^{p}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes\Lambda^{q}\overline{T}^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
(so that the contraction with
\theta_{0}^{\star}
gives a section of
E
).

Proof.

It is a nice exercise to prove this identity working with an arbitrary coordinate system. However, choosing
geodesic
coordinates at some point
x_{0}
is much more efficient. Indeed, at
x_{0}
formula (
7.10.2
) reads

[D_{h_{L}}^{\prime},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u_{x_{0}}=\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}(\partial_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{i}\varphi_{L})dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

where we use that
\partial\overline{\theta}_{i}=0
since the coefficients of
\theta_{0}
are holomorphic in the complement of
D
.

On the other hand, we have

\nabla^{1,0}u_{x_{0}}=\sum(\partial_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{i}\varphi_{L})dz_{i}\otimes dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

(because the other terms involve the Christoffel symbols, which vanish at
x_{0}
) so the lemma follows immediately.
∎

In conclusion, we have

[D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u=\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}u+[\bar{\partial},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u

(7.11.1)

and the local expression of the second term of the RHS of (
7.11.1
) is equal to

[\bar{\partial},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}\bar{\partial}(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k})\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor dz^{I}\wedge dz^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}.

(7.11.2)

Another simple calculation (which we skip) shows that the commutator
[\theta_{0},\theta_{0}^{\star}]
equals

[\theta_{0},\theta_{0}^{\star}]=|\theta_{0}|^{2}_{\omega_{D}}

(7.11.3)

that is to say, it is given by the norm of
\theta_{0}
with respect to the Poincaré
metric
\omega_{D}
.

7.2.2
Proof of Theorem
7.9
, smooth case

Now we would like to prove Theorem
7.9
in the case (
7.7.1
).
We will argue as follows:

(i)

We first show that there exists a constant
C>0
such that global scalar product
\langle[\bar{\partial},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle
is bounded by

\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|u\|^{2}+C\varepsilon\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}|u|^{2}dV_{\omega_{D}},

for any positive
\varepsilon>0
.

(ii)

For the term
\langle\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}u,u\rangle=\langle\nabla^{1,0}u,\theta_{0}\otimes u\rangle
, by Cauchy, we have

2|\langle\nabla^{1,0}u,\theta_{0}\otimes u\rangle|\leqslant\frac{3}{4}\|\nabla^{1,0}u\|^{2}+\frac{4}{3}\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|^{2}|u|^{2}dV

(iii)

We finally play the joker: modulo terms of order zero
\|u\|^{2}
, we have cf.
[
Siu82
]

\|\nabla^{1,0}u\|^{2}\simeq\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2}.

(iv)

We have

\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2}=\|D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}u\|^{2}+\|D_{h_{L}}^{\prime\star}u\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]u,u\rangle

since the curvature of
(L,h_{L})
is non-singular across
\Delta_{1}
, and
u
has conic singularities on
\Delta_{3}
(see
[
CP23
]
).

Proof.

The points
(ii)
and
(iv)
are clear. Concerning the point
(iii)
, it is a consequence of the Bochner-type result

\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2}+C\|u\|^{2}\geqslant\|\nabla^{1,0}u\|^{2}

(7.11.4)

for any form
u
with compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
and smooth in orbifold sense. We will explain it in more details in the subsection
7.13
.

For the point
(i)
, we will use the equality (
7.11.2
), together with Lemma
7.2
.
Given the equality (
7.11.2
), we have to find an upper bound for the expression

|\bar{\partial}(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}j})|_{\omega_{D}}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\right|_{\omega_{D}}

(7.11.5)

which we next do using a case-by-case analysis.

\bullet

We assume that
i\neq j
.
If
\max(i,j)\leqslant p
, then (
7.11.5
) becomes

\frac{1}{|z_{j}|\log 1/|z_{j}|}|\bar{\partial}(z_{j}\psi_{ij})|_{\omega_{D}},

by the third point of Lemma
7.2
, and the fact that the coefficients
\theta_{i}
can be written as
f_{i}(z)/z_{i}
, with
f_{i}
a local holomorphic function. Since the differential of
\psi_{ij}
with respect to
\omega_{D}
is bounded, we have

\sup_{\Omega\setminus D}|\bar{\partial}(\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}j})|_{\omega_{D}}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\right|_{\omega_{D}}<\infty.

If
i\leqslant p
and
p+q\geqslant j\geqslant p+1
, then (
7.11.5
) is equal to
\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}\psi_{ik}|_{\omega_{D}}|z_{j}|^{1/k}
(the
\overline{z}_{i}
is cancelled by the pole of
\overline{\theta}_{i}
), so it is clearly bounded.

If
i\leqslant p
and
j\geqslant p+q+1
, then up to a bounded function the expression (
7.11.5
) is equal to
\displaystyle|\bar{\partial}\psi_{ij}|_{\omega_{D}}
, so it is clearly bounded.

If
p+q\geqslant i,j\geqslant p+1
, then (
7.11.5
) is equal to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(|z_{j}|^{1/k})
, and again, this is bounded.

If
p+q\geqslant i\geqslant p+1
, and
j\geqslant p+q+1
then (
7.11.5
) is equal to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(1)
.

If both
i
and
k
are strictly greater than
p+q
, things are clear.

\bullet

We assume that
i=j
.
In case their common value is strictly greater than
p+q
, there is nothing to prove.
The remaining cases are
i=j\leqslant p
, and
p+1\leqslant i=j\leqslant p+q
so we have to deal with the following expressions

\frac{1}{|z_{i}|\log 1/|z_{i}|}\bar{\partial}(\overline{\theta}_{i}|z_{i}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}),\qquad\frac{1}{|z_{i}|^{1-1/k}}\bar{\partial}(|z_{i}|^{2-2/k}\psi_{i}).

The residue
a_{i}
of
\theta
along
z_{i}=0
is non-zero for
i=1,\dots,p
, the expression is bounded by
|a_{i}|\log 1/|z_{i}|
(up to a constant). In the second case a quick calculation shows that it is bounded as soon as
k\geqslant 2
.

Expressed in intrinsic terms, we have proved the existence of a constant
C>0
such that

\int_{X}\langle[\bar{\partial},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV\leqslant C\int_{X}|u|^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|a_{i}|\log 1/|s_{i}|dV.

(7.11.6)

The RHS of (
7.11.6
) is clearly bounded by the RHS of (i) above, as consequence of the Cauchy inequality.

∎

Now we can prove Theorem
7.9
in the case (
7.7.1
).

Proof.

By combining the points
(iii)
and
(iv)
, we have

\frac{1}{2}(\|D_{h_{L}}u\|^{2}+\|D_{h_{L}}^{\star}u\|^{2})+C\|u\|^{2}\geqslant\|\nabla^{1,0}u\|^{2}.

(7.11.7)

Together with
(i)
,
(ii)
and Lemma
7.11
, by choosing
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
in
(i)
, we know that

\begin{split}\left|\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{[}D_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV\right|\leqslant&\frac{3}{4}\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}|u|^{2}dV+\frac{3}{16}\int_{X}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV\\
&+C_{\varepsilon}\|u\|^{2}\end{split}

(7.11.8)

for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}
depending on
\varepsilon
. By using the formula (
7.9.1
), we infer that

C\|u\|^{2}+\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}u,u\rangle dV\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\int_{X}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV+\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}|u|^{2}dV,

(7.11.9)

which ends the proof.
∎

Theorem
7.9
admits the following version.

Corollary 7.12
.

In the context of
7.9
, for any
a_{\alpha}\geqslant 0
there exist positive constants
C_{i}
such that the inequality

\displaystyle C_{0}\int_{X}|u|^{2}d\mu_{a}+\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}u,u\rangle d\mu_{a}\geqslant

\displaystyle C_{1}\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}|u|^{2}d\mu_{a}

(7.12.1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle C_{2}\int_{X}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D^{\star}_{h_{L}}]u,u\rangle d\mu_{a}

holds. We denote by
d\mu_{a}
the measure

d\mu_{a}:=\prod_{i\in J}\log^{2a_{i}}(|s_{i}|^{2}+\delta^{2})dV_{\omega_{D}},

where
J\subset\{1,\dots m\}
is an arbitrary subset,
\delta\in[0,1]
. Notice that the constants above are independent of
\delta
.

The corollary follows immediately from Theorem
7.9
, by applying it to the form

u_{J}:=u\prod_{i\in J}\log^{a_{i}}(|s_{i}|^{2}+\delta^{2}).

Indeed, for example terms like
\displaystyle\int_{X}|D_{K}u_{J}|^{2}dV_{\omega_{D}}
are handled by writing the formula

D_{K}u_{J}=\big(\prod_{i\in J}\log^{a_{i}}(|s_{i}|^{2}+\delta^{2})\big)D_{K}u+d\big(\sum a_{i}\log\log(|s_{i}|^{2}+\delta^{2})\big)\wedge u_{J}

and observing that the gradient of the function
\log\log^{2}(|s_{i}|^{2}+\delta^{2})
with respect to
\omega_{D}

is bounded independently of
\delta
on
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
.

7.2.3
Proof of Theorem
7.9
, logarithmic case (
7.7.2
)

In Subsection
7.2.2
,
Theorem
7.9
was established for sections of the bundle

\oplus_{p+q=k}\Lambda^{p,q}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L

with conic singularities on
\Delta_{3}
and compact support in
X_{0}:=X\setminus|\Delta_{2}|
, that is to say, in the complement of the support of

\Delta_{2}
. Here we will do the same in logarithmic context (
7.7.2
).

The next notions were already mentioned in Convention
7.7
, but nevertheless we present a few details about the differential operators on
(F_{k})_{k\geqslant 0},
see (
7.4.11
), which are of interest for us. The first one is the twisted exterior derivative

D_{h}:F_{k}\to F_{k+1},\qquad D_{h}=D^{\prime}_{h}+\bar{\partial}

(7.12.2)

where
\bar{\partial}
is interpreted as operator on the vector bundle
\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta_{1})
(therefore the residues along the components of
\Delta_{1}
are not taken into account).

The form
\theta
equally induces a natural map

\theta:F_{k}\to F_{k+1},

(7.12.3)

and let

D_{K}:F_{k}\to F_{k+1},\qquad D_{K}:=D_{h}+2\theta_{0}

(7.12.4)

be the sum of (
7.12.2
) and (
7.12.3
). Notice that the definition of
D_{K}
is absolutely the same as in the absence of log poles.

Let
D_{h}^{\star}
be the formal adjoint of
D_{h}
with respect to the scalar product
(
7.5.1
), together with the corresponding Laplace operator

\Delta_{K}:=[D_{K},D_{K}^{\star}].

As in the previous subsection, we evaluate the commutator
\displaystyle[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta_{0}^{\star}]
. To begin with, we introduce some notations necessary for the computations in coordinates that will follow. Let
U\subset X
be an open coordinate set,
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
are local holomorphic coordinates defined on
U
, such that

U\cap\Delta_{1}=(w_{1}\dots w_{r}=0).

We also define

e_{i}:=w_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{i}},\qquad e^{i}:=\frac{dw_{i}}{w_{i}}

(7.12.5)

for
i=1,\dots r
and

e_{j}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{j}},\qquad e^{j}:={dw_{j}}

(7.12.6)

for
j\geqslant r+1
. For each ordered subset
I=(i_{1}<\dots<i_{p})
we denote by
e^{I}
the exterior product

\displaystyle e^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge e^{i_{p}}
, and if we write

\theta|_{U}=\sum\theta_{i}e^{i},\qquad g_{D}|_{U}=\sqrt{-1}\sum g_{\alpha\overline{\beta}}e^{\alpha}\wedge\overline{e}^{\beta},

then we have

\theta^{\star}=\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}g^{\overline{i}k}e_{k}\rfloor.

(7.12.7)

Moreover, let
u
be a section of
F_{k}
, which we locally write as

u|_{U}=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}.

We have

D^{\prime}_{h}u=\sum(e_{k}\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{L})e^{k}\wedge e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L},

(7.12.8)

and a short calculation which we skip shows that we have

\displaystyle[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta^{\star}]u=

\displaystyle\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}g^{\overline{i}p}(e_{p}\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}e_{p}\cdot\varphi_{L})e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}(e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p})u_{I\overline{J}}e^{q}\wedge(e_{p}\rfloor e^{I})\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

(7.12.9)

We see that the formula (
7.2.3
) is very similar to (
7.10.2
), except that the metric
g
is not Kähler. Nevertheless, we introduce the operator

\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}:F_{k}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes F_{k}

(7.12.10)

by composing the full
(1,0)
–covariant derivative
\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}:F_{k}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes F_{k}
with the
natural map
\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})
, and
evaluate

\displaystyle\theta^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u
. The coefficients of the connection for
(\Omega_{X}(\log\Delta_{1}),g)
are as follows

\Gamma^{i}_{jk}=\sum g_{k\overline{\alpha}}\partial_{j}g^{\overline{\alpha}i}

(7.12.11)

and we have

\nabla e^{i}=\sum\Gamma^{i}_{jk}dw_{j}\otimes e^{k}.

(7.12.12)

Thus we can write

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u=

\displaystyle\sum(e_{k}\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{L})e^{k}\otimes e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum u_{I\overline{J}}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}dw_{j}\otimes e^{I(i,k)}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum(e_{k}\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{L})e^{k}\otimes e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

(7.12.13)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum u_{I\overline{J}}g_{q\overline{\alpha}}(e_{k}\cdot g^{\overline{\alpha}p})e^{k}\otimes e^{I(p,q)}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

where
I(p,q)
means that the index
p
of
I
was replaced by
q
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u=

\displaystyle\sum\overline{\theta}_{i}g^{\overline{i}k}(e_{k}\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{L})e^{I}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum u_{I\overline{J}}\theta_{i}g^{\overline{i}k}g_{q\overline{\alpha}}(e_{k}\cdot g^{\overline{\alpha}p})e^{I(p,q)}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

(7.12.14)

at each point of
U
.

In our current situation, we have

[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u-\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u=\sum\big(e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p}-g_{q\overline{\alpha}}g^{\overline{i}k}e_{k}\cdot g^{\overline{\alpha}p}\big)\overline{\theta}_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}e^{I(p,q)}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

(7.12.15)

as consequence of (
7.2.3
) and (
7.2.3
).
One can see that if the metric
g_{D}
is Kähler, then the RHS of (
7.12.15
) is identically zero. Unfortunately, this may not be the case here, so we will have to
evaluate the contribution of the linear term (
7.12.15
): this is what we do next. In the first place,
notice the following string of equalities

\displaystyle e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p}-g_{q\overline{\alpha}}g^{\overline{i}k}e_{k}\cdot g^{\overline{\alpha}p}=

\displaystyle e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p}+g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}e_{k}\cdot g_{q\overline{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}(e_{k}\cdot g_{q\overline{\alpha}}-e_{q}\cdot g_{k\overline{\alpha}})+e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p}+g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}e_{q}\cdot g_{k\overline{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}(e_{k}\cdot g_{q\overline{\alpha}}-e_{q}\cdot g_{k\overline{\alpha}})+e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}p}-g^{\overline{\alpha}p}g_{k\overline{\alpha}}e_{q}\cdot g^{\overline{i}k}

(7.12.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}(e_{k}\cdot g_{q\overline{\alpha}}-e_{q}\cdot g_{k\overline{\alpha}})

which will simplify a lot the calculations (given the expression of the metric
g_{D}
) as we next see.

The metric
\omega_{D}
is Kähler, and we can assume that its restriction to
U
is
given by the Hessian of a function
\psi
. We then have

\omega_{D}|_{U}=\sqrt{-1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\psi)e^{\beta}\wedge\overline{e}^{\alpha}

(7.12.17)

and moreover

\sqrt{-1}\frac{D^{\prime}s_{i}\wedge\overline{D^{\prime}s_{i}}}{|s_{i}|^{2}}=\big(e^{i}-\sum_{k}(e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{i})e^{k}\big)\wedge\big(\overline{e}^{i}-\sum_{k}(e_{\overline{k}}\cdot\varphi_{i})\overline{e}^{k}\big)

(7.12.18)

for all
i=1,\dots,r
. Then the coefficients of the metric
g_{D}
can be computed as follows

g_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}=e_{\beta}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\psi)+\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\alpha,r}-\delta_{\beta,r}e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{\beta}-\delta_{\alpha,r}e_{\beta}\cdot\varphi_{\alpha}+\sum_{i}(e_{\beta}\cdot\varphi_{i})(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{i})

(7.12.19)

where
\delta_{\alpha\beta}
is the Kronecker symbol, and
\delta_{\alpha,r}
is equal to one if
\alpha\leqslant r
, and zero if not.

The difference in the last line of (
7.2.3
) is then easy to evaluate

\displaystyle e_{k}\cdot g_{q\overline{\alpha}}-e_{q}\cdot g_{k\overline{\alpha}}=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,r}e_{q}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{k})-\delta_{q,r}e_{k}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{q})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i}(e_{q}\cdot\varphi_{i})e_{k}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{i})-(e_{k}\cdot\varphi_{i})e_{q}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{i})

where we see that the function
\psi
is missing, because

e_{k}\cdot\big(e_{q}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\psi)\big)-e_{q}\cdot\big(e_{k}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\psi)\big)=0

for any
q,k
and
\alpha
.

We will next detail the calculation for the difference
\displaystyle[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta^{\star}]u-\theta^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u
, since this is basically the only difference between the context here and in Section ??. We assume that the frames of
\mathcal{O}(Y_{j})
have been chosen so that the first order derivatives of the weights
\varphi_{j}
at
w=0
vanish. Then we have

[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u-\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u=\sum g^{\overline{i}k}g^{\overline{\alpha}p}\big(\delta_{k,r}e_{q}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{k})-\delta_{q,r}e_{k}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{q})\big)\overline{\theta}_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}e^{I(p,q)}\otimes d\overline{w}^{J}\otimes e_{L}

at
w=0
, and a case-by-case analysis which we next detail will show that

|[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta^{\star}]u-\theta^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u|_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}\leqslant C|\theta||u|

(7.12.20)

where the constant
C>0
is uniform.

Consider first the expression
\sum_{i}g^{\overline{i}k}\overline{\theta}_{i}
, where
k
is arbitrary. The inequality

|g^{\overline{i}k}|\leqslant C|e^{i}|_{g}

holds true, since the inverse of
(g^{\overline{\alpha}\beta})
is positive definite and the diagonal coefficients are bounded from above. We infer that the relation

|\sum_{i}g^{\overline{i}k}\overline{\theta}_{i}|\leqslant C|\theta|_{g_{D}}

(7.12.21)

is satisfied. We next have to evaluate

|g^{\overline{\alpha}p}|\big|\big(\delta_{k,r}e_{q}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{k})-\delta_{q,r}e_{k}\cdot(e_{\overline{\alpha}}\cdot\varphi_{q})\big)\big||e^{q}||e_{p}|;

this quantity is clearly bounded from above, since the product

|g^{\overline{\alpha}p}|^{2}g_{p\overline{p}}

is uniformly bounded for any
\alpha,p
.

Given that, the same arguments as in the smooth case of Theorem
7.9
apply, finishing the proof. Indeed, the points (i) and (ii) in
7.2.2
are completely identical. Then we write

\langle\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}u,u\rangle=\langle\theta_{0}^{\star}\rfloor\nabla^{1,0}u-[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle+\langle[D^{\prime}_{h},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle

and the first term of the RHS of this equality is estimated by using (
7.12.20
).

Finally, the point (iii) works as well in our context, since the curvature of
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
is bounded, and so does (iv): this is due to the definition of
D^{\prime}_{h}
, see Convention
7.7
.

We end this section by a few basic consequences of Bochner-type formulas, which were used in our previous arguments.

7.2.4
Bochner formulas, I

In this subsection we assume that
\Delta_{1}=0
. The following statement is a direct consequence of
[
Siu82
]
, together with the fact that the
curvature of
(L,h_{L})
equals zero in the complement of
\Delta_{2}
, and the fact that the full curvature tensor of
(T_{X},\omega_{D})
is bounded.

Lemma 7.13
.

[
Siu82
]
Let
u
be a form of total degree
k
, with compact support in
X\setminus D
and values in
L
. We assume moreover
that
u\in L^{2}
, as well as
\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\in L^{2}
. Then the following inequalities hold.

(a)

There exists a constant
C>0
only depending on
(X,\omega_{D})
and
(L,h_{L})
such that the inequality

\|\nabla^{1,0}u\|^{2}\leqslant C(\|u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2})

holds.

(b)

There exists a constant
C>0
only depending on
(X,\omega_{D})
and
(L,h_{L})
such that the inequalities

-C\|u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2}\leqslant\|D^{\prime}_{h}u\|^{2}+\|{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\|^{2}\leqslant C\|u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2}

hold.

7.2.5
Bochner formulas, II

Here we discuss the logarithmic version of the previous inequalities. They are completely standard,
but we will provide a complete treatment since unfortunately we were not able to find an appropriate reference.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
u
be an
L
-valued smooth form with logarithmic poles along
\Delta_{1}
, conic singularities on
\Delta_{3}
, and compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
. Then the following inequalities hold.

(a)

There exists a constant
C>0
only depending on
(X,\omega_{D})
,
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
and
(L,h_{L})
such that the inequality

\|\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u\|^{2}\leqslant C(\|u\|^{2}+\|D^{\prime}_{h}u\|^{2}+\|{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\|^{2})

holds, where
\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}
was defined in (
7.12.10
) above.

(b)

Given
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, there exists a rescaling constant
\lambda>0
and a positive real number
C>0
only depending on
(X,\omega_{D})
,
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,\lambda g_{D})
and
(L,h_{L})
such that the inequality

\|D^{\prime}_{h}u\|^{2}+\|{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\|^{2}\leqslant C\|u\|^{2}+\varepsilon_{0}(\|\bar{\partial}u\|^{2}+\|\bar{\partial}^{\star}u\|^{2})

holds.

Remark 7.15
.

In our current setting, the principal symbols of the commutators
[D^{\prime}_{h},{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}]
and
[\bar{\partial},\bar{\partial}^{\star}]
are very different. Indeed, the former equals

g^{\overline{\beta}\alpha}e_{\alpha}\cdot(e_{\overline{\beta}}\cdot f)

and the later is

\omega^{\overline{\beta}\alpha}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial z^{\alpha}\partial z^{\overline{\beta}}}.

This accounts for the difference between Lemma
7.13
and Lemma
7.14
.

Proof.

We will discuss
(a)
first.
Let
x_{0}\in X\setminus D
be a point in the complement of the support of the divisor
D
. We evaluate the intrinsic quantities

[D^{\prime}_{h},{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}]u,\qquad\nabla^{1,0\star}_{\rm log}\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u

(7.15.1)

by a direct calculation. Consider an arbitrary holomorphic frame
\displaystyle(\xi^{i})_{i=1,\dots,n}
of the vector bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
, as well as coordinates
(w^{\alpha})_{\alpha=1,\dots,n}
. Then we can write

u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}\xi^{I}\otimes dw^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.2)

and (by the definition of the operator
D^{\prime}_{h}
) we have

D^{\prime}_{h}u=\sum(\partial_{\alpha}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi_{L})dw^{\alpha}\wedge\xi^{I}\otimes dw^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L},

(7.15.3)

where the differential
dw^{\alpha}
is interpreted as local section of the log cotangent bundle. Given a form
v
with support near
x_{0}
, we have

\int_{X}\langle v,D^{\prime}_{h}u\rangle e^{-\varphi_{L}}dV=\int_{X}\langle{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}v,u\rangle e^{-\varphi_{L}}dV

by the definition of the formal adjoint. With respect to the coordinates fixed above, the LHS becomes

\int_{(X,x_{0})}v_{K\overline{L}}\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}(e^{-\varphi_{L}}\overline{u}_{I\overline{J}})\langle\xi^{K},dw^{\alpha}\wedge\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}\langle dw^{J},dw^{L}\rangle_{\omega}\det\omega d\lambda

and integration by parts shows that this expression is equal to

-\int_{(X,x_{0})}e^{-\varphi_{L}}\overline{u}_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}\big(v_{K\overline{L}}\langle\xi^{K},dw^{\alpha}\wedge\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}\langle dw^{J},dw^{L}\rangle_{\omega}\det\omega\big)d\lambda.

(7.15.4)

Now, in order to obtain the expression of
{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}v
near the point
x_{0}
, we remark that we can write

dw^{\alpha}=\sum_{i}f^{\alpha}_{i}\xi^{i}

(7.15.5)

for some locally defined holomorphic functions
(f^{\alpha}_{i})_{i,\alpha=1,\dots,n}
.
Equation (
7.15.3
) becomes

D^{\prime}_{h}u=\sum(\partial_{\alpha}u_{I\overline{J}}-u_{I\overline{J}}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi_{L})f^{\alpha}_{i}\xi^{i}\wedge\xi^{I}\otimes dw^{\overline{J}}\otimes e_{L},

(7.15.6)

and moreover we have

\langle\xi^{K},dw^{\alpha}\wedge\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}=\sum_{i}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{i}}\langle\xi^{K},\xi^{i}\wedge\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}=\sum_{i}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{i}}g^{\overline{i}p}\langle\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K},\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}

where
g^{\overline{i}p}=\langle\xi^{p},\xi^{i}\rangle_{g}
are the coefficients of the metric
g_{D}
with respect to the basis
\displaystyle(\xi^{i})_{i=1,\dots,n}
, and
\displaystyle(\xi_{p})_{p=1,\dots,n}
is the dual basis.

By expanding the derivative under the integral in (
7.15.4
) we get a first term

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\big(\langle dw^{J},dw^{L}\rangle_{\omega}\det\omega\sum_{i}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{i}}g^{\overline{i}p}\langle\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K},\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}\big)\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}v_{K\overline{L}}

(7.15.7)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum\big(v_{K\overline{L}}\langle dw^{J},dw^{L}\rangle_{\omega}\det\omega g^{\overline{i}p}\langle\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K},\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}\big)\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{i}}}

as well as the following term

-\sum v_{K\overline{L}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{i}}\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}\big(\langle dw^{J},dw^{L}\rangle_{\omega}\det\omega g^{\overline{i}p}\langle\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K},\xi^{I}\rangle_{g}\big).

(7.15.8)

We infer that the adjoint of
D^{\prime}_{h}
can be expressed as follows

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}v\simeq

\displaystyle-\sum\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}v_{K\overline{M}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{i}}g^{\overline{i}p}\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.9)

\displaystyle-\sum v_{K\overline{M}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{i}}}g^{\overline{i}p}\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

modulo terms of order zero depending on the anti-holomorphic derivatives of
g^{\overline{i}p}
and
\omega^{\overline{\mu}\rho}
(which vanish at
x_{0}
).

Next, assume that the frame
\displaystyle(\xi^{i})_{i=1,\dots,n}
is normal at
x_{0}
, and that the coordinates
\displaystyle(w^{\tau})_{\tau=1,\dots,n}
are geodesic at
x_{0}
. Then as consequence of the fact that
g_{D}\gg\omega_{D}
(where we interpret here
g_{D}
as
singular Hermitian metric) il follows that

\sup|f^{\alpha}_{i}|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(X,x_{0})}\leqslant C

(7.15.10)

for some
uniform constant

C
. Indeed, this is immediately seen by using quasi-coordinates, with respect to which
\omega_{D}
becomes
a standard, smooth metric on
(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
.

At
x_{0}
we can write

\displaystyle D^{\prime}_{h}{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\simeq

\displaystyle-\sum\partial^{2}_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{p}}f^{\beta}_{q}\xi^{q}\wedge(\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K})\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.11)

\displaystyle-\sum\partial_{\beta}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{p}}}f^{\beta}_{q}\xi^{q}\wedge(\xi_{p}\rfloor\xi^{K})\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

modulo a term of order zero involving the curvature forms of
(T_{X},\omega_{D})
and
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
at
x_{0}
.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}D^{\prime}_{h}u\simeq

\displaystyle-\sum\partial^{2}_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{p}}f^{\beta}_{q}\xi_{p}\rfloor(\xi^{q}\wedge\xi^{K})\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.12)

\displaystyle-\sum\partial_{\beta}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{p}}}f^{\beta}_{q}\xi_{p}\rfloor(\xi^{q}\wedge\xi^{K})\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

which gives

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}D^{\prime}_{h}u+D^{\prime}_{h}{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\simeq

\displaystyle-\sum\partial^{2}_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{p}}f^{\beta}_{p}\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.13)

\displaystyle-\sum\partial_{\beta}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{p}}}f^{\beta}_{p}\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

as before, modulo linear terms only involving the curvature at
x_{0}
. Notice that the second line in this expression (
7.15.12
) marks an important difference with respect to the usual context (in which we only have a Laplace-like term plus curvature).

One can evaluate
\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}
and its adjoint in a similar manner, which is what we next do. We have

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u=

\displaystyle\sum(\partial_{\alpha}u_{K\overline{M}}-u_{K\overline{M}}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi_{L})f^{\alpha}_{p}\xi^{p}\otimes\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.14)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum u_{K\overline{M}}\Gamma^{i}_{\alpha j}f^{\alpha}_{p}\xi^{p}\otimes\xi^{K(i,j)}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

where
\Gamma^{i}_{\alpha j}
are the coefficients of the connection on the log cotangent bundle and
K(i,j)
is the
ordered multi-index deduced from
K
, in which
i
was replaced with
j
.

Furthermore, if
\displaystyle v:=\sum v_{pI\overline{J}}\xi^{p}\otimes\xi^{I}\otimes dw^{J}\otimes e_{L}
, then we have

\nabla^{1,0\star}_{\rm log}v=-\sum\partial_{\overline{\alpha}}(v_{pI\overline{J}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{p}})\xi^{I}\otimes dw^{J}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.15)

at
x_{0}
and
a quick calculation shows that we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0\star}_{\rm log}\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u\simeq

\displaystyle-\sum\partial^{2}_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{f^{\alpha}_{p}}f^{\beta}_{p}\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

(7.15.16)

\displaystyle-\sum\partial_{\beta}u_{K\overline{M}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}{f^{\alpha}_{p}}}f^{\beta}_{p}\xi^{K}\otimes dw^{\overline{M}}\otimes e_{L}

modulo linear terms only involving the curvature at
x_{0}
. We remark that this is the same expression as in (
7.15.13
), and we infer that the following holds

\big|\langle{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}D^{\prime}_{h}u+D^{\prime}_{h}{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u,u\rangle-\langle\nabla^{1,0\star}_{\rm log}\nabla^{1,0}_{\rm log}u,u\rangle\big|\leqslant C\langle u,u\rangle

(7.15.17)

at
x_{0}
, where the constant
C
is independent of
x_{0}
(no matter what is the distance between this point and the support of
D
). This is due to two facts: first one is (
7.15.10
), and the second one is that if we denote by

\mathscr{R}^{i}_{j\alpha\overline{\beta}}\xi^{j}\otimes\xi_{i}\otimes dw^{\alpha}\wedge dw^{\overline{\beta}}

(7.15.18)

and

\Theta^{i}_{j\alpha\overline{\beta}}\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{i}}\otimes dw^{j}\otimes dw^{\alpha}\wedge dw^{\overline{\beta}}

(7.15.19)

the curvature tensors of
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
and
(T_{X},\omega_{D})
at
x_{0}
, respectively, then we have

\max(|\mathscr{R}^{i}_{j\alpha\overline{\beta}}|,|\Theta^{i}_{j\alpha\overline{\beta}}|)\leqslant C

for some constant independent of
x_{0}
. Recall that we have already proved that the curvature forms are two-sided bounded, see Lemma
7.3
as well as Lemma
7.6
.

The proof of point
(a)
is completed by integrating over
X
.

As for the point
(b)
, it is a simple rescaling matter. Assume that the form
u
has total degree
k
, then we write

u=\sum_{p+q=k}u_{pq}

and thus the global
L^{2}
norm of its derivative equals

\|D^{\prime}_{h}u\|^{2}_{\lambda g_{D},\omega_{D}}=\sum\frac{1}{\lambda^{p+1}}\|D^{\prime}_{h}u_{pq}\|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}.

(7.15.20)

A quick calculation which we skip shows that we have

{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star\lambda}u=\frac{1}{\lambda}{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u

where, as notation suggests,
{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star\lambda}
is the adjoint of
D^{\prime}_{h}
with respect to the rescaled metric. We then have

\|{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star\lambda}u\|^{2}_{\lambda g_{D},\omega_{D}}=\sum\frac{1}{\lambda^{p+1}}\|{D^{\prime}_{h}}^{\star}u\|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}

(7.15.21)

and the conclusion follows by choosing
\lambda
in an appropriate manner.
∎

Now we can prove Theorem
7.9
in the log case (
7.7.2
).

Proof of Theorem
7.9
in the log case (
7.7.2
).

Fix a
\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
. By Lemma
7.14
, we have

\varepsilon_{0}(\|D_{h_{L}}u\|^{2}+\|D_{h_{L}}^{\star}u\|^{2})+C\|u\|^{2}\geqslant\|\nabla^{1,0}_{log}u\|^{2}.

(7.15.22)

Together (
7.12.20
), we know that

|\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{[}D^{\prime}_{h_{L}},\theta_{0}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV|\leqslant C\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|\cdot|u|^{2}dV+|\int_{X}\langle\nabla^{1,0}_{log}u,\theta_{0}\wedge u\rangle dV|

\leqslant C\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|\cdot|u|^{2}dV+2\|\nabla^{1,0}_{log}u\|^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|^{2}|u|^{2}dV

\leqslant(\frac{1}{8}+\varepsilon_{0})\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|^{2}\cdot|u|^{2}dV+2\varepsilon_{0}\int_{X}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV+\frac{C}{\varepsilon_{0}}\|u\|^{2}

Finally, by using the formula (
7.9.1
), we infer that

C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\|u\|^{2}+\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}u,u\rangle dV\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\int_{X}\langle[D_{h_{L}},D_{h_{L}}^{\star}]u,u\rangle dV+(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0})\int_{X}|\theta_{0}|^{2}|u|^{2}dV,

(7.15.23)

which ends the proof.

∎

8
Technicalities II: Hodge theory for
\Delta_{K}

By using the estimate in Theorem
7.9
, we establish in this section the Hodge decomposition for the Laplace operator
\Delta_{K}
. We first address this in the context of
L^{2}
-forms. Next, we define the sheaves of smooth forms
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
in the setting of
(X,\omega_{D},L,h_{L})
, and we show that the Hodge decomposition holds for both
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. An important remark, going back at least to the seminal article of Noguchi
[
Nog95
]
, is that a form with logarithmic poles naturally defines a current. In the last subsection, we extend the Hodge decomposition to the space of currents, relying on a technique due to de Rham and Kodaira. This additional result will be crucial in the proof of our version of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma.

8.1
Hodge decomposition for
L^{2}
-forms

We start this section by some details about the definition of
\Delta_{K}
as an unbounded operator.
Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{k}
denotes one of the following vector bundles

\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L,\qquad\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L.

(8.0.1)

Consider the set

\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K}):=\{u\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k}):D_{K}u,D_{K}^{\star}u\hbox{ and }\Delta_{K}u\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})\}

(8.0.2)

where
L^{2}
-norm is with respect to the metrics
\omega_{D}
and
h_{L}
in the smooth case, and

(g_{D},\omega_{D},h_{L})
in the logarithmic case.

Notice that for any
u\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
, we have the equality

\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}u,v\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}=\int_{X}\langle D_{K}u,D_{K}v\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}+\int_{X}\langle D_{K}^{\star}u,D_{K}^{\star}v\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}},

(8.0.3)

provided that
v\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K})\cap\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K}^{\star})
. This is a consequence of the definition of
\Delta_{K}
, the so-called
Friedrichs extension
of the commutator

[D_{K},D^{\star}_{K}]
acting on smooth forms with compact support. It is self-adjoint, and by elementary Hilbert space theory its domain -defined above in (
8.0.2
)- consists in

u\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
such that

u\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K})\cap\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K}^{\star}),\qquad D_{K}u\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K}^{\star}),\qquad D_{K}^{\star}u\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{K})

(8.0.4)

see e.g.
[
Dem12
]
.

We next consider the following Sobolev-type space.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
H^{i}
be the subspace of
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
such that the derivatives with respect to
D_{K}
and
D^{\star}_{K}
of order
\leqslant i
are
L^{2}
with respect to the metrics
\omega_{D}
and
h_{L}
in the smooth case, and
(g_{D},\omega_{D},h_{L})
in the logarithmic case.

In this context, Theorem
7.9
admits the following more general version.

Theorem 8.2
.

There exists a positive constant
C>0
such that
for any
u\in H^{1}
, the inequality

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{X}(|D_{K}u|^{2}+|D_{K}^{\star}u|^{2})dV_{\omega_{D}}\geqslant\frac{1}{C}\int_{X}\big(|u|^{2}|\theta_{0}|^{2}+|D_{h}u|^{2}+|D_{h}^{\star}u|^{2}\big)dV_{\omega_{D}}

holds. Moreover, as in Corollary
7.12
, the same inequality is true if we replace the volume element of
\omega_{D}
with
d\mu_{a}
.

Remark 8.3
.

In particular, it follows that for
u\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
we automatically have

D_{h}u,D^{\star}_{h}u\in L^{2}.

Proof.

As expected, we will proceed by approximation. In the first place, we can assume that the form
u\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
has compact support in
X_{0}=X\setminus\Delta_{2}
. Indeed, this is due to the fact that the metric
\omega_{D}
is complete near the support of the divisor
\Delta_{2}
.

Next, we cover
X
with a finite set of coordinate open subsets
(\Omega_{i},z_{i})
, as in Lemma
7.2
. If
(\Omega,z)
is one of them, we assume that the support of
u
is contained in
\Omega\setminus\Delta_{2}
and consider the local ramified maps

\pi:\mathbb{D}\to\Omega,\qquad\pi(w)=(w_{1},\dots,w_{r},w_{r+1}^{k}\dots,w_{r+p+q}^{k},w_{r+p+q+1},\dots,w_{n}).

(8.3.1)

Recall the following notation

u_{1}:=\frac{1}{w^{k\nu}}\pi^{\star}u

cf. the point
C_{5}
of Convention
7.7
.

Corresponding to each ramified map
\pi
we introduce the operators

d^{\prime}_{K}:=\partial-\alpha^{\prime},\qquad d^{\prime\prime}_{K}:=\bar{\partial}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}

(8.3.2)

acting on forms defined on
\mathbb{D}
, where
\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}:=-\pi^{\star}(\theta_{0}+\overline{\theta}_{0})
and

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}:=\pi^{\star}(\theta_{0}-\overline{\theta}_{0})
. We
set

d_{K}:=d_{K}^{\prime}+d_{K}^{\prime\prime}

and a simple verification shows that the equalities

d_{K}u_{1}=\frac{1}{w^{k\nu}}\pi^{\star}D_{K}u,\qquad d_{K}^{\star}u_{1}=\frac{1}{w^{k\nu}}\pi^{\star}(D_{K}^{\star}u)

(8.3.3)

hold (see
[
CP23
]
for a more detailed presentation).

Moreover, we have the following density result.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
u
be a form with compact support in the set
\Omega
. If we assume that
u
, together with its derivatives

D_{K}u
and
D_{K}^{\star}u
are in
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
then we have

u_{1}=\lim_{\alpha}u_{\alpha}

where
u_{\alpha}
are smooth and have compact support in
\mathbb{D}\setminus\pi^{-1}(\Delta_{2})
. The limit above is in the graph norm corresponding to the operators
d_{K},d^{\star}_{K}
.

Proof.

We first remark that, by hypothesis combined with the equality
(
8.3.3
) the relations

u_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D},\omega_{1}),\qquad d_{K}u_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D},\omega_{1}),\qquad d_{K}^{\star}u_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D},\omega_{1})

(8.4.1)

hold. Notice that the pull-back

\omega_{1}:=\pi^{\star}\omega_{D}

(8.4.2)

is a metric with Poincaré singularities along the
\pi
-inverse image of
\Delta_{2}
. In particular
\omega_{1}
is complete near the
\pi
-inverse image of
\Delta_{1}\cap\Omega
, and the form
u_{1}
has compact support in
\mathbb{D}
.

By completeness, there exists a family of cutoff functions
(\rho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
as in
[
Dem12
, VIII, Lemma 2.4]
, we have

\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{i}\to u_{1},\qquad d_{K}(\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{1})\to d_{K}u_{1},\qquad d_{K}^{\star}(\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{1})\to d_{K}^{\star}u_{1}

(8.4.3)

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{D},\omega_{1})
. Note that
\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{i}
is of compact support in
\mathbb{D}\setminus\pi^{-1}(\Delta_{2})
.

Finally, on any compact subset in the complement of
\pi^{-1}(\Delta_{2})
the operators
d_{K}
and
d^{\star}_{K}
are
smooth, of first order. The convolution of
\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{i}
with a smoothing kernel together with Friedrichs lemma (see
[
Dem12
]
) allow us to conclude.
∎

The proof of Theorem
8.2
is ended by applying Theorem
7.9
(or better say, its local version) to the regularisation of
\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{i}
and taking the limit.
∎

The next statement is a Gårding-type inequality.

Lemma 8.5
.

There exists a positive constant
C>0
such that
for any
\mathcal{E}_{k}
-valued section
f
with compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
, which is moreover smooth in the conic
(X,\Delta_{3})
-sense, we have

\|f\|_{H^{2}}\leqslant C(\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|g\|_{L^{2}})

where
g:=\Delta_{K}f
is the Laplacian of
f
.

Proof.

In the first place, the hypothesis implies that
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
. This is immediately seen by using the local ramified maps

\pi
in (
8.3.1
).
It therefore follows that the equality

\|D_{K}f\|^{2}+\|D^{\star}_{K}f\|^{2}=\langle g,f\rangle

holds, and therefore we have

\|f\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C(\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|g\|_{L^{2}}).

For the second order derivatives, since
D_{K},D^{\star}_{K}
commute with
\Delta_{K}
, and
D_{K}f
is still smooth in the conic sense and with compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
we have

D_{K}g=\Delta_{K}D_{K}f\qquad\text{and}\qquad D^{\star}_{K}g=\Delta_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f.

It follows that the equalities

\langle D_{K}g,D_{K}f\rangle=\langle\Delta_{K}D_{K}f,D_{K}f\rangle=\langle D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f,D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f\rangle

and

\langle D^{\star}_{K}g,D^{\star}_{K}f\rangle=\langle\Delta_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f,D^{\star}_{K}f\rangle=\langle D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f,D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f\rangle

hold. Since
\langle D_{K}g,D_{K}f\rangle+\langle D^{\star}_{K}g,D^{\star}_{K}f\rangle=\langle g,\Delta_{K}f\rangle=\|g\|^{2}
, we have

\langle D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f,D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f\rangle+\langle D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f,D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f\rangle=\|g\|^{2}.

In conclusion, we infer that the inequality

\|f\|_{H^{2}}\leqslant C(\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|g\|_{L^{2}})

is true, which ends the proof of our statement. ∎

Our next target is the following estimate, a consequence of Theorem
8.2
.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
\omega_{D}
be the metric considered in (
7.1.1
). We suppose that
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}

and
s_{i}
is the canonical section of
D_{i}
.
Let
g,f
be two
\mathcal{E}_{k}
-valued sections on
X
such that
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
and such that
\Delta_{K}f=g
. Then the following hold.

(1)

The inequalities

\int_{X}(|f|^{2}+|D^{\star}_{K}f|^{2}+|D_{K}f|^{2})(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log|s_{i}|^{2})\ dV_{\omega_{D}}<+\infty.

and

\int_{X}(|D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}f|^{2}+|D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f|^{2})dV_{\omega_{D}}<+\infty

hold.

(2)

Consider a non-empty subset
I\subset\{1,\cdots,m\}
as well as positive reals
a_{i}\geqslant 0
. If

\int_{X}|g|^{2}\prod_{i\in I}\log^{2a_{i}}|s_{i}|dV_{\omega_{D}}<+\infty

then we have

\int_{X}(|f|^{2}+|D^{\star}_{K}f|^{2}+|D_{K}f|^{2})(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log|s_{i}|^{2})\cdot\prod_{i\in I}\log^{2a_{i}}|s_{i}|dV_{\omega_{D}}<+\infty

and

\int_{X}(|D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}f|^{2}+|D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2})\prod_{i\in I}\log^{2a_{i}}|s_{i}|dV_{\omega_{D}}<+\infty

(8.6.1)

Proof.

We first remark that the second relation of the first point
(1)
follows from the fact that
f
belongs to the domain of
\Delta_{K}
, together with (
8.0.4
).

Concerning the first part of
(1)
,
it is a direct consequence of the main estimate in Theorem
8.2
and the fact that
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta_{0}
is non-vanishing on every component of
\Delta_{2}
, cf. Property
7.1
.

The argument showing that
D_{K}f
and
D_{K}^{\star}f
verify a similar estimate is the same, given that
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
, see (
8.0.4
). That is to say, we simply replace
f
with
D_{K}f
in Theorem
8.2
.

The second point
(2)
follows along the same lines as the first one, by using the Corollary
7.12
instead of Theorem
7.9
. We have

\int_{X}|f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV_{\omega_{D}}+\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV_{\omega_{D}}+\int_{X}|D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV_{\omega_{D}}\geqslant\frac{1}{C}\int_{X}|f|^{2}|\theta|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV_{\omega_{D}}

(8.6.2)

where
\displaystyle e^{\psi_{\delta}}:=\prod_{i\in I}\log^{2a_{i}}(\delta^{2}+|s_{i}|^{2})
. Notice that all terms are convergent since
\psi_{\delta}
is smooth.

We next claim that the inequality

\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\leqslant C\int_{X}|f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|g|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV

(8.6.3)

holds, for some constant
C>0
.

This is easy to verify: integration by parts -justified by the fact that
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
- shows that we have

\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV=\int_{X}\langle D_{K}f,D_{K}(e^{\psi_{\delta}}f)\rangle dV-\int_{X}\langle D_{K}f,d\psi_{\delta}\wedge f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV

(8.6.4)

The norm of
d\psi_{\delta}
with respect to
\omega_{D}
is pointwise uniformly bounded, so we write

\big|\int_{X}\langle D_{K}f,d\psi_{\delta}\wedge f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\big|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+C\int_{X}|f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV.

(8.6.5)

We have similar inequalities for
D_{K}^{\star}f
; together with the fact that

\int_{X}\langle D_{K}f,D_{K}(e^{\psi_{\delta}}f)\rangle dV+\int_{X}\langle D_{K}^{\star}f,D_{K}^{\star}(e^{\psi_{\delta}}f)\rangle dV=\int_{X}\langle g,f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV,

(8.6.6)

claim (
8.6.3
) is established.

We therefore obtain the inequality

C\big(\int_{X}|f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|g|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\big)\geqslant\int_{X}|f|^{2}|\theta|^{2}_{\omega_{D}}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV,

(8.6.7)

as consequence of
(
8.6.3
) and (
8.6.2
). Near the divisor
D
, it is clear that the RHS of (
8.6.7
) dominates the norm of
f
on the LHS, so we infer the inequality

C\big(\int_{X}|f|^{2}dV+\int_{X}|g|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\big)\geqslant\int_{X}|f|^{2}|\theta|^{2}_{\omega_{D}}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV,

(8.6.8)

for some other constant
C>0
. We take the limit
\delta\to 0
and the first part of (2) follows.

For the remaining part, notice that we have

\int_{X}\langle\Delta_{K}f,e^{\psi_{\delta}}f\rangle dV=\int_{X}\langle g,e^{\psi_{\delta}}f\rangle dV

(8.6.9)

by hypothesis. We infer that the inequality

\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\leqslant C\big(\int_{X}|g|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\big)

(8.6.10)

holds (here no further details are given, since the argument the same as above). In particular, there exists a positive constant
C>0
such that we have

\int_{X}|D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\leqslant C

(8.6.11)

for every
\delta\geqslant 0
.

In order to use Corollary
7.12
with e.g.
u=D_{K}f
, we need to obtain a uniform bound for the integrals

I_{\delta}:=\int_{X}|D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV+\int_{X}|D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV.

(8.6.12)

This is done as follows: first we remark that by hypothesis

\int_{X}|D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f+D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV=\int_{X}|g|^{2}e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\leqslant\int_{X}|g|^{2}d\mu<\infty,

(8.6.13)

and thus we have

I_{\delta}\leqslant C-2\mathrm{Re}\,\int_{X}\langle D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f,D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV.

(8.6.14)

On the other hand, integration by parts gives

\begin{split}\int_{X}\langle D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f,D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV=&\int_{X}\langle D_{K}(e^{\psi_{\delta}}D_{K}^{\star}f),D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f\rangle dV\\
-&\int_{X}\langle d\psi_{\delta}\wedge D_{K}^{\star}f,D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f\rangle e^{\psi_{\delta}}dV\\
\end{split}

(8.6.15)

and since
\displaystyle\sup_{X\setminus D}|d\psi_{\delta}|_{\omega_{D}}<\infty
, we infer that

I_{\delta}\leqslant C

(8.6.16)

by using (
8.6.11
).

Finally, we invoke Corollary
7.12
and infer that

\int_{X}(|D^{\star}_{K}f|^{2}+|D_{K}f|^{2})(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\log|s_{i}|^{2})d\mu_{a}<+\infty.

(8.6.17)

Accidentally, the proof just finished settles (
8.6.1
) as well.
∎

Our next statement deals with the Hodge decomposition for the operator
\Delta_{K}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
be the space of sections, which are
L^{2}
integrable with respect to
(\omega_{D},h_{L})
in the first case of (
8.0.1
) and
(\omega_{D},g_{D},h_{L})
in the second case of (
8.0.1
). Then the following decomposition

L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})=\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}\oplus\mathrm{Im}\,\Delta_{K},

holds, where
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
is the space of forms
u\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that
\Delta_{K}u=0

and
\mathrm{Im}\,\Delta_{K}
is the space of
\Delta_{K}(v)
where
v\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
. The sum is
is orthogonal with respect to the natural
L^{2}
-product.

Proof.

Since
\Delta_{K}
is a densely defined, self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space, the decomposition
