L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})=\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}\oplus\overline{\mathrm{Im}\,\Delta_{K}}

holds, by general results in functional analysis (since in general, for any closed, densely defined operator
T
the orthogonal of
\mathrm{Ker}\,T
is equal to the
\overline{\mathrm{Im}\,T^{\star}}
). The completion is with respect to the
L^{2}
-norm.

Let
g\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
such that
g
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
. In order to conclude, we have to show that
g
belongs to image of
\Delta_{K}
. By the general results invoked above, there exists a sequence of forms
f_{m}\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that

\Delta_{K}f_{m}\to g

in
L^{2}
and moreover,
f_{m}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
.

We claim that
there exists a positive constant
C>0
such that

\|f_{m}\|^{2}_{H_{1}}:=\|f_{m}\|^{2}+\|D_{K}f_{m}\|^{2}+\|D^{\star}_{K}f_{m}\|^{2}\leqslant C

(8.7.1)

for all

m
(up to the choice of a subsequence).

Indeed, in case (
8.7.1
) holds, we infer that
(f_{m})
converges to an element
f_{\infty}\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that
\Delta_{K}f_{\infty}=g
and this would end the proof of our result.

Suppose (by contradiction) that we cannot find a constant as in (
8.7.1
).
By passing to a subsequence and renormalisation, it follows that there exists
f_{m}\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that

\Delta_{K}f_{m}\to 0,\qquad\|f_{m}\|_{H^{1}}=1,\qquad f_{m}\perp\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}

hold.

We can then assume that
f_{m}
converges to some
f_{0}\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
. It follows that
\Delta_{K}f_{0}=0
, and on the other hand

f_{0}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
. This can only happen if
f_{0}\equiv 0
.

In order to derive a contradiction, notice that the equality

\|D_{K}f_{m}\|^{2}+\|(D_{K})^{\star}f_{m}\|^{2}=\langle\Delta_{K}f_{m},f_{m}\rangle

holds, since
f_{m}\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
. Given that
\Delta_{K}f_{m}\to 0
, we infer that

\|D_{K}f_{m}\|^{2}+\|(D_{K})^{\star}f_{m}\|^{2}\to 0

is true. Therefore, since
\|f_{m}\|_{H_{1}}=1
we must have

\lim_{m}\|f_{m}\|^{2}=1.

(8.7.2)

On the other hand, notice that
f_{m}
together with its Laplacian
\Delta_{K}
converges weakly to zero, and in the complement of

D
, the metrics
\omega_{D},h_{L}
and the form
\theta
are smooth. Therefore, we have

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\|f_{m}\|_{L^{2}(V)}=0

(8.7.3)

for any
V\Subset X\setminus D
. The main point for defining the metric
\omega_{D}
as we did in (
7.1.1
) is here: the relation
(
8.7.3
) equally holds for

V\Subset X\setminus\Delta_{2}.

(this is immediately verified by using the family of local uniformisations
\pi_{i}
).

By Theorem
7.9
the inequality

\langle|\theta|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}f_{m},f_{m}\rangle+\|D_{h}f_{m}\|^{2}+\|(D_{h})^{\star}f_{m}\|^{2}\leqslant C\|f_{m}\|_{L^{2}}+C\langle\Delta_{K}f_{m},f_{m}\rangle.

(8.7.4)

holds. Since by Property
7.1
, we have

|\theta|_{\omega_{D}}^{2}\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}\sum\log^{2}|s_{i}|^{2}

(8.7.5)

for some positive constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, it follows that for any small enough open subset
W
containing the support of the divisor
\Delta_{2}
we have

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\|f_{m}\|_{L^{2}(W)}=0.

(8.7.6)

This, together with (
8.7.3
) and (
8.7.2
) gives a contradiction and our result is proved.
∎

We derive the following useful consequence.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\omega_{D}
be the metric considered in (
7.1.1
).
Let
g,f\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
such that
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
and such that
\Delta_{K}f=g
. If
f
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
, then we have

\|f\|_{H^{2}}\leqslant C\|g\|_{L^{2}}

for some uniform constant
C
.

Proof.

We assume by contradiction there is no uniform constant. Then we can find a sequence
f_{m}\in H^{2}
with
\|f_{m}\|_{H^{2}}=1
such that
\Delta_{K}f_{m}=g_{m}
,
\|g_{m}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
and
f_{m}
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
.
By using Lemma
8.5
, we know that
\|f_{m}\|_{L^{2}}\geqslant C
for some uniform constant
C>0
.
Then the argument in Theorem
8.7
shows that
f_{m}\to 0
in
L^{2}
. We obtain thus a contradiction.
∎

8.2
Hodge decomposition in smooth setting

As usual, we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{k}
one of the following vector bundles

\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L,\qquad\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L

(8.8.1)

on
X_{0}
. The log pair
(X,D)
is endowed with the metric
\omega_{D}
defined in (
7.1.1
), and
L
with the metric
h_{L}
defined in
Proposition

6.12
, whose curvature current is equal to
\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
. We recall that
the coefficients
a_{i}
are rational numbers in
]-1,0]
. The logarithmic tangent bundle
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
is endowed with the metric
g_{D}
.

These objects induce a connection

\nabla:\mathcal{E}_{k}\to T^{\star}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{k}\oplus\overline{T}^{\star}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{k};

(8.8.2)

for example, in the absence of
L
and in the first case in (
8.8.1
) the connection
\nabla
is induced by Levi-Civita (= Chern, since the metric
\omega_{D}
is Kähler).

Note that (
8.8.2
) is not the logarithmic holomorphic connection defined in (
6.0.1
). In this section, whenever we use
\nabla
, it always refers to (
8.8.2
).

The following space is plays the role of smooth forms in our current setting. For similar considerations (in a simplified context) we refer to the elegant article
[
Biq97
]
.

Definition 8.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Fix any
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
and let
h
be a harmonic metric satisfying the properties in
7.1
.

We denote by
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
be the sheaf of
L
-valued forms
u
of total degree
k
such that for any integer
r\geqslant 0
, the iterated
covariant derivative
\nabla^{r}(u)
is
L^{2}
with respect to any measure

d\mu_{a}
. More precisely, this means that

\int_{X}|\nabla^{r}(u)|^{2}\,d\mu_{a}<+\infty

for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
and multi-indices
a=(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
.
Here we use the notation

\displaystyle d\mu_{a}:=\prod_{i\in I}\log^{2a_{i}}\!\bigl(|s_{i}|^{2}\bigr)\,dV_{\omega_{D}},

(8.9.1)

where we decompose
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
into prime components, and each
s_{i}
denotes the canonical section defining
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i}))
.
The norm
|s_{i}|
is taken with respect to a fixed smooth metric on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i})
.

Moreover, we use the following notations:

•

If
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
, we set
C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,L):=C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})

•

If
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L
, we set
C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L):=C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.

We sometimes write simply
C_{K}^{\infty}(X,L)
(resp.
C_{K}^{\infty}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
) when the degree is not essential.

Remark 8.10
.

In order to motivate the definition above, we have the next observations.

•

In case
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}=0
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
coincides with the sheaf of smooth forms.

•

In case
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}=0
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
coincides with the sheaf of smooth forms with logarithmic poles.

•

Of course,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
contains the smooth forms with compact support in
X\setminus(\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3})
.

Remark 8.11
.

The sheaf
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
appears to be well-adapted to our context. We will next show that the space
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
enjoys several very useful properties.

•

In Proposition
8.20
, we show that if
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
and
g\in L^{2}
with

\Delta_{K}f=g,

and if moreover
g\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
, then
f\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
In particular, any harmonic form
f\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
belongs to
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.

•

In
8.18
, we show that
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
behaves well with respect to the residue map.

•

Assume
u
is an
L^{2}
-section of
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. In general, the wedge product
\theta_{0}\wedge u
is not
L^{2}
. However, this is the case if
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
.
Namely, in
Proposition

8.19
we prove that if
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
, then
\theta_{0}\wedge u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
.

•

In
9.1
, we show that
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
provides a resolution of the complex

\nabla:\cdots\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(L)\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\to\cdots

(8.11.1)

In order to establish part the properties of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
stated in Remark
8.11
above, we will
use coordinates adapted to
(X,D)
, i.e. fix a finite covering

X=\bigcup U_{\alpha}

of
X
with coordinates sets
(U_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})
, where the coordinates

w_{\alpha}=(w_{\alpha}^{1},\dots,w_{\alpha}^{n})

are such that

D\cap U_{\alpha}=\big(\prod_{1}^{p+q+r}w_{\alpha}^{i}=0\big)

(8.11.2)

where the first
r
coordinates are the local equations of
\Delta_{1}
, the next
p
are the local equations of
\Delta_{2}
and finally
\Delta_{3}
is given locally by

\prod_{r+p+1}^{r+p+q}w_{\alpha}^{i}=0
.

By considering the usual ramified covering

z\to(z_{1},\dots,z_{r},z_{r+1}^{k},\dots,z_{r+p+q}^{k},z_{r+p+q+1},\dots,z_{n}),

for any local calculations we can assume that the following holds.

•

The divisor
\Delta_{3}=0
;

•

The metric
h_{L}
is smooth;

•

The form
\theta_{0}
is holomorphic, with log poles (along the pre-image of
\Delta_{2}
).

For the rest of this subsection we take this as granted.

Fix an index
\alpha
and call
(\Omega,z)
the corresponding coordinate system.
We next introduce new frames for the tangent bundle and its log version which are well-suited for the computations to follow.

\bullet
In the case of
T_{X}|_{\Omega\setminus D}
we define

e_{i}:=z^{i}\log|z^{i}|\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\qquad e_{i}^{\star}:=\frac{dz^{i}}{z^{i}\log|z^{i}|},

(8.11.3)

for each index
i=r+1,\dots,p+r

whereas for
i\geqslant p+r+1
or
i\leqslant r
we introduce

e_{i}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\qquad e_{i}^{\star}:={dz^{i}}.

(8.11.4)

\bullet
In the case of
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle|_{\Omega\setminus D}
we introduce

l_{i}:=z^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\qquad l_{i}^{\star}:=\frac{dz^{i}}{z^{i}},

(8.11.5)

for each index
i=1,\dots,r

and then

l_{i}:=z^{i}\log|z^{i}|\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\qquad l_{i}^{\star}:=\frac{dz^{i}}{z^{i}\log|z^{i}|},

(8.11.6)

for
i=r+1,\dots,r+p

whereas for
i\geqslant r+p+1
we introduce

l_{i}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\qquad l_{i}^{\star}:={dz^{i}}.

(8.11.7)

The main motivation for this definition is that the vectors
(e_{i})_{i=1,...,n}
induce an orthonormal basis with respect to the model metric with Poincaré singularities along
\Delta_{2}
, and therefore they are ’almost orthogonal’ with respect to
\omega_{D}
in the former case, and with respect to the metric
g_{D}
in the latter. Let

a_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}:=\big\langle e_{\beta},e_{\alpha}\big\rangle_{\omega_{D}},\qquad b_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}:=\big\langle l_{\beta},l_{\alpha}\big\rangle_{g_{D}}

(8.11.8)

be the coefficients of the metric
\omega_{D}
and
g_{D}
with respect to the frames above. As consequence of the evaluations in
sections
7.1.1
and
7.1.2
we have the following statement.

Lemma 8.12
.

Corresponding to each indexes
\alpha,\beta
exists smooth a function
F
such that we have

a_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}(z)=F\big(\delta_{\beta,p}z^{\beta}\log|z^{\beta}|,\delta_{\alpha,p}\overline{z}^{\alpha}\log|z^{\alpha}|,\frac{\delta_{i,p}}{\log|z^{i}|},z\big).

A similar statement holds true for the functions
b_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}
and their inverses
(a^{\overline{\alpha}\beta})
and
(b^{\overline{\alpha}\beta})
.
Here
\delta_{i,p}
is equal to one if
i\in\{r+1,\dots,r+p\}
and zero if this is not the case.

Indeed, in the sections mentioned above the evaluations are done with respect to different frames, but it is immediate to "switch" to the

(e_{\alpha})
or
(l_{\alpha})
. Of course the functions generically denoted by
F
in Lemma
8.12
depend on the indexes
\alpha,\beta
.

We use the same notation
\nabla
for the connection on
(T_{X},\omega_{D})
and
(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})
, and
write

\nabla e_{\alpha}^{\star}=\sum_{\beta}A^{\alpha}_{\beta}e_{\beta}^{\star},\qquad\nabla l_{\alpha}^{\star}=\sum_{\beta}B_{\beta}^{\alpha}l_{\beta}^{\star}.

(8.12.1)

where

A^{\alpha}_{\beta}:=\sum a_{\beta\overline{k}}\partial a^{\overline{k}\alpha},\qquad B^{\alpha}_{\beta}:=\sum b_{\beta\overline{k}}\partial b^{\overline{k}\alpha}.

(8.12.2)

The local
1
-forms
A
and
B
can be written as follows

A^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\sum A^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}e^{\star}_{\gamma},\qquad B^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\sum B^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}e^{\star}_{\gamma}

(8.12.3)

i.e. with respect to the frame we fixed above for
T^{\star}_{X}|_{\Omega}
, and as consequence of Lemma
8.12
we see that each of the coefficients in (
8.12.3
) can be written as follows

A^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}=F\big(z^{r+j}\log|z^{r+j}|,\overline{z}^{r+j}\log|z^{r+j}|,\frac{1}{\log|z^{r+j}|},z\big)

(8.12.4)

where it is understood that
j=1,\dots,p
.

Definition 8.13
.

Consider the following class of functions

z\to F\big(\log|z^{r+j}|,z^{r+j}\log^{k_{j}}|z^{r+j}|,\overline{z}^{r+j}\log^{m_{j}}|z^{r+j}|,\frac{1}{\log|z^{r+j}|},z\big)

(8.13.1)

where
k_{j},m_{j}
are positive integers. Moreover,
F
is
smooth and polynomial with respect to the first
p
variables
\log|z^{r+1}|,\dots,\log|z^{r+p}|
. In what follows we will refer to such
F
as a
function with logarithmic growth
.

It has the following simple, but very important property.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
\Psi
be a function with logarithmic growth, and consider its differential

d\Psi=\sum_{i}\Psi_{i}e_{i}^{\star}+\sum_{i}\Psi_{\overline{i}}\overline{e}_{i}^{\star}

expressed in the base
(e_{i}^{\star})_{i=1,\dots,n}
.
Then each of the coefficients
\Psi_{i}
have logarithmic growth.

Proof.

Indeed, for
\alpha=r+1,\dots,r+p
we have

\partial\log|z^{\alpha}|^{2}=\log|z^{\alpha}|e_{\alpha}^{\star},\qquad\partial\big(z^{\alpha}\log|z^{\alpha}|^{2}\big)=(1+\log|z^{\alpha}|^{2})z^{\alpha}\log|z^{\alpha}|e_{\alpha}^{\star}

from which our assertion follows.
∎

A first easy consequence of these considerations is the following "denseness" result.

Lemma 8.15
.

The space of smooth forms with compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
is dense in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
, in the following sense.
If
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
is an arbitrary form, then there exists a family
(u_{\varepsilon})\subset\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(X\setminus\Delta_{2},\mathcal{E}_{i})
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}|\nabla^{r}u-\nabla^{r}u_{\varepsilon}|d\mu_{a}=0

for any
r\geqslant 0
and for any multi-index
a
.

Here
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(X\setminus\Delta_{2},\mathcal{E}_{i})
is the space of the smooth sections (in the conic sense) and of compact support in
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
.

Before explaining the proof of this statement, we remark that it has the following consequence.

Remark 8.16
.

Let
T:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})\to\mathbb{C}
be a current of finite order, so that
T
is linear and we moreover have

|T(u)|\leqslant C\sum_{r=1}^{d}\|\nabla^{r}u\|_{2,a}

for all
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
. If the support of
T
is contained in
\Delta_{2}
, then
T
is identically zero. Indeed, we have
T(u_{\varepsilon})=0
for all
\varepsilon>0
, hence
T(u)=0
as consequence of Lemma
8.15
.

Proof.

We consider the truncation functions
\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}:=\Xi_{\varepsilon}\big(\log\log\frac{1}{|s_{D}|^{2}}\big)
, together with

u_{\varepsilon}:=\rho_{\varepsilon}u.

The norm of any covariant derivative of
\rho_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the Poincaré metric
\omega_{D}
is bounded by a polynomial expression in
\displaystyle\log\frac{1}{|s_{i}|^{2}}
,
where the
s_{i}
are the sections defining the components of
D
. Given this, the claim in our lemma is obvious. ∎

Let
u
be a section of the bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, such that
\mathrm{Supp}(u)\subset\Omega
, so that we can write

u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}e_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\otimes e_{L},\qquad u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}l_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\otimes e_{L}

(8.16.1)

with respect to the frames introduced in (
8.11.3
), (
8.11.4
) or their logarithmic versions. Then we have the following simple criteria for
u
to belong to the space

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
.

Lemma 8.17
.

For each couple of multi-indexes
M=(m_{i}),N=(n_{j})
of finite length and whose coefficients belong to
\{1,\dots,p\}
we introduce the following functions

z^{M}\log|z^{M}|:=\prod z^{m_{i}}\log|z^{m_{i}}|,\qquad\overline{z}^{N}\log|z^{N}|:=\prod\overline{z}^{n_{i}}\log|z^{n_{i}}|.

Let
u
be a form as in (
8.16.1
). Then
u
defines an element of the space
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
if and only if

z^{M}\overline{z}^{N}\log|z^{M}|\log|z^{N}|\partial_{M\overline{N}}\partial_{P\overline{Q}}u_{I\overline{J}}\in L^{2}(\Omega,e^{-\varphi_{L}}d\mu_{a})

for any
a,M,N,I,J
and
P,Q\subset\{p+1,\dots,n\}
. In the expression above we denote by
\partial_{M\overline{N}}u_{I\overline{J}}
the partial derivative of
u_{I\overline{J}}
of total order

|M|+|N|
, where we take holomorphic derivatives with respect to the variables corresponding to the indexes of
M
and anti-holomorphic derivative with respect to the variables corresponding to the indexes of
N
.

Proof.

For sections
u
on the LHS of (
8.16.1
), we write

\nabla^{1,0}u=\sum_{I,J,i}u_{I\overline{J}i}e_{i}^{\star}\otimes\big(e_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\big)+\sum_{I,J,\alpha,k}u_{I\overline{J}}A^{i_{\alpha}}_{ik}e_{i}^{\star}\otimes\big(e_{I(\alpha,k)}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\big),

where if
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})
, then
I(\alpha,k)
is the multi-index obtained by replacing
i_{\alpha}
by
k
.

Assume next that
u_{I\overline{J}}\in L^{2}(\Omega,e^{-\varphi_{L}}d\mu_{a})
. The previous formula -and the similar one for
\nabla^{0,1}
-show that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega,e^{-\varphi_{L}}d\mu_{a})
for all
a
if and only if
u_{I\overline{J}i}
and
u_{I\overline{J}\overline{i}}
are in
L^{2}(\Omega,e^{-\varphi_{L}}d\mu_{a})

for all
i,a,I,J
. On the other hand, we have
u_{I\overline{J}i}=z^{i}\log|z^{i}|\partial_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}
for
i=1,\dots,p
and
u_{I\overline{J}i}=\partial_{i}u_{I\overline{J}}
for
i\geqslant p+1
.

The proof is completed by iterating this argument (i.e. using induction), where we use the fact that the coefficients
A^{i}_{jk}
have logarithmic growth
(cf. Lemma
8.12
) combined with Lemma
8.14
. The same arguments apply in case of sections with log-poles, cf. the RHS of (
8.16.1
).
∎

Yet another confirmation that the sheaf
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
is
the right one
in our context is provided by the next statement, showing that this space behaves well with respect to the residue map
(corresponding to the components
of
\Delta_{1}
and to any of their complete intersections).

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
u\in C_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. Then for any irreducible component
Y
of
\Delta_{1}
, the residue

u_{Y}\in C_{\bullet-1,K}^{\infty}(Y,(\Delta_{1}-Y)\cap Y,L)
of
u
along
Y
.
Equivalently, for
u\in C_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(X,L)
, the restriction (trace)
u|_{Y}
is well defined and
u|_{Y}\in C_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(Y,L)
.

Proof.

Given that we define
\mathcal{C}_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
and
\mathcal{C}_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(X,L)
via
L^{2}
conditions, a-priori it is not clear that this space should behave well by restriction to a lower-dimensional sub-manifold.

Nevertheless, for any
u\in C_{\bullet,K}^{\infty}(X,L)
, we have the following inequalities

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\big|\nabla^{r}u_{Y}\big|^{2}d\mu_{a}\leqslant

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\big|\nabla^{r}u\big|^{2}_{|Y}d\mu_{a}

(8.18.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\big|\nabla^{r}u\big|^{2}\log^{2a}|s_{\Delta_{2}}|^{2}\omega_{D}^{n-1}\wedge\big(\Theta(Y)+\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\log|s_{Y}|^{2}\big)

(8.18.2)

where
u
is assumed to have compact support in the complement of
\Delta_{2}
. The first inequality (
8.18.1
) is clear, and in (
8.18.2
) we are using the Poincaré-Lelong theorem.

Since
\Theta(Y)\leqslant C\omega_{D}
for some constant
C>0
, the first term in (
8.18.2
) is bounded by the following quantity

C\int_{X}\big|\nabla^{r}u\big|^{2}d\mu_{a}

where we stress on the fact that
C
is independent of the form
u
.

The singular term in (
8.18.2
) is handled via integration by parts: it equals

\int_{X}\log|s_{Y}|^{2}\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\big(\big|\nabla^{r}u\big|^{2}\log^{2a}|s_{\Delta_{2}}|^{2}\big)\wedge\omega_{D}^{n-1}

(8.18.3)

since the metric
\omega_{D}
is Kähler.

As
\omega_{D}
has Poincaré singularities along
\Delta_{2}
, we have

\big|d\log^{2a}|s_{\Delta_{2}}|^{2}\big|_{\omega_{D}}\leqslant C(a)\log^{2a}|s_{\Delta_{2}}|^{2}

(8.18.4)

and moreover, we have the point-wise inequality

\big|\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{\omega_{D}}|\nabla^{r}u|^{2}\big|\leqslant C\sum_{k=r}^{r+2}\big|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}.

(8.18.5)

Because the support of
u
is contained in the complement of
\Delta_{2}
,
the singular part of
\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar{\partial}\log|s_{\Delta_{2}}|^{2}
can be ignored.

Therefore, the integral (
8.18.3
) is bounded by

C\sum_{k=r}^{r+2}\int_{X}\log|s_{Y}|^{2}\big|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}d\mu_{a},

(8.18.6)

as we see from (
8.18.4
), (
8.18.5
).

By the snc condition on the support of
D
, for any positive
N\geqslant 0
there exists a constant
C(N)
such that we have

\int_{X}\log^{N}\frac{1}{|s_{Y}|^{2}}dV_{\omega_{D}}\leqslant C(N).

(8.18.7)

Combined with the Hölder inequality, this shows that the integrals in (
8.18.6
) are bounded by something like the
\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\delta_{n}}\right)^{\rm th}
root of

\int_{X}\big(\big|\nabla^{k}u\big|^{2}\log^{2a}|s_{\Delta_{2}}|\big)^{2+2\delta_{n}}dV_{\omega_{D}}

(8.18.8)

where we adjust
N
on (
8.18.7
) so that
\delta_{n}
in
(
8.18.8
) equals the one in the Sobolev inequality of Lemma
7.4
.
By Lemma
7.4
and a few more calculations (which we skip since they are completely similar to (
8.18.4
), (
8.18.5
)), we are done. ∎

After all these preparations the following statement is easy to establish.

Proposition 8.19
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
and let
f
be a function on
X
, which has smooth on
X\setminus\Delta_{2}
and with logarithmic growth along
\Delta_{2}
. Then
f\cdot u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
. Moreover,
\theta\wedge u
and
\theta^{\star}(u)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet})
.

Proof.

By using a partition of unity, we can assume that
\mathrm{Supp}(u)\subset\Omega
, where
\Omega
is one of the coordinate subsets
U_{\alpha}
.
The coefficients of
f\cdot u
are simply
f\cdot u_{I\overline{J}}
, and by hypothesis, derivatives of any order

e_{i_{1}}\cdot\dots e_{i_{m}}\cdot f

of
f
still have logarithmic growth. It then follows that
f\cdot u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}
.

The same considerations apply for
\theta\wedge u
: locally we have

\theta|_{\Omega}=\sum_{i\leqslant p}\log|z^{i}|\tau_{i}e_{i}^{\star}+\sum_{i\geqslant p+1}\tau_{i}e_{i}^{\star}

where the functions
(\tau_{i})
are smooth -actually they are holomorphic, but this is irrelevant here. Since the expressions

z\to\log|z^{i}|\tau_{i}(z),\qquad z\to\tau_{j}(z)

clearly define functions with logarithmic growth for
i=1,\dots p
and
j\geqslant p+1
,
the conclusion follows from Lemma
8.17
.

Finally, we recall that the formula

\theta^{\star}(u)=\sum_{i,k}\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\rfloor u

(8.19.1)

holds, cf. (
7.10.1
). A closer look at the coefficients above shows that

\overline{\theta}_{i}\omega^{\overline{i}k}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}=\psi_{ik}e_{k}

(8.19.2)

where
\psi_{ik}
is a function with logarithmic growth. From this point on, we invoke Lemma
8.17
again to deduce that
\theta^{\star}(u)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i-1})
.

∎

The following regularity result will play a key role in the next sections.

Theorem 8.20
.

Let
g\in L^{2}
and
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that
\Delta_{K}f=g
. If
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
, then

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i}),\quad D_{K}f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i+1}),\quad D_{K}^{\star}f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i-1}).

In particular, if we consider the Hodge decomposition

u=\mathcal{H}(u)+\Delta_{K}(v)

of a form
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
, then its harmonic component
\mathcal{H}(u)
as well as
v
are elements of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
.

Proof.

A first simple remark is that in case we are able to show
that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
, then follows from Proposition
8.19

that this is equally the case for its differentials
D_{K}f
and
D_{K}^{\star}f
. In what follows we will prove that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})

The proof is based on the classical boot-strapping argument.
In the first place, Theorem
8.6
implies that the following

f,D_{K}f,D_{K}^{\star}f\in L^{2}\big(X,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet},d\mu_{a}\big)

holds.

The Bochner formulas of Lemma
7.13
show that we have

\nabla f\in L^{2}\big(X,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet},d\mu_{a}\big).

The last part of Theorem
8.6
equally shows that

D_{K}D_{K}^{\star}f,D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}f\in L^{2}\big(X,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet},d\mu_{a}\big),

from which we infer that

\nabla(D_{K}^{\star}f),\nabla(D_{K}f)\in L^{2}\big(X,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet},d\mu_{a}\big)

(8.20.1)

as well.

Assume now that we are in the local setting, and let
\xi
be one of
(e_{i})
. Then we have the following statement

Claim 8.21
.

The commutation relations

[D_{K},\nabla_{\xi}]u=\mathcal{P}(u,\nabla u),\qquad[D_{K}^{\star},\nabla_{\xi}]u=\mathcal{P}(u,\nabla u)

(8.21.1)

as well as

[\Delta_{K},\nabla_{\xi}]u=\mathcal{P}(u,\nabla u,\nabla^{2}u)

(8.21.2)

hold true, where
\mathcal{P}
is a differential operator whose coefficients are logarithmic growth functions.

We can restart the same procedure by replacing
f
with
\nabla_{\xi}f
, and since we can repeat this indefinitely, we are done,
provided that
Claim
8.21
holds, which is what we next prove.

First we consider differential operators of the following type

\mathcal{P}(f)=\sum_{\Lambda}a_{\Lambda}e_{\Lambda}\cdot f

(8.21.3)

acting on functions
f
, where
\Lambda
is an ordered collection of indexes among
1,\dots,n
together with
\overline{1},\dots,\overline{n}
, and

e_{\Lambda}:=e_{\lambda_{1}}\cdot\big(\dots(e_{\lambda_{d}}\cdot f)\big)

with the convention that
e_{\overline{\alpha}}=\overline{e}_{\alpha}
. Here we interpret the vectors
e_{\alpha},\overline{e}_{\alpha}
as derivations. Since they don’t commute, the order is relevant (but not too important). Finally, the coefficients
(a_{\Lambda})
in the expression (
8.21.3
) above are assumed to have logarithmic growth.

A very simple remark, consequence of Lemma
8.14
is that in case
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
are differential operators as in (
8.21.3
), whose respective orders are
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, then so is the commutator

[\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}]

and moreover its order is at most
d_{1}+d_{2}-1
.

Therefore it is sufficient to show that the operators appearing in the claim are a vector-valued version of (
8.21.3
). The verification is done by writing in coordinates each of the operators involved. If
u=\sum u_{I\overline{J}}e_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}
, then we have

\partial u=\sum_{I,J,\alpha}e_{\alpha}(u_{I\overline{J}})e_{\alpha}^{\star}\wedge e_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}-\sum_{I,J,j\in J}\delta_{j,p}u_{I\overline{J}}e_{j}^{\star}\wedge e_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}

where the second term is due to the fact that
\partial\overline{e}_{k}^{\star}=-e_{k}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{k}^{\star}
for
k=r+1,\dots,r+p
, and we denote
by
\delta_{j,p}=1
if
j\in\{r+1,\dots,r+p\}
and zero otherwise. A similar expression can be obtained for the antiholomorphic derivative

\bar{\partial}u
. Moreover, any form
\tau
of the following type

\tau=\sum a_{i}\frac{dz^{i}}{z^{i}}+\sum b_{i}\frac{d\overline{z}^{i}}{\overline{z}^{i}}

can be written as

\tau=\sum a_{i}\log|z^{i}|e_{i}^{\star}+\sum b_{i}\log|z^{i}|\overline{e}_{i}^{\star}

which accounts for the difference between
D_{K}
and
d
.

The operator
\nabla_{\xi}
is clearly of type (
8.21.3
), as consequence of (
8.12.1
). For the operator

D_{h}^{\star}
, one can argue either directly or by using the commutation relation
D_{h}^{\star}=\sqrt{-1}[\Lambda_{\omega},\bar{\partial}]
. Claim

8.21
is therefore established.
∎

Theorem
8.20
has several important consequences.

Proposition 8.22
.

The kernel
\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
is a finite dimensional subspace contained in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
.

Proof.

By
8.20
,
\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
is a subspace in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{i})
. Now we would like to show that it is of finite dimension.
Let
f\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
. Thanks to
7.9
, we know that

\|D_{h}f\|_{L^{2}}+\|D^{\star}_{h}f\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{X}|\theta|^{2}|f|^{2}\omega_{D}^{n}\leqslant C\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

(8.22.1)

Set
\|f\|^{2}_{H^{1}}:=\|D_{h}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|D^{\star}_{h}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}
.

Then we can find a sequence
f_{i}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}
such that
\|f_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=1
for every
i
. To prove the proposition, it is equivalent to show that by passing to some subsequence,
f_{i}
converges in
L^{2}
.

Note that by passing to some subsequence,
f_{i}
converges weakly to some
f_{0}
in
L^{2}
. Then
f_{i}-f_{0}
converges weakly to
0
in
L^{2}
.
Now
\|f_{i}-f_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 2
for every
i
.
Then on any open set
K\Subset X\setminus\Delta_{2}
, thanks to (
8.22.1
) and the compact embedding theorem with respect to smooth metric, we know that
f_{i}-f_{0}
converges to
0
in the
L^{2}
-norm on
K
. In a small
\varepsilon
-neighborhood
U_{\varepsilon}
of
\Delta_{1}
, we know that
|\theta|^{2}\geqslant\log^{2}\varepsilon
. By applying (
8.22.1
) to
f_{i}-f_{0}
, we obtain

\int_{U_{\varepsilon}}|f_{i}-f_{0}|^{2}\omega_{D}^{n}\leqslant\frac{C}{\log^{2}\varepsilon}\|f_{i}-f_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=\frac{C}{\log^{2}\varepsilon}.

Therefore, we obtain

\limsup_{i\to\infty}\int_{X}|f_{i}-f_{0}|^{2}\omega_{D}^{n}\leqslant\frac{C}{\log^{2}\varepsilon}.

By letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we know that
f_{i}-f_{0}\to 0
in
L^{2}
on
X
. The proposition is thus proved.
∎

Following the notations in
[
Sim92
]
, we define
D^{c}_{K}:=D^{\prime\prime}_{K}-D^{\prime}_{K}
and we have the relation

D^{c\star}_{K}=-\sqrt{-1}[\Lambda_{\omega},D_{K}]\qquad D_{K}^{\star}=\sqrt{-1}[\Lambda,D^{c}_{K}].

(8.22.2)

The reason why we introduce it is that, as
D^{c}_{K}D_{K}=D^{\prime\prime}_{K}D^{\prime}_{K}
, if we would like to solve
D_{K}
-equation in the above setting, so we need a
D^{c}_{K}D_{K}
-lemma which plays the same role as the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma.

We introduce
\Delta_{K}^{c}:=[D^{c}_{K},D^{c\star}_{K}]
, i.e., the Laplace operator corresponding to the derivative
D^{c}_{K}
,
and take
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\bigoplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
, then we have

\Delta_{K}=\Delta_{K}^{c}

(8.22.3)

since
[D^{\prime}_{K},D_{K}^{{}^{\prime\prime}\star}]=0
.
This gives rise to the following proposition.

Proposition 8.23

(
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma for
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
)
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
such that
u
is
D_{K}
-closed and
D^{c}_{K}
-exact. Then

u=D_{K}D^{c}_{K}v

for some
v\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet-2,K}(X,L)
.

Proof.

Note that we have
\Delta_{K}=\Delta^{c}_{K}
for
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
when
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\oplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
.
Then
8.20
together with the proof of standard
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma implies this proposition.
∎

Corollary 8.24
.

Assume that
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. Then
u
is
L^{2}
with respect to

(g_{D},\omega_{D},h_{L})
, and consider the corresponding Hodge decomposition

u=\mathcal{H}(u)+\Delta_{K}(v).

The components
v,\mathcal{H}(u)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.

Definition 8.25
.

Let
u
be an
L^{2}
-section with values in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. There exists a unique form
\mathcal{G}(u)\in\mathrm{Dom}(\Delta_{K})
such that

u=\mathcal{H}(u)+\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(u)

and such that
\mathcal{G}(u)
is orthogonal to the space of
\Delta_{K}
-harmonic forms.

Remark 8.26
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\oplus_{r+s=i}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L
.
As consequence of Proposition
8.19
we see that the image of the restriction of the Green operator
\mathcal{G}
to the space
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
is included in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
, i.e. the map

\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

is well-defined.

8.3
Hodge decomposition for currents and
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma

In this subsection, we focus on
L
-valued smooth forms
u
with logarithmic poles along the divisor
\Delta_{1}
and fixed degree
k
. We have already seen that
u
induces a section of the vector bundle

\bigoplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T_{X}^{\star}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L,

and moreover that it is
L^{2}
with respect to the metric induced on this bundle by
g_{D}
. However, the curvature of the bundle

(T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle,g_{D})

is, in general, nonzero.

However, it would be useful to have an analogue of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma in this context. But since the adjoint
D_{K}^{\star}
of
D_{K}
in the logarithmic case is computed with respect to
g_{D}
, the commutator
[D_{K}^{c},D_{K}^{\star}]
might not vanish (precisely because of the curvature form). Recall that the proof of the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma relies on the vanishing of the curvature.

In order to establish a
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma for forms with log poles along
\Delta_{1}
, we will consider
u
as an element in the dual space of
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star})
. This follows the ideas in
[
Nog95
]
. It is one of the main motivations for discussing next the following notion.

Definition 8.27
.

An
L
-valued current of degree
k
on
X
is a linear form

T:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{2n-k,K}(X,L^{\star})\to\mathbb{C}

which is continuous with respect to the topology induced by the family of semi-norms in Definition
8.9
. Here we denote by

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{2n-k,K}(X,L^{\star})
the space of
L^{\star}
-valued forms of degree
2n-k
, whose covariant derivative of an arbitrary order
is
L^{2}
-integrable with respect to
d\mu_{a}
for any
a
. The space of currents of degree
k
will be denoted by
\mathscr{D}_{K}(X,L)
.

The continuity requirement we impose here means that there exist
N,a
such that for any form
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{2n-k,K}(X,L^{\star})
we have

|T(\phi)|\leqslant C\big(\sum_{r=1}^{N}\|\nabla^{r}\phi\|_{L^{2},a}\big).

Let
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. We show next that
u
defines an element in
\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L)
.

Proposition 8.28
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.
There exists a positive constant
C>0
and a set of natural numbers
a_{i}
such that

\Big|\int_{X}u\wedge\phi\Big|\leqslant C(\|\phi\|_{L^{2},a}+\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2},a})

for any
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star})
. In particular,
u
defines an element in
\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L)
.

Here denote by
\displaystyle\|\cdot\|_{L^{2},a}
the
L^{2}
norm on forms with respect to
(\omega_{D},h_{L}^{\star})
and the volume form
d\mu_{a}
.

Proof.

There are probably many ways in which this result can be established, but here we see it as direct consequence of Sobolev inequality
for
(X,\omega_{D})
, cf. Lemma
7.4

combined with the Hölder inequality. Indeed, the form
u
belongs to
L^{p}
for any
p<2
, from which we easily conclude via (
7.4.1
).
∎

This proposition can be generalised for forms with log-poles defined on an intersection of components of
\Delta_{1}
.

Proposition 8.29
.

We suppose that
\Delta_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
and
\Xi:=\cap_{i\in I}D_{i}
for some
I\subset\{1,\cdots,m\}
. Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{K}(\Xi,(\Delta_{1}\setminus\sum_{i\in I}D_{i})|_{\Xi},L)
. We moreover assume that
\eta\in L^{2-\varepsilon}(\Xi,L,\omega_{D}|_{\Xi})
for any positive
\varepsilon>0
.
Then
\eta
induces a current on
X
.

Proof.

In order to illustrate the argument, assume that
\eta
has log-poles and is defined locally near
\Xi=(w_{1}=w_{2}=0)
, and let
u
be a test form. The linear form induced by
\eta
is the following

T(u):=\int_{\Xi}\eta\wedge u|_{\Xi}.

By the Sobolev inequality applied on
\Xi
, we infer that the following

|T(u)|^{2}\leqslant C\int_{\Xi}(|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2})\log^{2}|s|^{2}{\omega_{D}^{n-2}}

(8.29.1)

holds, where
C>0
is a positive real constant and
\log^{2}|s|^{2}
is an ad-hoc notation for the product
\prod\log^{2}|s_{i}|_{D}|^{2}
.

Since the metric
\omega_{D}
has Poincaré singularities, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2})\log^{2}|s|^{2}\leqslant C(|u\log|s||^{2}+|\nabla(u\log|s|)|^{2})

(8.29.2)

is verified. Therefore, we obtain

|T(u)|^{2}\leqslant C\int_{\Xi}(|u_{s}|^{2}+|\nabla(u_{s})|^{2}){\omega_{D}^{n-2}}

(8.29.3)

where
u_{s}:=u\log|s|
, as consequence of (
8.29.1
). The RHS of this inequality coincides with

\int_{X}(|u_{s}|^{2}+|\nabla(u_{s})|^{2})dd^{c}\frac{1}{|w_{1}|^{2}+|w_{2}|^{2}}\wedge dd^{c}(|w_{1}|^{2}+|w_{2}|^{2})\wedge\omega_{D}^{n-2}

(8.29.4)

and integration by parts shows that (
8.29.4
) is bounded by the integral

\int_{X}\frac{1}{|w_{1}|^{2}+|w_{2}|^{2}}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{4}|\nabla^{k}(u_{s})|^{2}\big)dV_{\omega_{D}}

(8.29.5)

up to a constant.

The function
\frac{1}{|w_{1}|^{2}+|w_{2}|^{2}}
belongs to the space
L^{2-\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon>0
, so Sobolev’s inequality shows that (
8.29.5
) is bounded by

\int_{X}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{6}|\nabla^{k}(u_{s})|^{2}\big)d\mu_{1}.

(8.29.6)

again, up to a constant. Recalling the definition of
u_{s}
, this is smaller than

\sum_{k=0}^{6}\int_{X}|\nabla^{k}(u)|^{2}d\mu_{a},

(8.29.7)

for some
a
, and the proposition is proved.
∎

Remark 8.30
.

If instead of
L^{2-\varepsilon}
we have the hypothesis
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log^{a}(1/|s|)}\eta\in L^{2}
, the proof just finished still works and we derive the same conclusion as in Proposition
8.29
.

8.3.1
Two applications

We derive two important consequences: a De Rham-Kodaira type decomposition for forms with log-poles along
\Delta_{1}
, and more importantly, the

D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
lemma, crucial in the proof of the next result. These two results will be discussed next.

Recall that by Proposition
8.22
,
\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
is of finite dimension. Let
(\xi_{i})_{i\in I}
be a basis of
\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
, which moreover is orthonormal with respect to the Hermitian structure on
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Given a current
T\in\mathscr{D}_{K}(X,L)
we introduce the following objects.

Definition 8.31
.

The harmonic projection of
T
is equal to

\mathcal{H}(T)=\sum c^{i}\xi_{i},

where the coefficients
c^{i}\in\mathbb{C}
are defined by the equality
\displaystyle c^{i}:=T(\xi_{i})
.

Recall that given an element
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star})
, thanks to
8.20
, we have the decomposition

u=\mathcal{H}(u)+\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(u)

and
\mathcal{G}(u)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star})
.
Now given current
T\in\mathscr{D}_{K}(X,L)
, we define
\mathcal{G}(T)
to be the current defined by duality as follows

\mathcal{G}(T)(u):=T\big(\mathcal{G}(u)\big).

(8.31.1)

Notice that this is well-defined, since
\mathcal{G}(u)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L^{\star})
.

We discuss the following very useful statement.

Theorem 8.32

(De Rham-Kodaira decomposition for currents)
.

Given a current
T\in\mathscr{D}_{K}(X,L)
, we have the following Hodge decomposition

T=\mathcal{H}(T)+\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(T)

(8.32.1)

and
\mathcal{G}(T)(\xi)=0
for every
\xi\in\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
.

Proof.

By Definition (
8.31.1
), we know that
\mathcal{G}(T)(\xi)=0
for every
\xi\in\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
.

We consider the current
T_{1}:=T-\mathcal{H}(T)
. By construction,
T_{1}(\xi)=0
for every
\xi\in\mathrm{Ker}\,(\Delta_{K})
. So we have

T_{1}(u)=T_{1}\big(\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(u)\big)\qquad\text{ for every }u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star}).

By
[\mathcal{G},\Delta_{K}]=0
and (
8.31.1
), we have

T_{1}\big(\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(u)\big)=T_{1}\big(\mathcal{G}\Delta_{K}(u)\big)=\mathcal{G}(T_{1})(\Delta_{K}u)=\Delta_{K}(\mathcal{G}(T_{1}))(u).

By combining the above two equations, we obtain

T_{1}(u)=\Delta_{K}(\mathcal{G}(T_{1}))(u)\qquad\text{ for every }u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star}).

Then
T_{1}=\Delta_{K}(\mathcal{G}(T_{1}))
. This implies (
8.32.1
).
∎

Now we would like to prove a
D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
-type lemma for
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. Recall that in
Proposition

8.23
, we proved a
D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
-type lemma for
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
. The key point in the proof is that we have
\Delta_{K}=\Delta^{c}_{K}
when
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\bigoplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\otimes L
. If instead

\mathcal{E}_{k}=\bigoplus_{r+s=k}\Lambda^{r,s}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L,

then we no longer have
\Delta_{K}=\Delta^{c}_{K}
since the metric
g_{D}
is not flat in general.

In order to establish a
D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
-type lemma for the sections in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
, we follow the idea in
[
Nog95
]
, making use of the above De Rham–Kodaira decomposition
8.32
. We then obtain the following result.

Lemma 8.33

(A
D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
-type lemma)
.

Let
u\in\mathscr{D}_{K}(X,L)
such that
D_{K}u=0
as currents on
X
.
We assume moreover that
u=D_{K}^{c}v
on
X
for some current
v
. Then
u
is in the image of
D_{K}D_{K}^{c}
on the total space
X
.

Proof.

By hypothesis
u=D_{K}^{c}v
for some current
v
. Now consider the decomposition

v=\mathcal{H}(v)+D_{K}D^{\star}_{K}\mathcal{G}(v)+D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}\mathcal{G}(v)

of
v
. We deduce the equality

u=D_{K}^{c}\mathcal{H}(v)+D_{K}^{c}D_{K}w+D_{K}^{c}D^{\star}_{K}\tau

where

w:=D^{\star}_{K}\mathcal{G}(v),\qquad\tau:=D_{K}\mathcal{G}(v).

By the equality (
8.22.3
) we deduce that
D_{K}^{c}\mathcal{H}(v)=0
. Moreover,
we have

[D_{K}^{c},D^{\star}_{K}]=0

and therefore the relation

u=D_{K}^{c}D_{K}w-D^{\star}_{K}D_{K}^{c}\tau

(8.33.1)

holds. Finally, the last term in (
8.33.1
) is equal to zero, since

D_{K}u=0
, so we obtain
u=D_{K}^{c}D_{K}w
, which is the conclusion we wanted to reach.
∎

In this respect, we have the following observation.

Remark 8.34
.

Let
\Theta
be an
L
-valued current, such that we have

D_{K}D_{K}^{c}\Theta=\Lambda

in the sense of currents, where
\Lambda
is an
L
-valued differential form,
such that the following holds

\Lambda\hbox{ and }D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}^{c\star}\Lambda\in L^{p}(X,\omega_{D})

for any
p<2
(for example, a smooth form with log-poles).

Given the equation satisfied by
\Theta
, we can assume that

\Theta\in\mathrm{Im}(D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}^{c\star})
. Indeed, we first write the
D_{K}
-Hodge
decomposition for
\Theta
and obtain that

D_{K}D_{K}^{c}\Theta=D_{K}D_{K}^{c}D_{K}^{\star}\Theta_{1}

and then the
D_{K}^{c}
-Hodge
decomposition for
\Theta_{1}
.

If that is the case, we have

\Delta_{K}\circ\Delta^{c}_{K}\Theta=D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}^{c\star}\Lambda

and by our assumption, the RHS of this equality belongs to
L^{p}
. Then so does
\Theta
. Indeed, we have

\Delta^{c}_{K}\Theta=\mathcal{G}(D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}^{c\star}\Lambda)

and then we argue as follows. In order to simplify the writing, we denote by

\xi:=D_{K}^{\star}D_{K}^{c\star}\Lambda
and write

\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{G}(\xi),u\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}=\int_{X}\langle\xi,\mathcal{G}(u)\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}

for any form
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
, by the definition of the Green operator.

Hölder’s inequality -combined with Sobolev’s inequality- implies that the square of the absolute value of the LHS of the previous equality is bounded by

\int_{X}(|\mathcal{G}(u)|^{2}+|\nabla\mathcal{G}(u)|^{2})dV_{\omega_{D}}.

Now Theorem
8.6
implies that we have

\int_{X}(|\mathcal{G}(u)|^{2}+|\nabla\mathcal{G}(u)|^{2})dV_{\omega_{D}}\leqslant C\int_{X}|u|^{2}d\mu_{a},

taking into account the fact that
\Delta_{K}\mathcal{G}(u)=u-\mathcal{H}(u)
, together with

\int_{X}|\mathcal{H}(u)|^{2}d\mu_{a}\leqslant C(a)\int_{X}|\mathcal{H}(u)|^{2}dV_{\omega_{D}}

again by Theorem
8.6
.

Thus, all in all, we have

\big|\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{G}(\xi),u\rangle dV_{\omega_{D}}\big|^{2}\leqslant C\int_{X}|u|^{2}d\mu_{a}

(8.34.1)

Since this is true for all
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
, it implies that
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log^{a}(1/|s|)}\mathcal{G}(\xi)\in L^{2}
.
The same argument shows that

\frac{1}{\log^{a}(1/|s|)}\Theta\in L^{2}.

9
Resolution of Hypercohomology and Harmonic Representatives

We consider a pair
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
. The aim of this section is to construct a natural resolution of the complex

\nabla:\ \cdots\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\to\cdots

(9.0.1)

induced by the flat connection
\nabla
.

Let
h_{L}
be a harmonic (possibly singular) metric on
L
satisfying
7.1
. Recall that we have a decomposition

D=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}

as in
7.1
, such that

\theta:=D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}-\nabla\in H^{0}\big(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D)\big)

has nonzero residue along
\Delta_{2}
and vanishing residue along
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
be the space defined in
8.9
.

One of the main results of this section is the following.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
(X,D,L,\nabla)
be as above.
Then the following two complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{n,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{n+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}

are quasi-isomorphic via the natural inclusion
i
.

Remark 9.2
.

Before giving the proof, we make a few comments about this statement.

(1)

The inclusion
i
is well defined: let

\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}}\wedge\frac{dz_{J}}{z_{J}}\otimes e_{L}

be a logarithmic form, where
z_{I}=0
defines some components of
\Delta_{1}
, and
z_{J}=0
defines some components of
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
, we know that

\frac{dz_{J}}{z_{J}}\otimes e_{L}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L).

Thus, the wedge product

\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}}\wedge\frac{dz_{J}}{z_{J}}\otimes e_{L}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L).

In summary, a form with poles along
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
is in fact “smooth” in our setting. The only logarithmic poles we need to consider are those along
\Delta_{1}
.

(2)

As a consequence of Theorem
9.1
, we obtain an explicit description of the hypercohomology groups, namely

\mathbb{H}^{\bullet}\big(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\big)\simeq\frac{\mathrm{Ker}\,D_{K}}{\mathrm{Im}\,D_{K}},

where

D_{K}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

is considered on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
(ignoring the divisorial part). Together with
8.24
, each element of

\mathbb{H}^{\bullet}\big(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\big)

admits a unique harmonic representative.

Before proving Theorem
9.1
, we need some preparations. Throughout this section, we denote

\mathcal{E}_{p,q}:=\Lambda^{p,q}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\bigoplus_{p+q=k}\Lambda^{p,q}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L.

The log pair
(X,D)
is endowed with the metric
\omega_{D}
defined in (
7.1.1
).
Let
g_{D}
be the metric on
T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
defined in (
7.4.12
).
Then
\mathcal{E}_{p,q}
is equipped with a smooth Hermitian metric:
g_{D}
on
\Lambda^{p}T^{\star}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
and
\omega_{D}
on
(\Lambda^{q}T_{X}^{0,1})^{\star}
.
Let
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{p,q})
denote the space of
L^{2}
-sections with respect to the above data, and let
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
be the space defined in
8.9
.

For technical reasons, we also consider the following subsheaf, which, loosely speaking, interpolates between
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
and
C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.

Definition 9.3
.

Let
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be the sum of the components on which the residue of
\theta
is nonzero.
Assume that each hypersurface
D_{i}
is locally defined by
z_{i}=0
.

Let
U\subset X
be an open set.
We denote by
\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
the subsheaf of
L^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
whose sections can be written locally as

f=\sum_{|I|+|J|=s}f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}\otimes e_{L},

where the coefficients
f_{I,J}
satisfy the condition

\int_{V}\big|f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}\big|^{2}_{h_{L},g_{D},\omega_{D}}\prod_{i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\setminus I}\log^{2}|z_{i}|\,dV_{D}<+\infty

for every relatively compact open set
V\Subset U
, where
dV_{D}:=\omega_{D}^{n}
.

There are several elementary properties of the space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(X,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.

Proposition 9.4
.

If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
, then
\theta\wedge f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the definition of the sheaf
\mathcal{L}^{2}
.
∎

The next result is a characterization of the sheaf of holomorphic forms in terms of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
U\subset X
be an open set and let
f\in H^{0}(U,L\otimes\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log D))
.
Then
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

Conversely, let
f
be an
L
-valued
(p,0)
-form on
X\setminus D
such that
\bar{\partial}f=0
on
U\setminus D
.
If
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
, then
f\in H^{0}(U,L\otimes\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log D))
.

Proof.

Write
D=\sum D_{i}
, where each
D_{i}
is locally defined by
z_{i}=0
. Consider the logarithmic form

\frac{dz_{J}}{z_{J}}\wedge\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}},

where
J
corresponds to the components in
\Delta_{1}
and
I
corresponds to the components in
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
.

Since the Lelong number of
h_{L}
is strictly negative along
\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
, and
g_{D}
is a smooth metric on
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
, we have

\Bigg|\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}}\wedge\frac{dz_{J}}{z_{J}}\Bigg|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D},h_{L}}\,\omega_{D}^{n}\leqslant\frac{dV_{\omega}}{\prod|z_{i}|^{2-\varepsilon}},

where
dV_{\omega}
is a smooth volume form and
\varepsilon>0
is a small positive constant.
Thus
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

Conversely, let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
such that
\bar{\partial}f=0
on
U\setminus D
.
Then
f
extends to a meromorphic form on
U
with poles along
D
.
Now, since the Lelong number of
h_{L}
is greater than
-1
,
f
can only have logarithmic poles of type
\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log D)
.
In fact, for a meromorphic form of type
\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{i}}
with
i\notin I
, a direct calculation shows that
\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{i}}
is not
L^{2}
near
D_{i}
.
∎

The following
L^{2}
-estimate is crucial in the proof of Theorem
9.1
.
We first fix some notation.
Assume that
\Delta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
, where each
D_{i}
is locally defined by
z_{i}=0
.
Let
J\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
be a subset, and denote by
d\mu_{J}
the measure

d\mu_{J}:=e^{-\varphi_{L}}\Bigg(\prod_{i\in J}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}\Bigg)dV_{\omega_{D}},

(9.5.1)

where
\varphi_{L}
is the local weight of
h_{L}
, and
\omega_{D}
is the model Poincaré/conic metric.
Using a result of A. Fujiki
[
Fuj92
]
, we obtain the following statement.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
v\in L^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{p,q},g_{D},\omega_{D},d\mu_{J})
with
\bar{\partial}v=0
, where
J\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
.
Then there exists a
(p,q-1)
-form
u
such that

\bar{\partial}u=v,\qquad\int_{U}\Big(|u|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}+|D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}u|^{2}_{g_{D},\omega_{D}}\Big)d\mu_{J}<\infty.

(9.6.1)

Here
D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}
is the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection on a
L
-valued form with respect to
h_{L}
.

Proof.

First assume that
\omega_{D}
has only Poincaré singularities (so that
\Delta_{3}=0
).
As a consequence of
[
Fuj92
, Prop. 2.1]
, there exists a solution
u_{0}
of the equation
\bar{\partial}u_{0}=v
such that

u_{0}\in L^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{p-1,q},g_{D},\omega_{D},d\mu_{J})
.

Let
u\in(\mathrm{Ker}\,\bar{\partial})^{\perp}
be the projection of
u_{0}
onto the orthogonal complement of
\mathrm{Ker}\,\bar{\partial}
,
where the scalar product is defined by the measure
d\mu_{J}
together with
(g_{D},\omega_{D})
on
\mathcal{E}_{p-1,q}
.
We now show that this particular solution of the
\bar{\partial}
-equation satisfies (
9.6.1
).

Note first that
\bar{\partial}^{\star}u=0
, where the adjoint is taken with respect to the weight
\varphi_{L}+\Xi
on
L
, with

\Xi:=-\log\!\Bigg(\prod_{i\in J}\log^{2}|z_{i}|^{2}\Bigg).

Let
\Psi_{\varepsilon}
be the standard cutoff function on
U\setminus\Delta_{2}
, which is equal to zero near the

\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\Delta_{2}
.
Since the norm of
\nabla\Psi_{\varepsilon}
with respect to
\omega_{D}
is uniformly bounded, we have

\int_{U}|\bar{\partial}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)|^{2}d\mu_{J}+\int_{U}|\bar{\partial}^{\star}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)|^{2}d\mu_{J}\leqslant C\Bigg(\int_{U}|\bar{\partial}u|^{2}d\mu_{J}+\int_{U}|u|^{2}d\mu_{J}\Bigg),

(9.6.2)

for some constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
.

We next control the
L^{2}
-norm of
D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}u
.
Compared with the situation in
7.14
, there is an additional curvature term

[dd^{c}\Xi,\Lambda_{\omega_{D}}],

which is pointwise bounded, as shown by the following calculation of the Hessian of
\Xi
.
Indeed, we have

\bar{\partial}\Xi=-2\sum_{k\in J}\frac{d\overline{z}_{k}}{\overline{z}_{k}\log|z_{k}|^{2}},

(9.6.3)

which implies

\sup_{U\setminus|D|}|\partial\Xi|_{\omega_{D}}<\infty.

(9.6.4)

Moreover,
dd^{c}\Xi
contains terms of the form

\sum_{k\in J}\frac{\sqrt{-1}\,dz_{k}\wedge d\overline{z}_{k}}{|z_{k}|^{2}\log^{2}|z_{k}|^{2}},

(9.6.5)

each of which is uniformly bounded in the
L^{\infty}
norm relative to
\omega_{D}
.
In particular, the eigenvalues of
dd^{c}\Xi
with respect to
\omega_{D}
are uniformly bounded, and hence the curvature operator above is bounded.

Applying point (b) of
7.14
, together with the above uniform estimate, we obtain

\int_{U}\big|D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)-\partial\Xi\wedge(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)\big|^{2}d\mu_{J}\leqslant C\Bigg(\int_{U}|\bar{\partial}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)|^{2}d\mu_{J}+\int_{U}|\bar{\partial}^{\star}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)|^{2}d\mu_{J}\Bigg).

(9.6.6)

Combining with (
9.6.2
), we obtain

\int_{U}\big|D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)-\partial\Xi\wedge(\Psi_{\varepsilon}u)\big|^{2}d\mu_{J}\leqslant C,

for some constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
.
Then, together with (
9.6.4
), letting
\varepsilon\to 0
shows that

D^{\prime}_{h_{L}}u\in L^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{p,q},g_{D},\omega_{D},d\mu_{J})
.
This proves (
9.6.1
).

The general case reduces to this one: via the local ramified cover
\pi
defined in (
8.3.1
),
the metric
\pi^{\star}\omega_{D}
has only Poincaré singularities.
We construct a solution
\widetilde{u}_{0}
of the equation
\bar{\partial}\widetilde{u}_{0}=\pi^{\star}v
,
and by the usual averaging procedure we may assume that

\widetilde{u}_{0}=\pi^{\star}u_{0}

(9.6.7)

for a form
u_{0}
defined on
U
, such that
\bar{\partial}u_{0}=v
, together with the required integrability properties.
∎

We next derive the following consequence.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
U
be a Stein open set and let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
If
\bar{\partial}f=0
on
U\setminus D
, then there exists a
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k-1})
such that

f=\bar{\partial}g
on
U\setminus D
and
D_{K}g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.

Proof.

Write
f=\sum f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}
. By definition,

\int_{U}\big|f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}\big|^{2}_{h_{L},g_{D},\omega_{D}}\prod_{i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\setminus I}\log^{2}|z_{i}|\,dV_{D}<+\infty.

For each fixed index
I
, since
\bar{\partial}f=0
on
U\setminus D
, we know that

\sum_{J}f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}

is
\bar{\partial}
-closed on
U\setminus D
.
Applying Lemma
9.6
to this form, with respect to the weight

\prod_{i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\setminus I}\log^{2}|z_{i}|
, we obtain

g_{I}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k-1})
such that

\sum_{J}f_{I,J}\,dz_{I}\wedge d\overline{z}_{J}=\bar{\partial}g_{I}\qquad\text{on }U,

and moreover
D^{\prime}_{h}g_{I}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
Set
g:=\sum_{I}g_{I}
. Then
f=\bar{\partial}g
and
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k-1})
.

Finally, we check that
D_{K}g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
Since
\bar{\partial}g=f
, we already have
\bar{\partial}g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
By Proposition
9.4
,
\theta\wedge g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
Together with
D^{\prime}_{h}g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
, this shows that indeed

D_{K}g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{k})
.
∎

The next regularity lemma is a consequence of Theorem
8.20
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
U\subset X
be some open subset and let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
. Assume that there exists
g_{0}\in L^{2}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\bullet-1})
such that the equality
f=D_{K}g_{0}
holds on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
. Then there exists a
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that

f=D_{K}g\qquad\text{on }X\setminus\Delta_{1}.

Proof.

Let
g
be the projection of
g_{0}
on
\mathrm{Ker}\,(D_{K}|_{U})^{\perp}
, the orthogonal of the kernel of the operator
D_{K}
restricted to
U
. Then we automatically have

D_{K}^{\star}g=0

and as consequence, a quick calculation shows that for any cutoff function
\psi

with support in
U
we have

\psi g\in\mathrm{Dom}\Delta_{K},\qquad\Delta_{K}(\psi g)-\Delta(\psi)g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)(U).

The conclusion follows by the usual boot-strapping procedure, using Theorem
8.20
.
∎

By combining
Proposition

9.7
and
9.8
, we obtain the following consequence.

Corollary 9.9
.

Let
U\subset X
be a small Stein open set and let
f\in C^{\infty}_{s,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
D_{K}f=0
and
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
.

(9.9.1)

If
s>n
, there exists
g\in C^{\infty}_{s-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
and
D_{K}g=f
on
U
.

(9.9.2)

If
s\leqslant n
, there exists
g\in C^{\infty}_{s-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
and

f-D_{K}g\in H^{0}(U,\Omega^{s}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L),\qquad\nabla(f-D_{K}g)=0.

Proof.

Write
f=\sum_{p+q=s}f^{p,q}
, where
f^{p,q}
is an
L
-valued
(p,q)
-form.
Then

f^{0,s}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U),\qquad\bar{\partial}f^{0,s}=0\text{ on }U\setminus D.

Applying Proposition
9.7
, there exists a
(0,s-1)
-form
g^{0,s-1}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
such that

\bar{\partial}g^{0,s-1}=f^{0,s}\quad\text{on }U\setminus D,\qquad D_{K}g^{0,s-1}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U).

Then
f-D_{K}g^{0,s-1}
is
D_{K}
-closed and belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
.

We repeat the above argument for
f-D_{K}g^{0,s-1}
. By construction, its
(0,s)
-part is zero.
Then
D_{K}
-closeness implies that the
(1,s-1)
-part is
\bar{\partial}
-closed.
Applying Proposition
9.7
again, we find a
(1,s-2)
-form
g^{1,s-2}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
such that

f-D_{K}g^{0,s-1}-D_{K}g^{1,s-2}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)

has no
(0,s)
or
(1,s-1)
component.
Iterating this process
\min\{s,n\}
times, we reach one of the following two situations:

(9.9.1)

If
s>n
, we obtain
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
such that
f=D_{K}g
on
U
.
By
9.8
, we can choose
g\in C^{\infty}_{s-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
, proving the corollary.

(9.9.2)

If
s\leqslant n
, we obtain
g\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U)
such that
f-D_{K}g
is a
D_{K}
-closed
(s,0)
-form.
In particular,
f-D_{K}g\in H^{0}(U,\Omega^{s}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L)
and
\nabla(f-D_{K}g)=0
.
Then
D_{K}g\in C^{\infty}_{s,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
.
By applying
9.8
to
D_{K}g
, there exists
g_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{s-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
D_{K}g_{0}=D_{K}g
.
Then
f-D_{K}g_{0}\in H^{0}(U,\Omega^{s}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L)
and
\nabla(f-D_{K}g_{0})=0
, which proves the corollary.

∎

Remark 9.10
.

The previous two statements, i.e. Proposition
9.7
and Lemma
9.8
together with their consequence we have just established show clearly the relevance of the space of forms
\mathcal{L}^{2}
. Indeed, it would be more difficult (but not impossible) to establish for example Proposition
9.7
directly in the context of
the spaces
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}
.

Now we are ready to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 9.11
.

Consider the natural inclusion morphism
i
between the following complexes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{n,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{n+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{K}}

Then
i
induces an isomorphism of sheaves:

[i]:\frac{\mathrm{Ker}\,\nabla}{\mathrm{Im}\,\nabla}\longrightarrow\frac{\mathrm{Ker}\,D_{K}}{\mathrm{Im}\,D_{K}}.

(9.11.1)

In other words, the two complexes above are quasi-isomorphic.

Proof.

As a consequence of Proposition
9.5
, a holomorphic log form
f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.
Therefore, the inclusion
i
is well-defined.

We first prove the surjectivity of (
9.11.1
). Let
U\subset X
be a small Stein open set and let
f\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
D_{K}f=0
.
We follow the argument used in the proof of the usual Dolbeault lemma (see, e.g.,
[
Huy05
, Cor. 1.3.9]
).
Let
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
be an increasing sequence of Stein open subsets of
U
with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=U
.

Consider first the case
k\geqslant n+1
. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, since
f\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(U_{m})
, by
9.9
we can find
g_{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(U_{m},\Delta_{1},L)
such that

f=D_{K}g_{m}\qquad\text{on }U_{m}.

We claim that we can choose a sequence
\widetilde{\beta}_{m}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(U_{m},\Delta_{1},L)
such that

f=D_{K}\widetilde{\beta}_{m}\qquad\text{on }U_{m},\qquad\widetilde{\beta}_{m}=\widetilde{\beta}_{m+1}\quad\text{on }U_{m-1}.

To prove the claim, assume by induction that
\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}
have already been constructed.
Consider
\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m}
. By construction,

D_{K}(\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m})=0\quad\text{on }U_{m-1}.

Since
k\geqslant n+1
, applying
9.9
to
\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m}
on
U_{m-1}
, we can find
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that

\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m}=D_{K}\gamma\quad\text{on }U_{m-2}.

Set

\widetilde{\beta}_{m}:=\beta_{m}+D_{K}\gamma.

Then
\widetilde{\beta}_{m}=\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}
on
U_{m-2}
and
D_{K}\widetilde{\beta}_{m}=f
on
U_{m}
. The claim is proved.

Thanks to the claim, the sequence
\widetilde{\beta}_{m}
converges to some
\beta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that

D_{K}\beta=f\quad\text{on }U.

This proves surjectivity for
k>n
.

If
k\leqslant n
, the only difference is that the
D_{K}
-closeness of
\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m}
on
U_{m-1}
implies

\widetilde{\beta}_{m-1}-\beta_{m}=D_{K}\gamma+F_{m}\quad\text{on }U_{m-2},

for some
F_{m}\in H^{0}(U_{m-2},\Omega^{s-1}_{X}(\log\sum D_{i})\otimes L)
with
\nabla F_{m}=0
.
Since
\mathrm{Ker}\,\nabla
is coherent, there exists
F\in H^{0}(U,\Omega^{s-1}_{X}(\log\sum D_{i})\otimes L)
with
\nabla F=0
such that
\|F-F_{m}\|\leqslant 2^{-m}
on
U_{m-2}
.
Then set

\widetilde{\beta}_{m}:=\beta_{m}+D_{K}\gamma+(F_{m}-F).

Then
D_{K}\widetilde{\beta}_{m}=0
on
U_{m}
, and
\widetilde{\beta}_{m}
converges on compact subsets of
U
.

For injectivity, let
F\in H^{0}(U,L\otimes\Omega^{k}_{X}(\log D))
satisfy
F=D_{K}g
for some
g\in C^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
.
As
F
is a
(k,0)
-form, all
(p,q)
-components of
D_{K}g
vanish for
q\geqslant 1
.
Using the previous argument, we can find
\gamma\in C^{\infty}_{k-2,K}(U,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
g-D_{K}\gamma
is a
(k-1,0)
-form.
Then

F=D_{K}(g-D_{K}\gamma),

so
g-D_{K}\gamma
is holomorphic on
U\setminus D
.
By the second part of Proposition
9.5
, we conclude
g-D_{K}\gamma\in H^{0}(U,L\otimes\Omega^{k-1}_{X}(\log D))
.
Hence
F
is
\nabla
-exact.
∎

For further reference, we record the following consequence.

Theorem 9.12
.

The following spaces are isomorphic:

\mathrm{Ker}\,\Delta_{K}\simeq\frac{\mathrm{Ker}\,(\bar{\partial}+\nabla):\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}{\mathrm{Im}\,(\bar{\partial}+\nabla):\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)}\simeq\mathbb{H}^{k}(X,L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log\sum D_{i})).

Proof.

This follows directly from
8.24
and
9.11
.
∎

Finally, we point out a variant of
9.11
. Nothing that the complex (
9.0.1
) does not change if we replace
\nabla
by

D_{K}=\nabla+\bar{\partial}
. Therefore, we have the complex

\cdots\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D_{K}}}{{\to}}\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L\to\cdots.

(9.12.1)

Now, for each
p\geqslant 0
, we have the following complex of sheaves:

\bar{\partial}:C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Lambda^{p,q}\Omega_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)\to C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Lambda^{p,q+1}\Omega_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L),

(9.12.2)

which is locally exact. This follows from a version of Fujiki’s lemma in the context of
C^{\infty}_{K}
, by considering solutions with minimal norm and applying the Bochner formula. The consequence is the same: the hypercohomology of (
9.12.1
) is computed by the cohomology of the double complex (
9.12.2
).

10
An extension theorem

10.1
Main results

In this section we prove the extension Theorem
10.1
below, which plays an important role in the "jumping of the cohomology locus" statement. The usual notation/conventions here are the usual ones fixed in Section ???

We introduce a few more notations: consider the decomposition

\Delta_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}Y_{i}

of
\Delta_{1}
as sum of prime divisors. For any subset

I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, we denote

Y_{I}:=Y_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap Y_{i_{p}},

which is a smooth closed submanifold of
X
of codimension
p
. We set

\Delta_{I;1}:=\sum_{i\notin I}(Y_{i}\cap Y_{I}),\quad\Delta_{I;2}:=\Delta_{2}\cap Y_{I},\quad\Delta_{I;3}:=\Delta_{3}\cap Y_{I}.

Then

D_{I}:=\Delta_{I;1}+\Delta_{I;2}+\Delta_{I;3}

is a simple normal crossing divisor on
Y_{I}
. We define

C^{\infty}_{i,K}(Y_{I},\Delta_{I;1},L)

the space of smooth forms in
8.9
for the log pair
(Y_{I},D_{I})
.

Note that, by
Proposition

6.12
, for any

\beta\in H^{0}\big(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\big)
, we have

\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}\beta=0
for each
i
. Hence,

\iota_{I}^{*}\beta\in H^{0}\big(Y_{I},\Omega_{Y_{I}}^{1}(\log D_{I})\big),

where
\iota_{I}:Y_{I}\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion map. Therefore,

\iota_{I}^{*}(L,\nabla)
defines a well-defined object in

M_{\mathrm{\rm DR}}(Y_{I}/D_{I})
. Moreover, the metric
\iota_{I}^{*}h
is a harmonic metric for
\iota_{I}^{*}L
, verifying the conditions of
Proposition

6.12
.

In what follows, we write
(L,h)
for
(\iota_{I}^{*}L,\iota_{I}^{*}h)
on
Y_{I}
by abuse of notation.

Our next result states as follows.

Theorem 10.1
.

In the above setting, let

\beta\in H^{0}\big(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\big)

be a log form and let
\xi
be a smooth
(0,1)
-form on
X
such that
d\xi=0
.
Let
u_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
such that the equality

D_{K}u_{0}=0\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus D.

We suppose that the following hold.

•

The form
u_{0}
extends to order one, i.e. there exists a log form
v_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
such that

D_{K}v_{1}=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u_{0}\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus D

•

For every non-empty
I\subset\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, any
D_{K}
-closed form in
C^{\infty}_{k-|I|,K}(Y_{I},L)
extends to order one, i.e, for any
s\in C^{\infty}_{k-|I|,K}(Y_{I},L)
if
D_{K}s=0
, then
-\iota_{I}^{*}(\xi+\beta)\wedge s
is
D_{K}
-exact on
Y_{I}
. Here
|I|
denotes the cardinality of
I
.

Then for any
k\geqslant 0
we can solve the equations

\begin{cases}D_{K}(u_{1})=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u_{0}\\
D_{K}(u_{2})=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u_{1}\\
\ldots\\
D_{K}(u_{k+1})=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u_{k}\\
\end{cases}

(10.1.1)

on
X\setminus D
such that
u_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
and such that
the series
\sum_{i\geqslant 0}t^{i}u_{i}
is convergent for
|t|\ll 1
.

Remark 10.2
.

By the first point of Proposition
6.12
, we know that
\beta
may have a logarithmic pole along
\Delta_{2}
and is smooth along
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
. Together with the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
), it follows that if

u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)\quad\text{(resp.\ }u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\text{)},

then the product

(\xi+\beta)\wedge u

belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
).

Remark 10.3
.

For each
t\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|t|\ll 1
we consider the twisted operator

D_{t,K}:=D_{K}+t(\xi+\beta)\wedge\cdot

(10.3.1)

and notice that
D_{t,K}^{2}=0
for each
t
. We will be interested in the dimension of the kernel of
D_{t,K}
acting on the space of
k
-forms
u
with log poles along
\Delta_{1}
as the parameter
t
varies.

In this setting, Theorem
10.1
can be reformulated as follows.

Theorem 10.4
.

Assume that an
L
-valued
D_{0,K}
-closed logarithmic form
u_{0}
on
X
extends to order one in the direction
\xi+\beta
, and that the same is true for every
L
-valued
D_{0,K}
-closed smooth form on every stratum of
\Delta_{1}
. Then

u_{0}
extends (as element of the kernel of
D_{t,K}
).

The proof of Theorem
10.1
will take us quite some time. The main steps are as follows. We show first that the system (
10.1.1
) can be solved in the space of currents, and then we show that we can "convert" (so to speak) currents to forms with log-poles. After that, the convergence issue is addressed by using the elliptic estimates in Section
8
.

Notations and conventions

To begin with, we fix some notations. Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold endowed with the metric
\omega_{D}
as above. We introduce

\alpha:=-\xi-\beta

in order to simplify the notations, and let

X_{0}:=X\setminus|\Delta_{2}|

be the complement of the divisor on which the singularities of
\omega_{D}
are of Poincaré-type. In the absence of the divisor
\Delta_{1}
, the operator
D_{K}
acts as follows

D_{K}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,L)

(10.4.1)

where
r
represents the total degree of the forms under considerations. The extension of this operator to forms with log poles
will be denoted by the same symbol

D_{K}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

(10.4.2)

by choosing to ignore the residues along the components of
\Delta_{1}
.

Next, each element
u
of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)(X_{0})
defines a twisted, smooth form with log poles along
\Delta_{1}
. By
8.18
, the residue of
u
along the component
Y_{i}
of
\Delta_{1}
is well defined and belongs to
C^{\infty}_{K}(Y_{i},\Delta_{1},L)
.
We denote it by
\mathcal{R}_{i}(u)
.
We define

\mathcal{D}_{K}(u):=D_{K}(u)+\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}(u)

(10.4.3)

The RHS of (
10.4.3
) is a current on
X_{0}
.
Notice that we have

D_{K}^{2}=0,\qquad\mathcal{D}_{K}^{2}=0\qquad\text{on }X_{0}.

(10.4.4)

To illustrate the idea, we first present the proof of
10.1
under the assumption that
\Delta_{1}=0
, which is essentially the same as in the compact case.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
if
\Delta_{1}=0
.

\bullet
Let
u_{0}
be a
D_{K}
-closed form with values in
L
, of total degree equal to
r
. By changing the representative (notice that this does not change anything to the problem we have to solve) we can assume that
u_{0}
is harmonic
–and therefore, we equally have
D_{K}^{c}u_{0}=0
.
We now use the hypothesis, and infer the existence of a
r
-form
v_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
so that the equality

D_{K}v_{1}-\alpha\wedge u_{0}=0.

(10.4.5)

\bullet

We show next that we can find a solution
v_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
of (
10.4.5
) which is actually
D_{K}^{c}
-exact.
Let
v_{1}
be a solution of (
10.4.5
). We now consider
D_{K}^{c}v_{1}
. It is
D_{K}^{c}
-exact. Moreover,
D_{K}^{c}v_{1}
is
D_{K}
–closed, since

D_{K}(D_{K}^{c}v_{1})=-D_{K}^{c}(D_{K}v_{1})=D_{K}^{c}(\alpha\wedge u_{0})=0,

(10.4.6)

where the last equality comes from the fact that
\alpha
and
u_{0}
belong to the kernel of
D_{K}^{c}
. By using the
D^{c}_{K}D_{K}
-lemma
Proposition

8.23
, we see that there exists a form
w_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
that

v_{1}-D_{K}w_{1}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,D_{K}^{c}

(10.4.7)

holds.
Therefore, we can assume that the solution
v_{1}
in (
10.4.5
) is
D_{K}^{c}
-closed. Finally, by
8.20
, we have the Hodge decomposition

v_{1}=\mathcal{H}(v_{1})+D_{K}^{c}w_{2}

(10.4.8)

of
v_{1}
, where
\mathcal{H}(v_{1})
is the harmonic part of
v_{1}
and
w_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
.
Set
u_{1}:=D^{c}_{K}w_{2}
. Then
u_{1}
is
D_{K}^{c}
-exact and

D_{K}u_{1}-\alpha\wedge u_{0}=0\qquad\text{ on }X.

\bullet
Now we solve the remaining equations. Consider next the exterior product

\alpha\wedge u_{1}.

(10.4.9)

By Remark
10.2
,
\alpha\wedge u_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,L)
.
Since
u_{1}
is
D_{K}^{c}
-exact,
\alpha\wedge u_{1}
is also
D_{K}^{c}
-exact.
\alpha\wedge u_{1}
is equally
D_{K}
-closed, since the derivative of
u_{1}
is a multiple of
\alpha
. By the
D^{c}_{K}D_{K}
-lemma
Proposition

8.23
, we can therefore find a
v_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
such that

D_{K}D_{K}^{c}v_{2}=\alpha\wedge u_{1}.

(10.4.10)

In particular we found a
u_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X)
such that

D_{K}u_{2}=\alpha\wedge u_{1},\qquad u_{2}=D_{K}^{c}v_{2}.

(10.4.11)

By this procedure we obtain successively

D_{K}u_{m}=\alpha\wedge u_{m-1},\qquad u_{m}=D_{K}^{c}v_{m}

(10.4.12)

for all
m\geqslant 1
. This means precisely that
u_{0}
can be extended to an arbitrary order. Finally, thanks to Proposition
10.21
, we have the convergence of
\sum_{i}t^{i}u_{i}
for
|t|\ll 1
.
∎

Remark 10.5
.

In particular we see that if
u_{0}
is
D_{K}

and

D_{K}^{c}
–closed and moreover it deforms to the first order, then
the equations (
10.1.1
) can be solved, with the additional constraint
u_{m}\in\mathrm{Im}\,D_{K}^{c}
for any
m\geqslant 1
.

In the remaining part of this section, we will give a proof of Theorem
10.1
in the general case. The structure is as follows.
In subsection
10.2
, we will solve the equations (
10.1.1
) in the sense of current. In subsection
10.3
, we will establish a regularity proposition, which tells us that we can transform the current solution to a log smooth solution. In the subsection
10.4
, we will complete the proof of Theorem
10.1
.

10.2
Solutions in the sense of currents

Note that in the proof of Theorem
10.1
when
\Delta_{1}=0
, the conclusion of the first bullet is a consequence of the fact that we have
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{K}=\Delta^{\prime}_{K}
on
X
.
In the case when
\Delta_{1}\neq 0
, the following lemma plays a similar role.

Lemma 10.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.1
, we suppose that
\Delta_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}
. Then there exists a current
u_{\Delta_{1}}
with support in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
and a current
v_{0}
on
X
such that
\widehat{u}_{0}
defined by

\widehat{u}_{0}=u_{0}+\mathcal{D}_{K}v_{0}+u_{\Delta_{1}}

satsifes the following properties:

•

We have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widehat{u}_{0}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i;0}\qquad\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{R}_{i;0})\subset Y_{i}

(10.6.1)

together with the successive relations

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p};0}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p};0},\qquad\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p};0})\subset Y_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p}}

(10.6.2)

•

For any
p\geqslant 1
and any multi-index
i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p}
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\widehat{u}_{0}=0,\quad\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p},0}=0.

Moreover, the currents
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p};0}
satisfy the skew-symmetry relations.

•

For every
p
, as a current on
X
,
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{p};0}
is supported in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.

Proof.

Note first that we have the relation

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{0}=\sum_{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i;0},

(10.6.3)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i;0}
are supported in
Y_{i}
, and in general for each
p\geqslant 1
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{p};0},

(10.6.4)

where the currents
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}
are supported in
\cap_{j=1}^{p}Y_{i_{j}}
.
Notice that the skew-symmetry equality
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\dots ij\dots;0}=-\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\dots ji\dots;0}
is automatic.

Here we discuss the construction of
\widetilde{R}_{i;0}
.
By
8.18
, we note that for any

u\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

we have

\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\in C^{\infty}_{k-1,K}(Y_{i},\Delta_{i;1},L).

We then define

\widetilde{R}_{i;0}:=(\iota_{i})_{*}\bigl(\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\bigr),

which is a current on
X
of degree
k+1
with coefficients in
L
.

Moreover, by
8.18
again, we have

\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}}\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\in C^{\infty}_{k-2,K}(Y_{i,j},\Delta_{i,j;1},L).

Since
D_{K}
has no poles along
\Delta_{1}
, its restriction to each
Y_{i}
is well defined.
In this case, using
D_{K}^{2}=0
, we obtain

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widetilde{R}_{i;0}=\sum_{j;j\neq i}(\iota_{i})_{*}(\tilde{\iota}_{ji})_{*}\bigl(\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}}\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\bigr)=({\iota}_{ji})_{*}\bigl(\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}}\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\bigr)

where each
\tilde{\iota}_{ji}:Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\hookrightarrow Y_{i}
and
\iota_{ji}:Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}\hookrightarrow X
are inclusions.

We then set

\widetilde{R}_{ji;0}:=(\iota_{ji})_{*}\bigl(\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}}\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{i}}(u)\bigr),

which is also a current of degree
k+2
on
X
with coefficients in
L
. By construction,
\widetilde{R}_{ji;0}
is supported in
Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
, and is skew-symmetric; i.e. we have

\widetilde{R}_{ji;0}=-\widetilde{R}_{ij;0}.

By iterating this procedure, for any
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we obtain
