\widetilde{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}
, which is

•

a current of degree
k+p
with coefficients in
L
;

•

supported in
Y_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}}:=Y_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap Y_{i_{p}}
for distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}
.

•

skew-symmetric.

By using (
10.6.4
), we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1\dots m;0}=0,

and so we can write

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1\dots m;0}=\mathcal{D}_{K}\Xi_{1\dots m}+\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;0},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;0}=0.

(10.6.5)

For any permutation
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
of the set
\{1,\dots,m\}
we define

\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m};0}
and
\Xi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}
by skew-symmetry.

Then we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m};0}=0\qquad\text{and }\qquad\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}

with the additional condition that
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m};0}
and
\Xi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}
are skew-symmetric.

Now we move the next "floor": considering the currents supported on the complete intersection of
(m-1)
-components
Y_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}
. By combining (
10.6.5
) and (
10.6.4
) we obtain

\mathcal{D}_{K}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}-\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1},\dots i_{m-1}}\big)=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0},

(10.6.6)

for which we notice that the RHS is
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-closed. Then we can write

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}=\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1}}+\mathcal{D}_{K}\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1}}+\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}.

(10.6.7)

where
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
.
Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}
and
\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1}}
are skew-symmetric. Then we can assume that this is also the case for
\Xi_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1};0}
. Indeed, this can be seen by first defining these objects for

i_{1}<\dots<i_{m-1}

and then imposing the skew-symmetry condition (precisely as we did in the proof of the first point).

We obtain

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}

(10.6.8)

as it follows from (
10.6.6
) and (
10.6.7
).

Consider next
\displaystyle\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}
as current on
Y_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-2}}
. By construction, we know that

\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots,i_{m-2};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}

on
Y_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}
.

Moroever, by (
10.6.7
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}-\sum_{k,j}\Xi_{kji_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}-\mathcal{D}_{K}\big(\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}-\mathcal{D}_{K}\big(\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}\big).

(10.6.9)

The equality

\mathcal{D}_{K}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}-\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}\big)=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}

(10.6.11)

follows.
As the RHS of (
10.6.11
) is
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-closed, we can write

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}=\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}+\mathcal{D}_{K}\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}+\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}

(10.6.12)

such that
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2};0}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
. As usual, we can impose that

\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-2}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-2};0}
are
skew-symmetric.

By iterating this procedure (i.e. using induction), we can thus construct
\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{p}}
such that

\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}-\sum_{j}\Xi_{ji_{1}\dots i_{p}}+\mathcal{D}_{K}\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{p}}

(10.6.13)

satisfies

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{p};0},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}

(10.6.14)

such that moreover
\Xi_{i_{1}\dots i_{p}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}
are skew-symmetric.

Finally, we consider the current

u_{0}-\sum_{i=1}\Xi_{i}

(10.6.15)

and evaluate its differential, which equals

\mathcal{D}_{K}\big(u_{0}-\sum_{i}\Xi_{i}\big)=\sum_{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{i;0}-\sum_{i}\mathcal{D}_{K}\Xi_{i}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i;0},

(10.6.16)

where the last equality is a consequence of (
10.6.13
) together with the skew-symmetry of
\Xi_{i,j}
.
The RHS of (
10.6.16
) is
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-closed, so we can write

u_{0}-\sum_{i}\Xi_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{K}(v_{0})+\widehat{u}_{0},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\widehat{u}_{0}=0.

(10.6.17)

In conclusion, from the relations above we get

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widehat{u}_{0}=\sum_{i=1}\mathcal{R}_{i;0},

(10.6.18)

and

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k};0}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{k};0}

(10.6.19)

where
\widehat{u}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k};0}
are
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-closed and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k};0}
are skew-symmetric. This ends the proof of the second point.

For the last point, it comes from the fact that every current
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k};0}
is constructed as a current on
Y_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
. Therefore, if we consider
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k};0}
as a current on
X
, it is supported in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.
∎

Now we would like to solve (
10.1.1
) in the sense of currents. More precisely, we have

Proposition 10.7
.

We are in the setting of Theorem
10.1
. Let
\widehat{u}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};0}
be the currents constructed in Lemma
10.6
. Then for every
p
and
k\geqslant 1
, there exists currents
u_{k}
on
X
and currents
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p};k}
supported on
\cap_{s=1}^{p}Y_{i_{s}}
such that

•

For every
p
and
k\geqslant 1
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{p};k}=\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{p};k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{p};k},

with
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{p};k}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
on
\cap_{s=1}^{p}Y_{i_{s}}
and the currents
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{p};k}
are skew-symmetric.

•

We have

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{1}=\alpha\wedge\widehat{u}_{0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,1}

(10.7.1)

and for every
k\geqslant 2
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{k}=\alpha\wedge u_{k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,k}.

(10.7.2)

Moreover, for every
k\geqslant 1
, we have
u_{k}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
.

•

For every
k,j
,
\mathcal{R}_{j;k}
is supported in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.

Proof.

Recall that by Lemma
10.6
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}\widehat{u}_{0}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i;0}\qquad\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{R}_{i;0})\subset Y_{i}

(10.7.3)

together with the successive relations: for every
p\geqslant 1

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p};0}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\cdots,i_{p};0},\qquad\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p};0})\subset\cap_{s=1}^{p}Y_{i_{s}}

(10.7.4)

and the relation
\mathcal{R}_{\cdots ij\cdots;0}=-\mathcal{R}_{\cdots ji\cdots;0}
.

Note that our second assumption concerning the first order extension translates as follows: for every
p\geqslant 1
, and every
harmonic form

u_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p}}
on
\cap_{s=1}^{p}Y_{i_{s}}
the following holds

\alpha\wedge u_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{p}}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}.

(10.7.5)

We start with the "bottom floor"
Y_{1\dots m}:=\cap_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}
.
Since (
10.7.4
) implies that
\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}
,
by applying (
10.7.5
), we infer that

\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;0}
is
\mathcal{D}_{K}
-exact on
Y_{1\dots m}
.
The case
\Delta_{1}=\emptyset
being already settled, we obtain solutions of the following equations

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;k}=\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;k-1},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;k}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}

(10.7.6)

for any
k\geqslant 1
.

Since the initial family of residues
(\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m};0})
satisfies the skew-symmetry property, we can assume that the same is true for
(\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m};k})
, for any
k\geqslant 1
. This is immediate: we first construct

\mathcal{R}_{1\dots m;k}
, for any
k\geqslant 1
, then impose the relation

\mathcal{R}_{\dots ij\dots;k}=-\mathcal{R}_{\dots ji\dots;k}

and remark that the corresponding equalities (
10.7.6
) are satisfied for
any permutation
(i_{1}\dots i_{m})
of
\{1,\dots,m\}
.

Now we move to the next floor and consider next the equations

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}=\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}.

(10.7.7)

We intend to show that the RHS is
\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-exact.

First of all, we claim that the RHS of (
10.7.7
) belongs to
\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}+\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
.
In fact we have
\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
by (
10.7.6
).
To treat the first term in RHS of (
10.7.7
), by Lemma
10.6
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
. Therefore we can write

\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}=a+b,

where
a\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
and
b
is harmonic. As
\alpha\wedge b
is
\mathcal{D}_{K}
-exact by (
10.7.5
), we know that

\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};0}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}+\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}.

As a consequence, the RHS of (
10.7.7
) belongs to
\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}+\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
.

Secondly, we claim that the RHS of (
10.7.7
) is
\mathcal{D}_{K}
and
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-closed. This is verified by a direct calculation, cf.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{K}(\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1})

\displaystyle=-\alpha\wedge\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}+\alpha\wedge\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}=0

as well as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\big(\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}\big)

\displaystyle=-\alpha\wedge\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\mathcal{R}_{ji_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}=0.

As a consequence of the above two claims, by using the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma, the RHS of (
10.7.7
) is
\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-exact on
Y_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1}}
, for each multi-index
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1})
and it follows that we can find a solution
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{m-1};1}
of (
10.7.7
) such that

\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}.

Our next claim is that we can determine
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1};1}

in such a way that they are skew-symmetric. Indeed, for each ordered multi-index

j_{1}<\dots<j_{m-1}

we take an arbitrary solution
\mathcal{R}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1};0}
of (
10.7.7
), which belongs to the image of
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
. Then, for each permutation
\sigma
of

\{1,\dots,m-1\}
we define

\mathcal{R}_{j_{\sigma(1)}\dots j_{\sigma(m-1)};1}:=\varepsilon(\sigma)\mathcal{R}_{j_{1}\dots j_{m-1};1}

where
\varepsilon(\sigma)
is the signature of
\sigma
. We then have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{j_{\sigma(1)}\dots j_{\sigma(m-1)};1}=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\sigma)\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{j_{1}\dots j_{m-1};1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\sigma)\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{j_{1}\dots j_{m-1};0}+\varepsilon(\sigma)\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{jj_{1}\dots j_{m-1};1}

(10.7.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{j_{\sigma(1)}\dots j_{\sigma(m-1)};0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{jj_{\sigma(1)}\dots j_{\sigma(m-1)};1}.

(10.7.9)

In conclusion, we can solve the equation (
10.7.7
) in such a way that the solution belongs to the image of
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
and it is skew-symmetric with respect to the indexes
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1})
.

Now we solve the successive equations

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}=\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k},

(10.7.11)

for
k\geqslant 2
. In this case (i.e.
k\geqslant 2
) the RHS of (
10.7.11
) is
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
-exact by induction. Moreover, it is
\mathcal{D}_{K}
-closed by the following calculation, where we argue by induction on
k
in (
10.7.11
).

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{K}(\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k})=

\displaystyle-\alpha\wedge\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k-1}

\displaystyle+\alpha\wedge\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k-1}+\sum_{s,j}\mathcal{R}_{s,j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}

(10.7.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(10.7.13)

By the
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma we infer the existence of
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}
such that

\mathcal{D}_{K}\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}=\alpha\wedge\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k},

with
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
. By the same argument as before, we can assume that the currents

\mathcal{R}_{i_{1},\dots i_{m-1};k}
are skew-symmetric for all
k\geqslant 2
.

Induction and the same arguments as above show that we can find the solutions
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{p},k}
for any
1\leqslant p\leqslant m
and any
k
. The first point of the proposition is proved.

For the second point of the proposition, we determine first
u_{1}
so that

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{1}=\alpha\wedge u_{0}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j,1}

(10.7.14)

holds. As before, the RHS is
\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}\mathcal{D}_{K}
-exact. Then we can find a solution
u_{1}
of (
10.7.14
) such that
u_{1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathcal{D}_{K}^{c}
. To finish we remark that we can iterate this and solve the equations

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{k}=\alpha\wedge u_{k-1}+\sum_{j}\mathcal{R}_{j;k}

(10.7.15)

for all
k\geqslant 2
. Since the support of the currents
\mathcal{R}_{j;k}
is contained in
\Delta_{1}
, the second point is completely established.

For the last point, it comes from the fact that every current
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{j};k}
is constructed as a current on
Y_{i_{1}\dots i_{j}}
. Therefore, if we consider
\mathcal{R}_{i_{1}\dots i_{j};k}
as a current on
X
, it is supported in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.

∎

10.3
The regularity statement

By Proposition
10.7
, Theorem
10.1

is almost proved, modulo the fact that instead of being forms with log poles, the solutions
(u_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
are currents. We explain next how this last issue is addressed.
In order to illustrate the method we will use, we first analyze the following particular case.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
X
be a Kähler manifold, and let
\Delta
be a snc divisor on
X
. We denote by
V
a holomorphic vector bundle on
X
, and
let
u_{X}
be a
V
-valued smooth
(p,q)
-form with log poles along
\Delta
. We suppose that there exists a
V
-valued current
T
on
X
such that the following equality

\bar{\partial}T=u_{X}+T_{\Delta}

(10.8.1)

holds, where
T_{\Delta}
is a
V
-current with support in
\Delta
of order
0
, namely

\int_{X}T_{\Delta}\wedge\phi=0

for any test
V^{\star}
- valued form
\phi
such that
\phi|_{\Delta}=0
. Then there exists a smooth
V
-valued
(p,q-1)
-form with log poles
v
such that the equality

\bar{\partial}v=u_{X}

(10.8.2)

holds on
X\setminus\Delta
.

Proof.

One of the key points in the next arguments is taken from the paper by K. Liu, S. Rao and X. Wan
[
LRW19
, Prop 2.4]
.
In order to avoid any risk of confusion, we denote by

C^{\infty}_{0,q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta)\otimes V)

the space of
V
-valued smooth
(p,q)
-form with log poles along
\Delta
.

Let

\mathcal{I}:C^{\infty}_{0,q}(X,\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log\Delta)\otimes V)\to C^{\infty}_{0,q}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V)

be the map which identifies a logarithmic
(p,q)
-form with a

(0,q)
-form with values in the holomorphic vector bundle
\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V
. Let
\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle
be the dual of
\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle
.
We equally introduce the natural map

\iota:C^{\infty}_{n,n-q}(X,\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V^{\star})\to C^{\infty}_{n-p,n-q}(X,V^{\star})

obtained by contracting with the vector fields.

We suppose that
\Delta=\sum D_{i}
. We recall the statements Lemma 2.1 and Lemma 2.2 in
[
LRW19
]
: we have

\iota(\gamma)|_{D_{i}}=0,\qquad\int_{X}\alpha\wedge\iota(\beta)=\int_{X}\mathcal{I}(\alpha)\wedge\beta

(10.8.3)

where
\gamma
is a
\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V^{\star}
-valued form of type
(n,n-q)
, and the second equality in (
10.8.3
) has the following meaning:

\bullet
On the LHS,
\alpha
is a
V
-valued smooth
(p,q)
–form with log poles which is wedged with the
V^{\star}
-valued
(n-p,n-q)
form
\iota(\beta)
.

\bullet
On the RHS,
\mathcal{I}(\alpha)
is a
(0,q)
-form with valued in
\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V
, and
\beta
is a
(n,n-q)

form with values in the dual bundle (so the wedge makes perfectly sense).

We remark that by (
10.8.1
), we have
\bar{\partial}\mathcal{I}(u_{X})=0
.
Let us fix an arbitrary, Hermitian metric
h
on
\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V
, as well as a Kähler metric on
X
. The Hodge decomposition (for the Laplace operator associated to
\bar{\partial}
) induced by this data gives

\mathcal{I}(u_{X})=\mathcal{H}+\bar{\partial}v

(10.8.4)

where
\mathcal{H}
is
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-harmonic and
v
corresponds to a form with log poles
w
.

We introduce the following antilinear-operator

\sharp:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V)\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V^{\star})

such that the equality

\int_{X}\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle_{h}\omega_{X}^{n}=\int_{X}s_{1}\wedge\sharp s_{2}

(10.8.5)

holds for every
s_{1},s_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V)
.

We first show that

\bar{\partial}(\sharp\mathcal{H})=0.

(10.8.6)

In fact, for any
s^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta\rangle\otimes V)
, we have

\int_{X}s^{\prime}\wedge\bar{\partial}\sharp(\mathcal{H})=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\bar{\partial}s^{\prime}\wedge\sharp\mathcal{H}=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\langle\bar{\partial}s^{\prime},\mathcal{H}\rangle_{h}\omega^{n}_{X}

=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\langle s^{\prime},\bar{\partial}^{\star}\mathcal{H}\rangle_{h}\omega^{n}_{X}=0.

Therefore we obtain (
10.8.6
).

To prove the Lemma, we need show that the harmonic part
\mathcal{H}
in (
10.8.4
) must vanish.
We have

\int_{X}|\mathcal{H}|^{2}dV=\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{I}(u_{X}),\mathcal{H}\rangle dV=\int_{X}\mathcal{I}(u_{X})\wedge\sharp\mathcal{H}

(10.8.7)

which equals

\int_{X}u_{X}\wedge\iota(\sharp\mathcal{H})

(10.8.8)

by (
10.8.3
). The main point is that we have

\int_{X}u_{X}\wedge\iota(\sharp\mathcal{H})=\int_{X}(u_{X}+T_{\Delta})\wedge\iota(\sharp\mathcal{H})

(10.8.9)

by the first part of (
10.8.3
), and the properties of the current
T_{\Delta}
. By hypothesis, the RHS of (
10.8.9
) is equal to

\int_{X}\bar{\partial}T\wedge\iota(\sharp\mathcal{H})=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}T\wedge\bar{\partial}\big(\iota\circ\sharp\mathcal{H}\big)=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}T\wedge\iota\big(\bar{\partial}(\sharp\mathcal{H})\big)=0

(10.8.10)

where the last equality is a consequence of (
10.8.6
).
∎

10.3.1
A few more notations

We are next aiming at the version of
10.8
in our setting and as a first step we introduce/recall some notations.
We consider the holomorphic vector bundles

E_{p}:=\Omega^{p}_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L,\qquad E^{\star}_{p}:=\wedge^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L^{\star}.

The metric
g_{D}
on
T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
together with
h_{L}
induce a Hermitian structure on
E_{p}
and its dual.
Furthermore, the space
X_{0}
is endowed with the metric
\omega_{D}
and let

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p}),\qquad\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})

be the spaces obtained as in Definition
8.9
.

Let
C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
be the space of
L
-valued log smooth form and let

\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L)
be the space of
L
-currents, which is dual to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{K}(X,L^{\star})
cf.
8.27
.
The identification map

\mathcal{I}:C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)\simeq\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})

(10.8.11)

is defined by applying the
"\mathcal{I}"
in the proof of Lemma
10.8
component-wise.
If the
\bullet
on the RHS of (
10.8.11
) is equal
i
, then this coincides with
\mathcal{E}_{i}
in
7.7.2
of the Convention
7.7
.

We introduce a differential on the space
\oplus_{p+q=i}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
via the operator
\mathcal{I}

D_{K,E}:\oplus_{p+q=i}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})\to\oplus_{p+q=i+1}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})

(10.8.12)

by imposing that the equality

D_{K,E}\circ\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}\circ D_{K}

(10.8.13)

holds.

In a similar way, the operator

D_{K,E^{\star}}:\oplus_{p+q=i}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})\to\oplus_{p+q=i-1}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})

(10.8.14)

is defined
so that we have

\int_{X}D_{K,E}(s)\wedge t=(-1)^{q+1}\int_{X}s\wedge D_{K,E^{\star}}(t)

(10.8.15)

for any
s\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p}),t\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})
.

It will be useful to introduce the following anti-linear-operator

\sharp:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})

so that the equality

\int_{X}\langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle_{g_{D},h_{L}}\omega_{D}^{n}=\int_{X}s_{1}\wedge\sharp s_{2}

(10.8.16)

is verified for any
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
. Note that although the two metrics
g_{D}
and
\omega_{D}
are
not smooth, the following holds.

Lemma 10.9
.

We consider a smooth form
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
. Then we automatically have
\sharp u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E_{p}^{\star})
.

Proof.

The matter is very easy: by using the coordinates/frames as in (
8.16.1
), we write locally

u=\sum_{|I|=p,|J|=q}u_{I\overline{J}}l_{I}^{\star}\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\otimes e_{L}.

(10.9.1)

Then we have the formula

\sharp u=\sum_{|I|=p,|J|=n-q}\overline{u}_{K\overline{L}}a^{\overline{K}I}b^{\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{J}L}\det(a_{\beta\overline{\alpha}})l_{I}\wedge e\wedge\overline{e}_{J}^{\star}\otimes e_{L}

(10.9.2)

where we use the notations in Section
8.2
. This quickly follows from the definition (
10.8.16
). The criteria of Lemma
8.17
together with the fact that the coefficients
a_{\beta\overline{\alpha}},b_{\beta\overline{\alpha}}
and each of their inverses are functions with polynomial growth
finish the proof.
∎

Remark 10.10
.

The three operators we have just defined are not unrelated. One can immediately verify that the equality

\sharp\circ D_{K,E}^{\star}=D_{K,E^{\star}}\circ\sharp

holds (possibly up to a sign…), where
D_{K,E}^{\star}
is computed with respect to the metrics
\omega_{D},g_{D}
on
X_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}
. We will not discuss this equality here, because it follows from the proof of Proposition
10.12
below.

10.3.2
Proof of the regularity result

Consider next
u\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
, a smooth form with log poles.
Then as consequence of
Proposition

8.28
, the form
u
induces a current which will be denoted by

T_{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L).

(10.10.1)

Recall that we have a natural contraction map
\iota
defined on the space
C^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E_{p}^{\star})
as follows

\iota:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E_{p}^{\star})=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\otimes L^{\star})\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n-p,n-q),K}(X,L^{\star}).

(10.10.2)

Remark 10.11
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
be a smooth form with log poles. Then with respect to the notations above we can write

\mathcal{D}_{K}\circ T_{u}=T_{D_{K}u}+\mathrm{Res}\,_{\Delta_{1}}u.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{K}
and
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
be the operators on the spaces of currents
\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,E_{\bullet})

induced by
D_{K}
and
D_{K,E}
.
We list next several properties which will be used later.

Proposition 10.12
.

Let
s\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
and
t\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})
. Then we have

(1)

D_{K}\circ\iota(t)=\iota\circ D_{K,E^{\star}}(t)
.

(2)

If
D^{\star}_{K,E}s=0
, then
D_{K,E^{\star}}\circ\sharp s=0
.

Proof.

We establish the first point by duality. Note that
\iota(t)\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L^{\star})
.
Let
w\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
. We have

\int_{X}w\wedge D_{K}\circ\iota(t)=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}D_{K}w\wedge\iota(t)=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}D_{K,E}\mathcal{I}(w)\wedge t=\int_{X}\mathcal{I}(w)\wedge D_{K,E^{\star}}(t).

Then we obtain
D_{K}\circ\iota(t)=\iota\circ D_{K,E^{\star}}(t)
.

For the second point, note that for every
s^{\prime}\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
, we have

\int_{X}s^{\prime}\wedge D_{K,E^{\star}}\circ\sharp s=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}D_{K,E}s^{\prime}\wedge\sharp s=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\langle D_{K,E}s^{\prime},s\rangle_{g_{D},h_{L}}\omega^{n}_{D}

=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\langle s^{\prime},D^{\star}_{K,E}s\rangle_{g_{D},h_{L}}\omega^{n}_{D}=0.

Then
D_{K,E^{\star}}\circ\sharp s=0
.
∎

Let
\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
be the dual space of
\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(n,n-q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})
. We show next that there exists a natural morphism

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}:\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)\to\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})

(10.12.1)

induced by the identification map
\mathcal{I}
(which justifies the use of almost the same notation).

Proposition 10.13
.

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
be a
L
-valued current.
Then it induces an element
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
by the formula

\int_{X}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\wedge\phi:=\int_{X}\tau\wedge\iota(\phi),

for every
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{(n,q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})
.
Moreover, we have

\int_{X}\mathcal{D}_{K,E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\tau))\wedge\phi=(-1)^{\bullet}\int_{X}\tau\wedge D_{K}\circ\iota(\phi)

so that we can write

\mathcal{D}_{K,E}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ\mathcal{D}_{K}(\tau).

Proof.

It is clear that
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)
is linear. The continuity follows from inequalities of type

\sum_{r=0}^{N}\|\nabla^{r}\iota(\phi)\|^{2}_{a}\leqslant C(N,a)\sum_{r=0}^{N}\|\nabla^{r}\phi\|^{2}_{a}

(10.13.1)

In fact, when
r=0
, by the construction of
g_{D}
, there exists some constant
C>0
such that

|e|_{g_{D}}\geqslant C|e|_{\omega_{D}}\qquad\text{for every }e\in T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle.

As a consequence, for any
u\in\Lambda^{p}T_{X}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle
and
v\in K_{X}
, we have

|u(v)|_{\omega_{D}}\leqslant C|v|_{\omega_{D}}\cdot|u|_{g_{D}}.

We obtain thus (
10.13.1
) for
r=0
. If
r\geqslant 1
, this is immediately verified by using the coordinates as in Section
8.2
, so we don’t provide any further details.

The last part of our statement is a direct consequence of Proposition
10.12
.
∎

Remark 10.14
.

Note that the right-hand side of (
10.13.1
) cannot be controlled by the left-hand side of (
10.13.1
). As a matter of fact, we will show in
Proposition

10.16
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
is not injective, so the inverse of
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
does not exist.

Before proving the regularity result Proposition
10.17
, we need a few auxiliary statements. The first of them states as follows.

Proposition 10.15
.

We consider the map

\rho:\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})\to\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p}),

as in (
10.10.1
),
so that
\rho(s)
is the current associated to a section
s\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
.
Let
s\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
. If
\rho(s)
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact, then
s
is
D_{K,E}
-exact.

In particular, let
u\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u}
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact, then there exists a
v\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
such that

u=D_{K}v\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus\Delta_{1}.

Proof.

Note first that since
\rho(s)
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact, the equality

D_{K,E}s=0

follows. The Laplace operator
\Delta_{K,E}=[D_{K,E},D_{K,E}^{\star}]
is elliptic (where the adjoint is computed with respect to the usual metrics) and we have already seen that the Hodge decomposition holds.
We can thus write

s=\mathcal{H}+D_{K,E}v

where
\mathcal{H}
is
\Delta_{K,E}
-harmonic and
v\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
. We show next that the harmonic part
\mathcal{H}
is equal to zero.

To this end, remark that the equalities

\displaystyle\int_{X}\langle\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}\rangle_{g_{D},h_{L}}\omega^{n}_{D}=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\langle s,\mathcal{H}\rangle_{g_{D},h_{L}}\omega^{n}_{D}=\int_{X}s\wedge\sharp\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\rho(s)\wedge\sharp\mathcal{H}

hold. Since
\Delta_{K,E}\mathcal{H}=0
, by
(2)
of
Proposition

10.12
the form
\sharp\mathcal{H}
is
D_{K,E^{\star}}
-closed. Together with the fact that
\rho(s)
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact, we obtain

\int_{X}\rho(s)\wedge\sharp\mathcal{H}=0.

Therefore
\mathcal{H}=0
and
s
is
D_{K,E}
-exact.

For the second part, note that we have the equality
\rho\circ\mathcal{I}(u)=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u}
. Then
\rho\circ\mathcal{I}(u)
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact.
By the first part, we know that
\mathcal{I}(u)
is
D_{K,E}
-exact, namely

\mathcal{I}(u)=D_{K,E}f

for some
f\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
. Set
v:=\mathcal{I}^{-1}(f)
. Then we have

u=D_{K}v\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus\Delta_{1},

which concludes the proof. ∎

The following simple remark will equally be useful.

Proposition 10.16
.

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
be a
L
-valued current of order zero. Assume moreover that the support of
\tau
is contained in
\Delta_{1}
. Then we have

\mathcal{I}(\tau)=0\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p}).

Proof.

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{(n,q),K}(X,E^{\star}_{p})
. By definition we have

\int_{X}\mathcal{I}(\tau)\wedge\phi=\int_{X}\tau\wedge\iota(\phi)=0,

where the last equality is a consequence of the fact that
\iota(\phi)
vanishes on
\Delta_{1}
.
We deduce that
\mathcal{I}(\tau)=0
.
∎

Finally, we establish the following version of Lemma
10.8
in our setting.

Proposition 10.17
.

Let
u_{X}\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
be an
L
-valued, smooth form with log poles along
\Delta_{1}
. We suppose that there exists a current
T
on
X
with the property that

\mathcal{D}_{K}T=T_{u_{X}}+T_{\Delta_{1}}

(10.17.1)

where
T_{\Delta_{1}}
is a current with support in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.
Then there exists a form
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
such that the equality

u_{X}=D_{K}v\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus\Delta_{1}.

(10.17.2)

Proof.

We have
\displaystyle T_{u_{X}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)
-cf. (
10.10.1
)- and via the map
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
in (
10.12.1
) we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u_{X}}\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}\mathcal{D}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p}).

On the other hand, by applying
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
in (
10.17.1
), we see that the equality

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ\mathcal{D}_{K}T=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u_{X}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\Delta_{1}})

holds.
Thanks to the second part of
Proposition

10.13
and
Proposition

10.16
, we obtain

\mathcal{D}_{K,E}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(T)=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u_{X}}.

Therefore
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\circ T_{u_{X}}
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact in the sense of currents.
Thanks to the second part of
Proposition

10.15
, we obtain that

u_{X}=D_{K}(v)\qquad\text{ on }X\setminus\Delta_{1}

for some
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
.
The proof is finished.
∎

10.4
End of the proof of Theorem
10.1

In order to make the presentation more fluid, we will next use the following terminology. Given a smooth form
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
, we say that it is
D_{K}
-closed if
D_{K}u=0
on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
.
Similarly, we say
that
u
is
D_{K}
-exact (resp.
D^{c}_{K}
-exact,
D_{K}D^{c}_{K}
-exact) if
u=D_{K}v
(resp.
u=D^{c}_{K}v
,
u=D_{K}D^{c}_{K}v
) on
X\setminus\Delta_{1}
for some
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.

We first
convert
the currents obtained at Section

10.2
into log smooth forms, disregarding the convergence issues.
Let
\widehat{u}_{0}
and
\{u_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L)
be the currents constructed in
Proposition

10.7
so that we have

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{1}=\widehat{u}_{0}\wedge\alpha+T_{0}

and for
k\geqslant 1

\mathcal{D}_{K}u_{k}=u_{k-1}\wedge\alpha+T_{k}

(10.17.3)

(up to a sign), where the support of
\{T_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{D}_{K}(X,L)
are currents supported in
\Delta_{1}
of order
0
.
Recall that by
Proposition

10.13
, we have a natural morphism

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}:\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,L)\to\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,E_{\bullet}).

We claim that there exist a set of
E_{\bullet}
-valued currents

\{v_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,E_{\bullet})
such that the form

s_{0}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\widehat{u}_{0})+\mathcal{D}_{K,E}v_{0}\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})=\mathcal{I}\big(C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\big)

(10.17.4)

as well as

s_{k}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(u_{k})+\mathcal{D}_{K,E}v_{k}+v_{k-1}\wedge\alpha\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})=\mathcal{I}\big(C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\big),

(10.17.5)

where
k\geqslant 1
.

Before providing the arguments for this claim, note that these assumptions imply that
\{s_{k}\}
solve the equations (
10.1.1
). Indeed, we have the string of equalities

\displaystyle D_{K}s_{k}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{K}(u_{k}+\mathcal{D}_{k}v_{k}+v_{k-1}\wedge\alpha)|_{X\setminus\Delta_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{K}(u_{k})+\mathcal{D}_{K}v_{k-1}\wedge\alpha)|_{X\setminus\Delta_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(u_{k-1}\wedge\alpha+\mathcal{D}_{K}v_{k-1}\wedge\alpha)|_{X\setminus\Delta_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((u_{k-1}+\mathcal{D}_{K}v_{k-1}+v_{k-2}\wedge\alpha)\wedge\alpha)|_{X\setminus\Delta_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{k-1}\wedge\alpha

as required. Next we go ahead and prove the claim by induction.

Proof.

For
k=0
, we just take the
v_{0}
to be the
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(v_{0})
(after changing sign) for the
v_{0}
in
10.6
. Note that by
Proposition

10.16
,
\mathcal{I}(u_{\Delta_{1}})=0
. In other words,
s_{0}
equals to the original log smooth form
u_{0}
.

We assume that
v_{0},\cdots,v_{k}
have been constructed and satisfy (
10.17.5
). To obtain
v_{k+1}
, we use the equality (
10.17.3
). Together with
Proposition

10.13
and
Proposition

10.16
, this implies that the equality

\mathcal{D}_{K,E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(u_{k+1})+v_{k}\wedge\alpha)=(\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(u_{k})+\mathcal{D}_{K,E}v_{k}+v_{k-1}\wedge\alpha)\wedge\alpha=s_{k}\wedge\alpha

(10.17.6)

holds. Then
s_{k}\wedge\alpha
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact in the sense of currents. The induction hypothesis says that
s_{k}\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
and the second part of
Proposition

10.15
, implies the existence of some form

f\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet-1}C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})
such that

\mathcal{D}_{K,E}f=s_{k}\wedge\alpha.

(10.17.7)

This equality and (
10.17.6
) imply that
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(u_{k+1})+v_{k}\wedge\alpha-f
is
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-closed on
X
, hence
\mathcal{D}_{K,E}
-exact up to a smooth form, as it follows from Hodge decomposition of this current.
In particular, there exists a current
v_{k+1}\in\mathcal{D}_{\bullet,K}(X,E_{\bullet})
and a smooth form
f_{1}
such that

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(u_{k+1})+v_{k}\wedge\alpha-f_{1}=-\mathcal{D}_{K,E}v_{k+1}

and thus the equality

\mathcal{I}(u_{k+1})+\mathcal{D}_{K,E}v_{k+1}+v_{k}\wedge\alpha=f_{1}\in\oplus_{p+q=\bullet}C^{\infty}_{(0,q),K}(X,E_{p})

follows. Our claim is thus proved.
∎

In this setting, we propose the following problem.

Conjecture 10.18
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
u
is
D_{K}
-closed and
D^{c}_{K}
-exact.
Then
u
is
D_{K}D_{K}^{c}
-exact if and only if
\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{I}}u
is
D_{K}
-exact on
Y_{I}
for every index
I
, where
Y_{I}:=\cap_{i\in I}Y_{i}
and
\Delta_{1}=\sum Y_{i}
.

Notice that one direction is almost immediate: the form
u
is in particular
D_{K}
-exact, thus

u=D_{K}v

for some
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
. We write locally

v=\sum_{I}\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}}\wedge v_{I}
for some
v_{I}\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,L)
.
Then

u=\sum_{I}\varepsilon(I)\frac{dz_{I}}{z_{I}}\wedge D_{K}v_{I},

from which we infer that
\mathrm{Res}\,_{Y_{I}}u
is
D_{K}
-exact for any multi-index

I
.

We show next that we can take
k=\infty
, so to speak and end the proof of the main theorem.
First, Theorem
10.1
has the following consequence.

Corollary 10.19
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.1
, let
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
. If
-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u
is
D_{K}
-exact, then there exists a form
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\log(\Delta_{1}))
such that
-(\xi+\beta)\wedge u=D_{K}v
on
X\setminus D
and
(\xi+\beta)\wedge v
is
D_{K}
-exact.

A quick remark: given a form
u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
, we say that
u
is
D_{K}
-exact, if the equality
\displaystyle u=D_{K}v
holds on
X\setminus D
, where
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}\big(X,\log(\Delta_{1})\big)
.
The following statement is as consequence of the results in Section
8
.

Proposition 10.20
.

There exists a uniform constant
C
such that given any
D_{K}
-exact form

u\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L),

there exists
v\in C^{\infty}_{K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
such that the equality

u=D_{K}v

holds, and moreover
\|v\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|u\|_{H^{1}}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Proposition

8.8
, as we will see next. We select the solution
v

of the equation above such that
v\perp\mathrm{Ker}\,(D_{K})
. Then we have

D_{K}^{\star}u=\Delta_{K}v,

and Proposition
8.8
with
f:=v
and
g:=D_{K}^{\star}u
gives the result.
∎

By combining the two proposition above, we have the following statement.

Proposition 10.21
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.1
, let
f\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.
If
(\xi+\beta)\wedge f
is
D_{K}
-exact, then there exists a form
g\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
such that
the following hold.

•

D_{K}g=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge f
on
X\setminus D
.

•

(\xi+\beta)\wedge g
is
D_{K}
-exact.

•

\|g\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{H^{1}}
for some uniform constant
C
.

Here
C
depends on
X
and
(\xi+\beta)
, but is independent of
f
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the space of harmonic forms of total degree
k
: notice that it is finite dimensional.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}
be the space such that
(\xi+\beta)\wedge u
is
D_{K}
-exact for every
u\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
.
We have an orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
with respect to the
L^{2}
-norm.

Thanks to Corollary
10.19
, there exists
g_{1}
such that

D_{K}g_{1}=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge f

on
X\setminus D
and
(\xi+\beta)\wedge g_{1}
is
D_{K}
-exact. Now by Proposition
10.20
, we can find a
g_{2}
such that

D_{K}g_{2}=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge f

on
X\setminus D
and

\|g_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{H^{1}}.

(10.21.1)

Then
g_{1}-g_{2}
is
D_{K}
-closed and we have a decompoisiton

g_{1}=g_{2}+a_{1}+a_{2}+D_{K}u

where
a_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
a_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
u\in C^{\infty}_{k,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)
.
We claim that

g:=g_{2}+a_{2}

satisfies the three points in our statement.

By the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, the solution
g
satisfies the first two points. For the third point, we would like to
\|a_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|g_{2}\|_{H^{1}}
for some uniform constant
C
. It is a consequence of the following argument.

We define a linear morphism

\varphi:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathrm{Ker}\,C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

by taking
\varphi(u):=(\xi+\beta)\wedge u
for
u\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
\varphi
is injective. By the construction of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, we know that
Im(\varphi)\cap ImD_{K}=0
. Then we can equip the finite linear space
Im(\varphi)
with the following norm
\|\cdot\|_{2}
: for every
a\in Im(\varphi)
,
\|a\|_{2}
is defined to be the
L^{2}
-norm of the harmonic representation of
a
.
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is norm on
Im(\varphi)
since we have

Im(\varphi)\subset\mathrm{Ker}\,D_{K}\qquad\text{and }\qquad Im(\varphi)\cap ImD_{K}=0.

Note that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism between two finite dimensional spaces
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
Im(\varphi)
.
Then any two norms on these two spaces are equvalent. Therefore, there is a constant
C
such that for every
u\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we have

\|u\|_{H_{1}}\leqslant C\|\varphi(u)\|_{2}.

By applying it to
a_{2}
, we know that

\|a_{2}\|_{H_{1}}\leqslant C\|\varphi(a_{2})\|_{2}.

(10.21.2)

On the other hand, as
\varphi(a_{1})
is
D_{K}
-exact, we know that
\varphi(a_{2})-\varphi(g)
is
D_{K}
-exact. Then the harmonic reprensetaive of
\varphi(a_{2})
and
\varphi(g)
coincide. The
L^{2}
-norm of the harmonic representative of
\varphi(a_{2})
equals to
\|\varphi(a_{2})\|_{2}
by the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{2}
. Moreover, the
L^{2}
-norm of the harmonic representation of
\varphi(g)
is less than the
L^{2}
-norm of
\varphi(g)
. As these two harmonic representations coincides, we have thus

\|\varphi(a_{2})\|_{2}\leqslant\|(\xi+\beta)\wedge g_{2}\|_{L^{2}}

Together with (
10.21.2
), we have

\|a_{2}\|_{H_{1}}\leqslant C\|(\xi+\beta)\wedge g_{2}\|_{L_{2}}\leqslant C^{\prime}\|g_{2}\|_{H_{1}}.

Together with (
10.21.1
), we obtain

\|g\|_{H_{1}}=\|g_{2}+h_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{H^{1}}.

The proposition is proved.
∎

We infer the following consequence.

Theorem 10.22
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.1
, we can find a sequence

\{v_{k}\}_{k=0}^{+\infty}\subset C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

such that
v_{0}=u_{0}
and for every
k
, we have

D_{K}v_{k+1}=-(\xi+\beta)\wedge v_{k}\qquad\text{on }X\setminus D

and

\|v_{k+1}\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant C\|v_{k}\|_{H^{1}}

for some uniform constant
C
.

In particular,
\sum_{k\geqslant 0}t^{k}v_{k}
is convergent when
|t|\ll 1
.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Proposition
10.21
. We can construct
v_{k}
by using an induction on
k
and by taking
f=v_{k}
in Proposition
10.21
.
∎

11
Jumping loci: main result and its proof

The main goal of this section is to provide a new proof of
11.11
, based on the techniques developed in the preceding sections.
We note that
11.11
was previously established by Budur–Wang
[
BW15
,
BW20
]
using entirely different methods.

11.1
Some properties of De Rham moduli spaces and the Riemann-Hilbert map

In this subsection, we recall the definition of De Rham moduli spaces
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
and the Riemann-Hilbert map and
D=D_{1}+\cdots+D_{k}
.
Let
\mathrm{Res}\,
be the residue map of a logarithmic connection. Let
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}
be small loops around
D_{i}
. Let
{\rm Ev}:M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
be the evaluation of any representation given by
\varrho\mapsto(\varrho(\ell_{1}),\ldots,\varrho(\ell_{k}))
. For
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
, it can be defined explicitly as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm DR}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm DR}(X/D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm B}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{C}^{\star})^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm RH}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm RH}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm Res}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm Ev}

(11.0.1)

Here
M_{\rm DR}(X)
,
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
are complex Lie groups if
X
is a compact Kähler manifold. Note that
M_{\rm DR}(X)
and
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
are complex algebraic groups if
X
is projective. In any case,
M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
and
M_{\rm B}(X)
are complex algebraic tori. The Riemannian-Hilbert maps
{\rm RH}:M_{\rm DR}(X)\to M_{\rm B}(X)
and
{\rm RH}_{D}:M_{\rm DR}(X/D)\to M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
are defined by the monodromy representation, which are holomorphic maps. Let us denote by
M_{{\rm DR}}^{0}(X/D)
and
M_{\rm B}^{0}(X\setminus D)
the identity components of the corresponding complex Lie groups. We shall give an explicit description of
M^{0}_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.

First, we note the following fact.

Lemma 11.1
.

For any
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
, there exists some
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

c_{1}(L)=a_{1}c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+a_{k}c_{1}(D_{k}).

Conversely, if
c_{1}(L)=b_{1}c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+b_{k}c_{1}(D_{k})
for some
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
, then there exists a logarithmic connection
\nabla
of
L
such that
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.

Proof.

If
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
, then the curvature current of the connection
\nabla+\bar{\partial}_{L}
is
\frac{i}{2\pi}[\bar{\partial}_{L},\nabla]=-{\rm Res}(\nabla,D_{i})[D_{i}]
. Therefore, we have

c_{1}(L)=-{\rm Res}(\nabla,D_{i})[D_{i}].

Note that the
c_{1}(L)
and
c_{1}(D_{i})
are
\mathbb{Q}
-points in
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. The above equality implies that
c_{1}(L)\in\mathbb{Q}c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+\mathbb{Q}c_{1}(D_{k})
.

Conversely, if
c_{1}(L)=b_{1}c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+b_{k}c_{1}(D_{k})
for some
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
, then we have

c_{1}(L)=\mathrm{Re}\,(b_{1})c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+\mathrm{Re}\,(b_{k})c_{1}(D_{k})

as
c_{1}(L)\in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb{R})
.
This can be sen as a family of rational equations, which has a solution over
\mathbb{R}
. Then it has a solution over
\mathbb{Q}
, so we can write

c_{1}(L)=a_{1}c_{1}(D_{1})+\cdots+a_{k}c_{1}(D_{k})

for some rational numbers
a_{i}
.
Moreover,
there exists a singular hermitian metric
h
for
L
such that the curvature current

i\Theta_{h}(L)=\mathrm{Re}\,(b_{1})[D_{1}]+\cdots+\mathrm{Re}\,(b_{k})[D_{k}].

In particular,
i\Theta_{h}(L)=0
on
X\setminus D
. Then
(L,D_{h}^{\prime})\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.
The lemma is proved.
∎

Let
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
and let
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
close to
(L,\nabla)
. Let
\bar{\partial}
be the complex structure of
L
. Since
c_{1}(L)=c_{1}(L^{\prime})
on
X
, there exists a
\xi\in H^{0,1}(X)
such that
\bar{\partial}+\xi
defines the complex structure of
L^{\prime}
. We can let
\xi
be a harmonic form. Then we have
d\xi=0
. Now
\nabla^{\prime}=\nabla+\beta
where
\beta
is a
(1,0)
-form with log pole along
D
.
Moreover, the flat and holomorphic conditions imply that

(\nabla+\beta)^{2}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad[\nabla+\beta,\bar{\partial}+\xi]=0\text{ on }X_{0}.

(11.1.1)

The first condition means that
\partial\beta=0
on
X_{0}
. For the second condition, since
\partial\xi=0
, we have
\bar{\partial}\beta=0
. In particular, we we know that
\beta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))
.
To conclude, we have

Proposition 11.2
.

Fix a point
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
and let
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
close to
(L,\nabla)
. Then

(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\simeq(L,\bar{\partial}+\xi,\nabla+\beta)

for some
\xi\in H^{0,1}(X)
smooth form with
d\xi=0
and
\beta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
and let
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
close to
(L,\nabla)
. Let
h_{L}
be a harmonic metric on
L
in Proposition
6.12
. Then there exists a unique harmonic metric (up to a constant)
h_{L^{\prime}}
on
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
such that

i\Theta_{h_{L}}(L)=i\Theta_{h_{L^{\prime}}}(L^{\prime})\qquad\text{ on }X.

(11.3.1)

Morevoer, if
(L,\nabla,h_{L})
satisfies Proposition
6.12
, then
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime},h_{L^{\prime}})
satisfes the same properties in Proposition
6.12
. In particular, the following two complexes

\nabla^{\prime}:\cdots\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L^{\prime}\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{X}(\log D)\otimes L^{\prime}\to\cdots

(11.3.2)

D_{K,L^{\prime}}:\cdots\to C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L^{\prime})\to C^{\infty}_{\bullet+1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L^{\prime})\to\cdots

are quasi-isomorphic.

Proof.

Since
h_{L}
is harmonic, we have
i\Theta_{h_{L}}(L)=\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
on
X
. Now as
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
is in the same component of
(L,\nabla)
, we have
c_{1}(L^{\prime})=\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]
. Then there exists a unique harmonic (after a constant)
h_{L^{\prime}}
on
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
such that

i\Theta_{h_{L^{\prime}}}(L^{\prime})=\sum a_{i}[D_{i}]=i\Theta_{h_{L}}(L).

The first part is proved.

For the second part, thanks to (
11.3.1
), we know that
\nu(h_{L^{\prime}})=\nu(h_{L})
for every component of
D
. Then the third point of Proposition
6.12
is satisified for
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
. Set
\theta^{\prime}:=\nabla^{\prime}-D^{\prime}_{h_{L^{\prime}}}
. By the first point of Proposition
6.12
, we know that
Res\theta^{\prime}=0
on
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}
. As
\nabla^{\prime}
is close to
\nabla
and
\nu(h_{L^{\prime}})=\nu(h_{L})
, then
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta^{\prime}
is close to
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta
. Note that
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta\neq 0
on
\Delta_{2}
. Then
\mathrm{Res}\,\theta\neq 0
on
\Delta_{2}
when
\nabla^{\prime}
is close to
\nabla
. The second point of Proposition
6.12
is thus verified for
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
.
∎

The objects in
M^{0}_{\rm DR}(X/D)
correspond to a pair
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
with
c_{1}(L)=0
. By Deligne’s mixed Hodge theory, we have an isomorphism

H^{1}(X\setminus D,\mathbb{C})\simeq H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X).

For any
\xi\in H^{1}(X\setminus D,\mathbb{C})
, let
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)
be the corresponding element of
\xi
. This gives rise to a pair
(L,\nabla)
as follows. The line bundle
L
is defined by
\bar{\partial}+\xi_{2}
. Namely, locally there exists a function
f
such that
\xi_{2}=\bar{\partial}f
. Then a local holomorphic section of
L
is given by
e^{-f}
as

(\bar{\partial}+\xi_{2})(e^{-f})=0.

Then
\nabla:=\partial+\xi_{1}
is a holomorphic connection for
L
. Indeed, one has

(\partial+\xi_{1})(e^{-f})=(-\partial f+\xi_{1})e^{-f},

and by
\bar{\partial}(-\partial f+\xi_{1})=0
, it follows that
(-\partial f+\xi_{1})e^{-f}
is a local holomorphic section of
\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\otimes L
.

Note that
(L,\nabla)
is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{O}_{X},d)
if there is a nowhere vanishing smooth function
f\in C^{0}(X)
that is holomorphic with respect to
L
and flat with respect to
\nabla
(i.e.
f
is gauge transformation). Therefore, for any local smooth function
e
, one has

de=f^{-1}\circ(d+(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}))\circ(f\cdot e)=de+f^{-1}df+(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}).

This implies that
\xi_{1}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
(i.e. it does not have log poles along
D
), and
(d+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})(f)=0
. If we impose the condition that
f\left(x_{0}\right)=1
, the solution is necessarily given by the integral

f=\exp\left(-\int_{x_{0}}^{x}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\right)

This is well-defined on
X
if and only if all periods of
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}
belong to
2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)\simeq\frac{H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)}{\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)\mid(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\mbox{ has periods in }2\pi i\mathbb{Z}\}}.

(11.3.3)

Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\in\pi_{1}(X_{0},x_{0})
such that they generate a basis for
H_{1}(X_{0},\mathbb{Z})/{\rm torsion}
. Then
M^{0}_{\rm B}(X_{0})\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{\star})^{m}
with the isomorphism being
\varrho\mapsto(\varrho(\gamma_{1}),\ldots,\varrho(\gamma_{m}))
. Then the Riemann-Hilbert map is given by the holonomy representation. It can be made explicitly as follows. Take
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)
. We denote by
[(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})]
its image in
M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)
. Then we have

\displaystyle M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)

\displaystyle\to M_{\rm B}^{0}(X_{0})

\displaystyle{\rm RH}_{D}:[(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})]

\displaystyle\mapsto(\exp(-\int_{\gamma_{1}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})),\ldots,\exp(-\int_{\gamma_{m}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}))).

(11.3.4)

Lemma 11.4
.

{\rm RH}_{D}|_{M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)}
is a surjective group morphism between complex Lie groups, that is a covering map.

Proof.

It is easy to see that it is a group morphism of complex Lie groups. Since their Lie algebras are isomorphic, it is an covering map. Choose any
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{m}
. Since we have the isomorphisms

(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{m}\simeq{\rm Hom}(H_{1}(X_{0},\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{C})\simeq H^{1}(X_{0},\mathbb{C})\simeq H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X),

there exists
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)
such that

(\exp(-\int_{\gamma_{1}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})),\ldots,\exp(-\int_{\gamma_{m}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})))=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}).

Therefore,
{\rm RH}_{D}|_{M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)}
is surjective.
∎

Remark 11.5
.

Note that in general,
{\rm RH}_{D}|_{M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)}
is not injective and it can be an infinite covering. For example, let
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
D=\{0,\infty\}
. Then
\pi_{1}(X_{0})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Consider the pair
(\mathcal{O}_{X},d+\log z)
. Then
{\rm RH}_{D}([(\mathcal{O}_{X},d+\log z)])=1
, that is the trivial representation. However,
(\mathcal{O}_{X},d+\log z)
is not isomorphic to
(\mathcal{O}_{X},d)
. Note that
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)=H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\simeq\mathbb{C}
, and
M_{\rm B}(X_{0})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{*}
, with
{\rm RH}_{D}
being the exponential map. Hence
{\rm RH}_{D}|_{M_{\rm DR}^{0}(X/D)}
is an infinite covering map.

Let us state the following criterion for the translate of subtori.

Lemma 11.6
.

Let
X\subset(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{m}=:T
be an irreducible Zariski closed subset of the complex algebraic torus
T
. Assume that there exists a Zariski open subset
X^{\circ}\subset X
satisfying the following properties

•

X^{\circ}
is smooth,

•

For any
p\in X^{\circ}
, and any
\xi\in T_{p}X
, there exists some
\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\mid\exp(z\xi_{0})p\}\subset X.

(11.6.1)

Here
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
is uniquely defined so that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}|_{z=0}\exp(z\xi_{0})p=\xi

(11.6.2)

Then
X
is a translate of a subtorus in
T
.

Proof.

By (
11.6.1
), there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\mid\exp(z\xi_{0})p\}\subset X

(11.6.3)

Since
X
is Zariski closed, it follows that

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\exp(z\xi_{0})p\}\subset X

(11.6.4)

for any
\xi\in T_{p}X
with
\xi_{0}
defined by (
11.6.2
).

Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in T_{p}X
be a basis for
T_{p}X
. Since
p
is a smooth point of
X
, then
\dim X=k
.
\xi_{i,0}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
is uniquely defined so that
\frac{d}{dz}|_{z=0}\exp(z\xi_{i,0})p=\xi_{i}
. Then by (
11.6.4
), we have

\displaystyle\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{k}\mid\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{k}z\xi_{i})p\}\subset X.

(11.6.5)

Consider a group morphism
f
defined by

\displaystyle f:(\mathbb{C}^{k},+)

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{m}

(11.6.6)

\displaystyle(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{k}z\xi_{i})p

By construction,
f
is a
local
immersion at the origin. By (
11.6.5
), the image
f(\mathbb{C}^{k})
is contained in an irreducible Zariski closed subset
X\cdot p^{-1}
of dimension
k
. Therefore,
f(\mathbb{C}^{k})
is Zariski closed, and a subtorus of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{m}
. and thus
X=f(\mathbb{C}^{k})\cdot p
is a translate of subtori.
∎

11.2
A fine resolution for relative log De Rham complex

Fix a
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
. Let
(\xi,\omega):\mathbb{D}\to\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)\times H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))
be a holomorphic map such that
(\xi(0),\omega(0))=(0,0)
. Let
p_{1}:X\times\mathbb{D}\to X
be the projection map.
We consider the local universal holomorphic line bundle

\mathcal{L}\to X\times\mathbb{D}
defined by

\mathcal{L}:=(p_{1}^{*}L,p_{1}^{*}\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z)).

Namely, the complex structure on
p_{1}^{*}L
is given by
p_{1}^{*}\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z)
. The aim of this subsection is to generalise the constructions in Section
9
to
\mathcal{L}
.

To begin with, we first construct a metric on
\mathcal{L}
.
Let us equip
L
with a harmonic metric
h
defined in
Proposition

6.12
. We equip
\mathcal{L}
the pullback metric
p_{1}^{*}h
. Then

Lemma 11.7
.

There exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
,
p_{1}^{*}h|_{X\times\{z\}}
is a harmonic metric for
(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z),\nabla+\omega(z))\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
satisfying the properties in
Proposition

6.12
.

Proof.

Write
L_{z}
for the holomorphic line bundle
(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z))
. Let
\theta_{z}
be the corresponding logarithmic Higgs field of
(L_{z},\nabla+\omega(z))
with respect to the metric
h
, and let
D_{z}^{\prime}
be the
(1,0)
-part of the Chern connection of
(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z),h)
. By the construction in
Section

6.1
, we have

D_{z}^{\prime}=D_{0}^{\prime}-\overline{\xi(z)},

and

-2\theta_{z}=D_{0}^{\prime}-\overline{\xi(z)}-\nabla-\omega(z)=-2\theta_{0}-\overline{\xi(z)}-\omega(z).

Then

i\Theta_{h}(L_{z})=i[\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z),D_{0}^{\prime}-\overline{\xi(z)}]=i\Theta_{h}(L),

and

{\rm Res}_{D_{i}}\theta_{z}={\rm Res}_{D_{i}}\theta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}{\rm Res}_{D_{i}}\omega(z)

Hence there are some
\varepsilon>0
such that the three conditions in
Proposition

6.12
are fulfilled for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Now we would like to construct a relative logarithmic De-Rham complex on
\mathcal{L}
.
We shall abusively write
\omega(z)
and
\xi(z)
for
p_{1}^{*}\omega(z)
and
p_{1}^{*}\xi(z)
. Set
\mathcal{X}:=X\times\mathbb{D}
. Note that
p_{1}^{*}(\nabla+\bar{\partial}_{L})+\omega(z)+\xi(z)
is a connection for the holomorphic line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. Note that this connection is not flat, since its curvature is
dz\wedge(\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial z}+\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial z})
. However, if we consider the relative logarithmic complex, it will be flat.

Set
\mathcal{D}:=D\times\mathbb{D}
. We define
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D})
to be the quotient

p_{2}^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{D}}^{1}\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}(\log\mathcal{D})\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D})

where
p_{2}:X\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
is the projection on the second factor. Since
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}(\log\mathcal{D})=p_{1}^{*}\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\oplus p_{2}^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{D}}^{1}
, it follows that

\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D})=p_{1}^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D).

Then

\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D}):=\Lambda^{i}\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D})=p_{1}^{*}\Omega^{i}_{X}(\log D).

Note that

\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}}(\log\mathcal{D})=\oplus_{k+\ell=i}p_{1}^{*}\Omega_{X}^{k}(\log D)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}p_{2}^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{D}}^{\ell}.

Hence
\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D})
is a direct factor of
\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}}(\log\mathcal{D})
, and there is thus a natural quotient

q:\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}}(\log\mathcal{D})\to\Omega^{i}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}(\log\mathcal{D}).

For any open subset
U
of
\mathcal{X}
, and any holomorphic section

e\in\mathcal{L}\otimes\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}^{i}(U)=\mathcal{L}\otimes\Omega_{X}^{i}(U),

we define

\tilde{\nabla}(e):=q((p_{1}^{*}\nabla+\omega(z))(u)).

Then we have
\tilde{\nabla}^{2}=0
. Let
\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
be the holomorphic map defined by
\gamma(z):=(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+\xi(z),\omega(z))
.
We can thus define a
relative logarithmic De-Rham complex
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma}:=\mathcal{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\nabla}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\otimes\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}^{1}(\log D)\to\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\nabla}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\otimes\Omega_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}}^{n}(\log D)

(11.7.1)

As in Section
9
, we would like construct a fine resolution of the complex (
11.7.1
) . To begin with, we have the following easy lemma.

Lemma 11.8
.

Set
L_{z}:=\mathcal{L}|_{X\times\{z\}}
. Consider the complex of sheaves

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L):\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D_{K}}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D_{K}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{2n,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

(11.8.1)

defined in
8.9
. For any
z\in\mathbb{D}
, we have the identification

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L_{z})

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
8.9
.
∎

We now replace
\mathcal{X}
by
X\times\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
. Write
\tilde{\Delta}_{1}:=\Delta_{1}\times\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
. Consider the sheaf
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
defined (
8.9
). By
11.8
, we have
a splitting

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})=p_{2}^{*}dz\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})\oplus p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\oplus p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

(11.8.2)

where

p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)=p_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\otimes_{p^{-1}C^{\infty}_{X}}C^{\infty}_{\mathcal{X}}

Let

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
be the quotient of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
by
p_{2}^{*}dz\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
. Then it is isomorphic to

\displaystyle p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\oplus p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L).

(11.8.3)

For the connection
\tilde{D}_{K}:=p_{1}^{*}(\nabla+\bar{\partial}_{L})+\omega(z)+\xi(z)
, we note that

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{K}:p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L),

(11.8.4)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}}:p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-2,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\oplus p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\to

(11.8.5)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{*}d\bar{z}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet-1,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)\oplus p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,\Delta_{1},L)

and

\tilde{D}_{K}^{2}=dz\wedge(\frac{\partial\omega}{\partial z}+\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial z}).

It thus induces an integrable connection on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
, denoted abusively by
\tilde{D}_{K}
. Hence we have a complex

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{K,\gamma}^{\bullet}:=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{D}_{K}}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{D}_{K}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})

(11.8.6)

As an analogue of Theorem
9.1
, we have the following relative version.

Proposition 11.9
.

The two complexes (
11.7.1
) and (
11.8.6
) are quasi-isomorphic via the natural morphism.

Proof.

Fix any
x\in X
, we take an open neighborhood
U
of
x
, that is biholomorphic to some polydisk. Pick any
e\in\mathcal{C}_{K,\gamma}^{i}(U)
such that
\tilde{D}_{K}(e)=0
. By (
11.8.2
), we can decompose

e=\sum_{k=0,\ldots,\ell}e^{i-k,k}\quad{\rm modulo}\quad p_{2}^{*}dz\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{i-1,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})

such that each
e^{k,i-k}
are in (
11.8.3
). Then
\tilde{D}_{K}(e)=0
together with (
11.8.5
) and (
11.8.4
) implies that

\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}}(e^{i-\ell,\ell})=0.

By
Proposition

9.7
, there exists
f
such that
\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}}f=-e^{i-\ell,\ell}
. So we have

e+\tilde{D}_{K}(f)=\sum_{k=0,\ldots,\ell-1}e_{1}^{i-k,k}\quad{\rm modulo}\quad p_{2}^{*}dz\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{i-1,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L}),

with each
e_{1}^{i-k,k}
lying in (
11.8.3
).
Keep this algorithm in this manner, we finally find some smooth
f
such that

e+\tilde{D}_{K}(f)=e_{0}^{i,0}\quad{\rm modulo}\quad p_{2}^{*}dz\wedge\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{i-1,K}(\mathcal{X},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L}),

such that
\tilde{D}_{K}(e_{0}^{i,0})=0
, and
e_{0}^{i,0}
is a smooth
(i,0)
-form lying in (
11.8.3
). This implies that
\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{L}}(e_{0}^{i,0})=0
, and
\tilde{\nabla}(e_{0}^{i,0})=0
. Hence we prove the quasi-isomorphism between the two complexes.
∎

11.3
Cohomology jumping loci property

Consider the subset

\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}:=\left\{(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)\;\middle|\;\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}\!\left(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L\right)\geqslant k\right\}.

It is an analytic subset of
M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.
Note that we have the descending chain

\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}\supset\Sigma_{k+1}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}\supset\cdots.

Therefore, for a
generic
point
(L,\nabla)\in\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
, there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
such that

\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}\!\left(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L^{\prime}\right)

remains constant for all
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in U
.

Let
\Delta_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}
and we consider any
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
. We shall adopt the notations in
Section

10.1
. Then for a
generic
point
(L,\nabla)\in\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
, there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
such that

\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}\!\left(Y_{I},\Omega_{Y_{I}}^{\bullet}(\log(D_{I}\setminus\Delta_{1}))\otimes L^{\prime}\right)

remains constant for all
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in U
.

Theorem 11.10
.

Let
(L,\nabla)\in\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
be a generic point and we suppose that

\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,_{D_{k}}(L,\nabla))\in]-1,0]

for every component
D_{k}
of
D
.
Let
\mathbb{D}
be the unit disc in
\mathbb{C}
and we consider a holomorphic map

\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}

such that
\gamma(0)=(L,\nabla)
.
We denote by
\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)\times H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))
the first order deformation of
\gamma
. Then

\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L_{\gamma(0)+z\gamma^{\prime}(0)})\geqslant k

(11.10.1)

for every
|z|\ll 1
. Here
\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L_{\gamma(0)+z\gamma^{\prime}(0)}
is the logarithmic De Rham complex induced by
\gamma(0)+z\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
.

Proof.

By the above arguments, for a generic point
(L,\nabla)\in\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
, there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
of
(L,\nabla)
such that

\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}\!\left(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L^{\prime}\right)

and

\displaystyle\dim\mathbb{H}^{i-|I|}\!\left(Y_{I},\Omega_{Y_{I}}^{\bullet}(\log(D_{I}\setminus\Delta_{1}))\otimes L^{\prime}\right)

(11.10.2)

all remain constant for all
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})\in U
and any non-empty
I\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

As
\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
is a holomorphic curve with
\gamma(0)=(L,\nabla)
, there exists some
\varepsilon>0
and holomorphic maps
(\alpha,\beta):\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)\times H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))
such that
(\alpha(0),\beta(0))=(0,0)
,
\gamma(z)=(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+\alpha(z),\nabla+\beta(z))
, and
\gamma(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})\subset U
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}^{\bullet}
and
C^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma}
be the two complexes defined in
Equation

11.7.1
and (
11.8.6
). By applying
Proposition

11.9
, after shrinking
\varepsilon
, we can assume that
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

As
(L,\nabla)
is a generic point in
\Sigma
, we know that the restiction

\Gamma(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{i}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma}))\to\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma(0)})

is surjective by the cohomology and base change theorem. Note that we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{R}^{i}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{i}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma(0)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(\mathcal{X},C^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(X,{\Delta}_{1},L))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

where the first vertical isomorphism comes from the definition.
Note that the relative complexes
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}^{\bullet}
and
C^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma}
are quasi-isomorphic. Hence the vertical arrows in the above diagram are all isomorphisms. It follows that the bottom arrow is also surjective. Therefore, for an element
e\in\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{K,\gamma(0)})
, we can find a section
u\in C^{\infty}_{\bullet,K}(\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\Delta}_{1},\mathcal{L})
such that
\psi(u)=k(e)
.

Now we denote
u(z):=u|_{X\times\{z\}}
. Then
u(0)
represents
e
and
D_{K}u=0
in the sense of the relative complexe. In particular, we have

D_{K,z}u(z)=0
for each
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
. Here
D_{k,z}:=\nabla+\bar{\partial}_{L}+\alpha(z)+\omega(z)
. Set
\xi:=\frac{\partial\alpha}{\partial z}(0)
and
\omega:=\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial z}(0)
. If we take the first order expansion for
D_{K,z}u(z)=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle D_{K}u(0)=0,\quad D_{K}\frac{\partial u}{\partial z}(0)=-(\xi+\omega)\wedge u(0).

(11.10.3)

Moreover, by (
11.10.2
) and the same arguments as above, we can show that, for any non-empty
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
, (
11.10.3
) holds for any
Y_{I}
. In other words, every
D_{K}
-closed form on
C_{i-|I|,K}^{\infty}(Y_{I},L)
can be extended to order one. Then the second condition in
10.1
is verified. Hence we can apply
10.1
.

Let us consider another holomorphic map
\gamma_{2}:\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}\to M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
defined by
\gamma_{2}(z)=(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+z\xi,\nabla+z\omega)
. We can shrink
\varepsilon
such that for the two complexes
\mathcal{L}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}
,
C_{K,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}
, the inclusion
C_{K,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}\to\mathcal{L}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}
is also a quasi-isomorphism by
Proposition

11.9
.

By
10.1
, there exists
u_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{i,K}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}(\log\Delta_{1})\otimes L)
such that they satisfy (
10.1.1
). Moreover, after possibly shrinking
\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
,
for

\sigma:=u(0)+zu_{1}+\cdots+\cdots+z^{j}u_{j}+\cdots,

we have
\sigma\in\mathcal{C}_{K,\gamma_{2}}^{i}(\mathcal{X})
by
10.22
.
Let
\tilde{D}_{K}:C_{K,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}\to C_{K,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet+1}
be the relative differential defined in
Section

11.2
. By (
10.1.1
), we have

\tilde{D}_{K}(\sigma)=0.

By
Proposition

11.9
, this implies that

\mathbb{R}^{i}(p_{1})_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2}})(\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon})\to\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}|_{X\times\{0\}})=\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,L^{\bullet})

is surjective. Set
L_{z}:=(L,\bar{\partial}_{L}+z\xi)
and
\nabla_{z}:=\nabla+z\omega
. Then
(L_{z},\nabla_{z})=\gamma_{2}(z)=\gamma(0)+z\gamma^{\prime}(0)
. Let
L_{z}^{\bullet}
be the logarithmic De Rham complex

L_{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{z}}}{{\to}}L_{z}\otimes\Omega_{X}(\log D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{z}}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{z}}}{{\to}}L_{z}\otimes\Omega^{\dim X}_{X}(\log D).

Hence, by the
cohomology and base change theorem
, after possibly shrinking
\varepsilon
, we have

\mathbb{R}^{i}(p_{1})_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{\gamma})
is locally free over
\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,L_{z}^{\bullet})
is constant for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
.
The theorem is thus proved.
∎

Now we would like to prove the main theorem in this section.

Theorem 11.11
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor. Then the jumping locus
\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X\setminus D)_{\rm B}
is a finite union of translate of subtori.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma
be an irreducible component of
\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X\setminus D)_{\rm B}
. We pick a general representation
\varrho\in\Sigma
, that is contained in the smooth locus of
\Sigma
. Let
(L,\nabla)\in M_{\rm DR}(X/D)
such that
\varrho={\rm RH}_{D}((L,\nabla))
and
\mathrm{Re}\,(\mathrm{Res}\,(L,\nabla))\in]-1,0]
for every component of
D
.

Pick any
\xi\in T_{\varrho}\Sigma
. By
11.4
, there exists
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X)
such that, for the family of representations
\varrho_{t}:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
defined by

\varrho_{t}(\gamma)=\varrho(\gamma)\cdot\exp(-z\int_{\gamma}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})),\quad\ \forall\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X_{0}),

we have

\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\varrho_{t}=\xi\in T_{\varrho}\Sigma.

We also consider a family of log flat connections parametrized by
z\in\mathbb{C}
defined by

(E_{z},\nabla^{\prime}_{z}):=(\bar{\partial}+z\xi_{2},\partial+z\xi_{1}).

Then

{\rm RH}_{D}(E_{z},\nabla^{\prime}_{z})(\gamma_{i})=\exp(-z\int_{\gamma_{i}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}))

by (
11.3.4
). We define a family of pairs
(L_{z},\nabla_{z}):=(L,\nabla)\otimes(E_{z},\nabla_{z}^{\prime})
parametrized by
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Then we have

{\rm RH}_{D}(L_{z},\nabla_{z})(\gamma)=\varrho(\gamma)\cdot\exp(-z\int_{\gamma}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}))=\varrho_{t}(\gamma)\quad\forall\ \gamma\in\pi_{1}(X_{0})

with
t=\exp z
. This implies that

{\rm RH}_{D}(L_{z},\nabla_{z})=\varrho_{t}.

Note that the residue map
M_{\rm DR}(X)\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
is a holomorphic map. Then for any representation
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
in a small neighborhood of
(L,\nabla)
in
\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}
, its residues are not positive integers along every component of
D
.
Therefore, by Theorem
6.1
, we have

\dim\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}(\log D)\otimes L^{\prime})=\dim H^{i}(X_{0},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is the local system associated with
(L^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
. It follows from
11.4
that

(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in T_{\varrho}\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X/D)_{\rm DR}.

Therefore, we conclude from Theorem
11.10
that, there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varrho_{t}\in\Sigma
for any
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
. By
11.6
, we conclude that
\Sigma_{k}^{i}(X\setminus D)_{\rm B}
is a translate of subtori of
M_{\rm B}(X\setminus D)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Let
W
be an irreducible component of
\Sigma^{p}_{k}(X\setminus D)_{\rm B}
containing
\gamma(\mathbb{C})
. By Theorem
11.11

W
is a translate of a sub-torus of
M_{\rm B}(X\setminus B)
whose tangent space contains
\dot{\alpha}=d\gamma(0)
. In particular
\tau+z\dot{\alpha}\subset W
and the result follows.

∎

12
Generic vanishing Theorem

Based on the above ideas, in this section, we can give straightforward proofs for two generic vanishing theorems by Green-Lazarsfeld
[
GL87
]
.

The first one is the Kodaira type vanishing theorem.

Theorem 12.1

(Green-Lazarsfeld)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-fold with maximal Albanese dimension. Let

V^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\{L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\ s.t.\ h^{i}(X,L)\neq 0\},

then
{\rm codim}_{{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)}\mathcal{W}\geqslant n-i
for any irreducible component of
V^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
In particular, for a general
L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
, we have
H^{j}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}+L))=0
for any
j>0
.

Proof.

Fix any
i>0
then
V^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
is an analytic subset of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
. Let
L\in\mathcal{W}\subset V^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
be a general point of the component
\mathcal{W}
, then for any
[\xi]\in T_{L}\mathcal{W}\subset T_{L}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
we pick a representative
\xi\in A^{0,1}(X)
such that
\bar{\partial}\xi=0
.
For any
t\in\mathbb{C}
, consider the holomorphic line bundles
L_{t\xi}
with complex structure defined by
\bar{\partial}_{t}:=\bar{\partial}_{L}+t\xi
. Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that if
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
,
\dim H^{i}(X,L_{t\xi})
is constant.

We choose any
u\in A^{0,i}(X,L)
such that
\bar{\partial}u=0
. By
[
Kod86
, Theorem 7.4]
, there exists
u(t)\in A^{0,i}(X,L_{t\xi})
for any
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
, such that
u(0)=u
,
\bar{\partial}_{t}u(t)=0
, that deforms smoothly. Therefore, one can write

u(t)=u_{0}+tu_{1}+t^{2}u_{2}+\cdots,

and we have

\bar{\partial}u_{1}+\xi\wedge u_{0}=0.

Equivalently, the first order deformation of
u_{0}
is always unobstructed along any direction
\xi
in
W:=T_{L}\mathcal{W}\subset H^{0,1}(X)
. Therefore, the natural map

H^{0,i}(X,L)\times W\to H^{0,i+1}(X,L)

defined by the cup product is always zero.
Since
L
has a hermitian flat metric, if we take the conjugate
\bar{W}\subset H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})
, the map

H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{i}\otimes L^{\star})\times\bar{W}\to H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{i+1}\otimes L^{\star})

is trivial. Since
X
has maximal Albanese dimension, there exists
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
such that
\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{n}\neq 0
. If
w=\dim W
, then we may assume that
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{w}
is a basis of
\bar{W}
. If
v\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{i}\otimes L^{\star})
is a non-zero section, it follows that, at a general point
x\in X
, that
v\wedge\omega_{i}(x)=0
for
i=1,\ldots,w
and so
i\geqslant w
, i.e.

{\rm codim}_{{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)}\mathcal{W}=n-w\geqslant n-i.

The first assertion is thus proved. The second assertion follows from hard Serre duality. The theorem is thus proved.
∎

In
[
GL87
]
, they also proved a Nakano type generic vanishing theorem as follows.

Theorem 12.2

(Green-Lazarsfeld)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-fold. Define

w(X):={\rm max}\{{\rm codim}_{X}{Z(w)}\mid 0\neq\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\},

where
Z(\omega)
is the zero locus of
\omega
. Then for a general
L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
, we have

H^{i}(X,\Omega_{X}^{j}\otimes L)

for
i+j<w(X)
. If there is a nowhere vanishing one-form on
X
, then the Theorem holds with
w(X)=\infty
.

We will prove
12.2
in the rest of the section. We first prove the fllowing lemma.

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
be a holomorphic form such that
{\rm codim}_{X}{Z(w)}=m
, and let
L\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
. Then the hypercohomology
\mathbb{H}^{i}(X,L^{\bullet}_{\omega})
of the Dolbeault complex

\displaystyle L^{\bullet}_{\omega}:=L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge\omega}}{{\to}}L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\cdots\to\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge\omega}}{{\to}}L\otimes\Omega_{X}^{n},

(12.3.1)

vanishes for
i<m
.

Proof.

By
[
GL87
, Proposition 3.4]
, we know that
L^{\bullet}_{\omega}
is exact for
i<m
. By
[
EZT14
, Section 2.4.1]
, there exists a natural spectral sequence for
L^{\bullet}_{\omega}
such that
E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(X,\mathcal{H}^{q})
